**2875.** Pada is words that express [meaning]; of this there are many kinds.
Words such as "tena," "tena" are for the sake of auspiciousness.

**2876.** Patha is the syllables born of instruments: "dha," "dha," "dhilanga" and the like;
tala is chachchatputa, yati and the rest, according to the rules.[^706]

**2877.** These six angas the ancient acharyas define;
some name four: words, svara, tala and tena.

**2878.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*The six angas of a prabandha are svara, biruda, pada,*
*tenaka, patha and tala. The svaras are sa, ri, ga, ma and the rest.*

**2879.** *That which [serves] to mention qualities is declared to be biruda;*
*and that which expresses something else is called pada.*

**2880.** *The word "tena" is tena; it is determined as having the sense of auspiciousness.*
*"Dham dham," "dhuga-dhuga" and the like are the pathas, the multitudes of instrumental syllables.*

**2881.** *Adi, yati and the rest are the talas; and tala will be described [later].*

**2882.** *In the Sangita-parijata:*
*Pada, tala, svara, patha, tena and [what] is called biruda —*
*these are the six angas of song, named by the wise.*

**2883.** *Padas are words that express [meaning]; the talas are chachchatputa and the rest;*
*the svaras are shadja and the rest; patha is a syllable born of an instrument;*

**2884.** *tena is an auspicious word; biruda is [a word] joined with the names of qualities.*

**2885.** The jatis of prabandha are five: medini, nandini,
dipani, pavani, taravali — so says the muni.[^707]

**2886.** [A prabandha] with six angas is named medini, with five angas nandini,
with four angas dipani, and this one, with three angas, pavani.

**2887.** With two angas it is taravali, say the knowers of song.
Hence know: a prabandha with one anga is not accepted.

**2888.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*The jatis of prabandhas are five, approved by the sages:*
*medini, nandini, dipani, and also pavani*

**2889.** *and taravali. Now their characteristics are set forth:*
*medini is said to have six angas, nandini five,*

**2890.** *dipani has four angas, pavani is held to have three,*
*taravali, as the ancient knowers of song have said, has two.*
*(By this it is established that there is no prabandha with one anga.)*

**2891.** *In the Sangita-parijata:*
*The jatis of prabandhas are five, and they [follow] in order:*
*with six angas there is medini, with five nandini;*

**2892.** *with four it is called dipani, with three angas pavani;*
*with two angas the jati taravali arises.*

**2893.** The varieties of shuddha prabandha have no end;
the knowers of sangita define [them] in various ways.

**2894.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*Because of the endlessness of shuddha prabandhas, their variety is indeed only one.*

**2895.** *Even so:*
*with one tala, with two instruments, with three or with many,*
*a good poet may certainly compose prabandhas as he wishes.*[^708]

**2896.** *Moreover:*
*prabandhas with many talas are to be composed with many ragas*
*or with one raga — according to the rules for patha and the rest.*

**2897.** *Their varieties are very many; who will tell of them completely?*

**2898.** *Therefore it is said:*
*Neither to ragas, nor to talas, nor, especially, to instruments,*
*nor even to prabandha songs is there an end in the world.*

**2899.** O Srinivasa! Krishna with His beloveds in the rasa dance
reveals shuddha prabandha, unattainable for Brahma and the others.

**2900.** Seeing the beauty of Rai and Kanu, absorbed in song,
Vrinda-devi in her bliss cannot restrain her heart.

**2901.** [She] bids the shukas, sharis and cuckoos
to describe the glory of the qualities of Sri Krishna and Radhika.[^709]

**2902.** At Vrinda's bidding the delighted shukas, sharis and cuckoos
describe the qualities of Sri Krishna and Radhika.

**2903.** *The shuka says — an example of a medini song with six angas:*
Glory, glory! Delighter of the people, lotus-eyed,
the young nagara like dense collyrium, e e!
Taker of family fidelity from the noble [wives] of Gokula,
moon-faced, ocean of virtues, e e!

**2904.** Son of Nanda, ornament of Vraja, full of rasa,
with charming arms, increaser of joy, e e!
Treasure of the heart of the daughter of Sri Vrishabhanu,
crusher of the pride of myriads of Madanas, e e!

**2905.** Skilled in song, delighting in the ways of Nidhuvana,
incomparable master of the tandava dance, e e!
In the expanse of the courtyard — the bank of the Daughter of the Sun —
adorned with jewels, the hosts of lovely women, e e!

**2906.** Holder of the flute, holder of the mountain, with graceful
red lips, beautiful, e e!
With teeth [like] kunda jasmine, charming, slender-waisted,
the Purandara of the Vrinda forest, e e!

**2907.** Krishna, the foremost champion of love-quarrels,
dha dha dhi dhi ta ga dhenna e e!
Sa sa ri ga ri — the Lord of Narahari,
e i a iti ai ai atenna e e!

**2908.** *The shari says — an example of a medini song:*
Glory! Worshipped by the world, the renowned princess,
Radhika, moon-faced, dispeller of sorrow!
Delighter of Shyama's heart, breaker of [His] firm composure,
with doe's eyes that put to shame the lotus, the khanjana and the fish!

**2909.** Whose luster has conquered the lightning, supremely charming, the beautiful lady,
crusher of the pride of the Daughter of the Ocean and others,
with a lovely gentle smile, with graceful sweet speech, enchantress of the world,
increaser of the joy of Lalita and the others![^710]

**2910.** Lovely in the adornment of love, [in] pastimes ever new and new,
the delight of the Vrinda forest, with an elephant's gait,
rapt in the rasa of the rasa dance, a river of sweetness,
the jewel of all women, the mistress of Narahari!

**2911.** Jhanta jham jhanta takha vitakato thunna drimiki
trigao takata ta thaiya.
Sari rigama pamaga manma gari sas sati ai
tenna tenna tenam ti ai e a.[^711]

**2912.** *The cuckoo says — an example of a nandini song with five angas:*
Glory, glory, Krishna, merciful Keshava,
lotus-eyed, delighter of the people, a!
Elephant in the lotus forest of young maidens, charming
khanjana in the cage of the beloved's heart, a!

**2913.** With a sweet smile on the lovely moon of the face,
breaker of the weight of Radha's composure, a!
Beautiful, best of dancers, son of Nanda,
collyrium for the eyes of ever new young maidens, a!

**2914.** Sari gama gama pama manmama garisa
tenna tennati ati ai iya.
Ai, increaser of Narahari's joy, e e,
ai ati ai tiya.

**2915.** O Srinivasa! The birds in various ways
sing the fair fame of Radha and Krishna in shuddha songs.

**2916.** The varieties of song prabandhas cannot be told;
the shastras define [them] by shakti, varna, particular features and the rest.[^712]

**2917.** Ela and the rest are difficult; among them are twenty-six songs;
what is easy and what is hard in this the shastras have revealed.[^713]

**2918.** The first is named Panchataleshvara;
after it come four varieties of Varnasvara:

**2919.** Varnasvara beginning with svara, Varnasvara beginning with patha,
Varnasvara beginning with pada, Varnasvara beginning with tena.

**2920.** Next they name the song Svarartha-matrika.
Thus the knowers of song define the twenty-six.

**2921.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*There are difficult prabandhas — Ela and the rest — spoken of by the sages;*
*of them twenty-six are named by the learned Harinayaka.*

**2922.** *And they are listed in order, by name alone:*
*Panchataleshvara, Varnasvara and Angacharini,*[^714]

**2923.** *and also Svarartha-matrika, Ragakadambaka,*
*Svaradya-karana, then Vartani, and likewise Talarnava,*

**2924.** *Shriranga, Shrivilasa, then Panchabhangi,*
*Panchanana, Matilaka, and also Simhanila.*

**2925.** *Tribhangi, Hamsanila, and also Harivilasaka,*
*Sudarshana, Svaranga, Shrivardhana, Harshavardhana,*

**2926.** *Vira, Shrimangala, and Lahadi is also named;*
*[the prabandha] called by the name Navaratna is named, and also Sarabhanilaka,*[^715]

**2927.** *and the one that is called Kanthabharana: these are held to be the twenty-six.*
*There are also six others — Chandraprakashaka and the rest.*

**2928.** The characteristics of all these prabandhas are well known;
the poets describe [them] so that all hearts are delighted.

**2929.** At Vrinda's bidding the bee, with great eagerness,
sings a svarartha prabandha in the guise of humming.

**2930.** The syllables of the svarartha prabandha are sa, ri, ga, ma and the rest;
in two kinds, shuddha and mishra, [it] expresses [meaning] at will.[^716]

**2931.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*Where the desired meaning is expressed by the syllables of the svaras alone,*
*that [prabandha] is svarartha; it is twofold, by the distinction of shuddha and mishra.*
*(If by the syllables of the svaras — sa, ri, ga, ma, pa, dha, ni — the desired meaning is expressed at will, then it is svarartha: such is the sense.)*

**2932.** Joyfully the bee reveals the svarartha prabandha;
hearing [it], Sri Lalita and the other sakhis are filled with happiness.

**2933.** *For example — the raga Kedara:*[^717]
Glory! Crown of rasikas, Krishna, tender-limbed,
shining like dense collyrium!
With the moon of [Your] face anointed with the nectar of smiles,
making the directions clear with lovely rays!

**2934.** With charming wide eyes that have conquered the lotus,
stealer of the wealth of the young maidens' composure!
Triumphant in Vraja, the young crown prince, the best of dancers,
holder of the flute, with reddish lips!

**2935.** Crusher of the pride of the Lord of Rati, enjoyer of the sweet rasa dance,
beautiful, incomparable!
Graceful, like a jewel, greedily drawn to the fragrance
of the lotus faces of the jewels among the women of Vraja![^718]

**2936.** King of the new kunjas, subduer of the serpent,
in charming attire of every kind!
Increaser of Ghanashyama's joy — pama gamamma gari
mapa dhani padha nidha![^719]

**2937.** Thus Vrinda directs a multitude of birds;
with songs of various prabandhas they all give delight.

**2938.** O Srinivasa! Krishna with His beloveds in the rasa dance
reveals the highest limit of the prabandhas of shuddha song.

**2939.** Within shuddha some speak of shuda prabandha;
others reveal shuda within chhayalaga.[^720]

**2940.** *Now, chhayalaga:*
Because [it] clings to the shadow of shuddha, it is called "chhayalaga";
in it shuda is composed with talas, instruments and the rest.

**2941.** The weaving together of many talas — such is the charming shuda;
the term "chhayalaga" has another name, salaga.[^721]

**2942.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*That which clings to the shadow of shuddha they know as chhayalaga;*
*it is charming, composed as shuda with talas, instruments and the rest.*
*(Shuda is the weaving together of many talas in one: such is the sense. By the words "clings to the shadow" it is said that it arises with some of the characteristics of shuddha: such is the sense.)*

**2943.** *It is said of this:*
*If [in it] there is only a shadow of the features stated [above],*
*it is to be known as chhayalaga — [so say] the munis Bharata and the others.*

**2944.** *(It also has another name, salaga: such is the sense.) Of this Harinayaka has said:*
*Now, the shuda that [is] chhayalaga is itself salaga.*

**2945.** According to differing views the salaga shudas are many;
and so they define dhruvaka and the rest [as] excellent.

**2946.** *Thus [it is said] in Damodara and in the Panchama-sara-samhita:*
*Dhruvaka, manthaka, pratimantha, nisharuka,*
*vasaka, pratitala, and also ekatalika,*
*yati and jhumari — thus is the salaga shuda named.*[^722]

**2947.** *[Now] the varieties of dhruvaka and the rest are told:*
*Dhruvakas are said to be sixteen, manthakas of six kinds,*
*pratimanthas five, nisharukas are known to be seven;*

**2948.** *vasakas are said to be four, pratitalakas four,*
*ekatali of three kinds, yatis are held to be four;*

**2949.** *and jhumari is only one: these are the salagas named.*

**2950.** *Some say there are ten other salagas — charcharika and the rest;*
*thus they, the salagas, come to be nineteen — a multitude.*[^723]

**2951.** The characteristics of dhruvaka and the rest are extremely difficult;
shuda in nine talas — it gives joy in every way.

**2952.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*Adi, yati, nasaru, and also addatala, triputa,*
*rupaka, jhampaka, mantha, ekatali — so [the talas] are named.*[^724]

**2953.** *That which is composed with these nine talas is called shuda;*
*such a shuda is charming in singing, in playing instruments and in dance.*

**2954.** The varieties of prabandhas — shuda and the rest — are manifold;
their characteristics, examples and so on are set forth in the shastras.

**2955.** In song tala is joined; without tala there is no purity —
as it is with a boat without a helmsman.

**2956.** The derivation of the word "tala" [is explained] in many ways;
the acharyas have made this known.

**2957.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*Without tala the purity of song does not arise in song and the rest,*
*as with a boat without a helmsman; therefore we set them forth.*[^725]

**2958.** *Here the acharyas have explained the derivation of the word "tala" in many ways.*

**2959.** *Harinayaka [says] of this:*
*Because by the evenness of time and, moreover, by its charm*
*it establishes sangita, it is called tala.*
*("Talayati" — establishes; the root "tal" is in the sense of "establishing.")*

**2960.** *But in the Sangita-sara:*
*"Ta" is Isha, Girija is "la";*
*hence tala, from the union of Shiva and Shakti.*
*Or else here "tala" is from the root "tal" with [the suffix] ghañ;*
*or "tala" is from the union of "ta" and "la."*[^726]

**2961.** *In the Ratna-mala:*
*"Ta" is Sharajanma, "a" is called Vishnu,*
*"la" is declared to be Maruta: these gods dwell in the tala.*[^727]

**2962.** *But Vachaspati [says]:*
*The action — stretching and bending of the fingers of the hand and the rest —*
*and the measure of time [set] by it is here called tala.*[^728]

**2963.** *Now [it is told] of the talas:*
The talas chachchatputa, chachaputa and the rest are the chief ones;
a hundred and one talas — [this] is accepted everywhere.

**2964.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*Chachchatputa, chachaputa, and also shatpitaputraka,*
*sampakveshtaka, udghatta, aditala and darpana,*[^729]

**2965.** *charchari, simhanila, kandarpa, simhavikrama,*
*shriranga, ranganila, rangatala, parikrama,*

**2966.** *pratyanga, gajalila, tribhinna, viravikrama,*
*hamsanila, varnanila, and also rajachudamani,*

**2967.** *rangadyota, rajatala, and also simhavikridita,*
*vanamali, varnatala, mishra, rangapradipaka,*

**2968.** *hamsanada, simhanada, the one called mallikamoda,*
*then sharabhalila and rangabharana,*

**2969.** *then turagalila, and after it simhanandana,*
*jayashri, vijayananda, pratitala, dvitiyaka,*

**2970.** *makaranda, kirtitala, vijaya, jayamangala,*
*rajavidyadhara, mantha, jayatala, sudurbala,*[^730]

**2971.** *then nihsharuka, krida, tribhangi, kokilapriya,*
*shrikanta, bindumali, samatala and nandana,*

**2972.** *udikshana, mallika, dhenkika, varnamanthika,*
*abhinanda, antarakrida, laghutala and dipaka,*

**2973.** *anangatala, vishama, sandikunda and mukundaka,*
*ekatali, kankala, chatustala and khamkhudi,*[^731]

**2974.** *abhanga, rajavankara, and also laghushekhara,*
*and another, pratapashekhara, jagajhampa, chaturmukha,*

**2975.** *vankara, pratimantha, and also the tala tritiyaka;*
*after it, as should be known, vasanta, lalita, shiva,*

**2975a.** *karashakha, shattala, vardhana, and also vardhaka;*
*then, as the learned declare, rajanarayana;*
*madana too should be known, and also parvatilochana.*[^732]

**2976.** *Then there is sarangatala, then shrinandivardhana,*
*and another, lilavilokita, and also lalitapriya,*

**2977.** *janaka, lakshmisha, the one called ragavardhana,*
*and utsava: thus the talas are one more than a hundred.*

**2978.** *In Damodara and others some of these are found otherwise;*
*since the opinions of the rishis are many, what harm is there in variants among them?*

**2979.** The characteristics and examples of all these talas
the knowers of sangita have joyfully made known.

**2980.** The angas of tala are fivefold, beginning with anudruta, [they] say,
and they define the rules: [how many] matras in laghu and the rest.[^733]

**2981.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*Anudruta, druta, laghu, and after it guru*
*and pluta: thus in order the angas of tala are fivefold.*
*Apart from anudruta, the names of the others are [from the syllables] da, la, ga, pa.*

**2982.** *Laghu is of one matra, guru of two matras,*
*pluta of three matras, druta of half a matra,*
*and anudruta of half of druta's matra,*
*and its name may also be "virama."*

**2983.** *Anudruta, druta, laghu, guru, pluta — such are the signs.*
*(The signs are these: …)*[^734]

**2984.** *[Now] the places of their strokes, with [their] measure, are told:*[^735]
Druta is a stroke of the hand [from a height of] four fingers;
la, ga, pa — eight, sixteen, twenty-four.

**2985.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*For [the stroke] called druta the height is said to be four fingers.*[^736]
*("Uchchhrita" means "high.")*
*Laghu is declared to be of eight fingers, guru is of sixteen fingers,*
*and pluta of three times eight fingers; and druta is a slight movement of the hand.*[^737]

**2986.** *Now [it is told] of the ways of marking them:*
Tala is marked in two ways: with sound and without sound;
in guru, pluta and the rest a soundless [stroke] is needed.[^738]

**2987.** Tala: in guru, one sounded, one soundless;
in pluta, one sounded, two soundless; in anudruta,[^739]

**2988.** laghu and druta the tala is without soundless [strokes].
With a loud stroke of the hand the tala is called sounded.

**2989.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*The marking of tala is twofold: with sound and without sound.*
*A loud stroke is sounded; laghu has only one [stroke].*

**2990.** *For guru a double stroke is declared: one is sound, the other soundless;*
*and [the stroke] that goes to half — from half the sound of laghu — [is called] druta.*[^740]

**2991.** *In pluta there is one sounded stroke, then two strokes;*
*they are soundless: one [is made] upward, and the other downward.*

**2992.** To the varieties of tala there is no end;
in the Sri rasa-mandala they all became embodied.

**2993.** Krishna, joining both hands in a sweet curve,
keeps the tala thus — with what can it be compared?

**2994.** Sri Radhika, displaying a wondrous grace,
joins hand to hand, smiling slightly.

**2995.** The wonder that arises in the blending of the sounds
of the strokes of the hands, the bracelets and the rest — who will describe it?

**2996.** Strokes of the hands in many ways, many talas in the songs —
Lakshmi and the others are amazed: with what can it be compared?

**2997.** All of Radhika's companions are wonderful:
one in one tala, another in another, reveals songs.

**2998.** Singing in chhayalaga is extremely difficult;
the lovely Lalita joyfully reveals it.

**2999.** Krishna, supremely fond of play, to Lalita and the others
gave leave to sing kshudra songs.

**3000.** *Now [it is told] of kshudra song:*
Mere words joined with tala and dhatu are kshudra song;
the dhatus are udgraha and the rest, described before, as is fitting.

**3001.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*Mere words joined with tala and dhatu are called kshudra.*

**3002.** A kshudra song is for the most part like shuddha and salaga;
consonance at the ends [of the lines] in it the shastras call praiseworthy.

**3003.** The kinds of kshudra song are four: chitrapada, and also
chitrakala, dhruvapada, panchali — [so] it is known.[^741]

**3004.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*It is indeed fourfold: among them chitrapada is the first,*
*[then] chitrakala, dhruvapada and panchali — by their distinctions.*

**3005.** The characteristics of these songs are very extensive.
By the variety of its padas the [song] called chitrapada is known.[^742]

**3006.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*Where variety is seen in the padas alone,*
*and there is no variety in dhatu and the rest, know that as chitrapada.*
*(Variety of padas, moreover, is the possession of qualities: gentle consonance, clarity and the rest.)*
*Such is chitrapada.*

**3007.** *Now, chitrakala:*
Chitrakala: in the dhruva the matras are fewer, in the other [parts] equal;
from three to eight lines — such is the rule of this song.[^743]

**3008.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*If in the udgraha and abhoga the matras are equal, but in the dhruva fewer,*
*[and the song] is rich in lines — from three up to eight — know: that is chitrakala.*

**3009.** The characteristics of dhruvapada and the rest are known everywhere.
In the vernacular and in Sanskrit they sing many kinds of songs.

**3010.** Songs in Sanskrit, in the vernacular and others are well known;
the knowers of sangita define [their] kinds: divya and the rest.[^744]

**3011.** *This is said:*
*Song is divya, manusha, and also divya-manusha.*
*Divya is composed in Sanskrit, manusha arises from Prakrit;*

**3012.** *that arising from Sanskrit and Prakrit is called divya-manusha.*
*And some consider as manusha [a song] in a language that arose in a particular country.*

**3013.** *Anga, Vanga, Kalinga and other [countries] are the sources of regional languages and the rest.*
*In whatever countries whatever languages are cherished,*
*those, taken from the speech of the people there, one should employ [in song].*[^745]

**3014.** Some define the particulars of the composition of song and the rest:
three varieties, named sama, ardhasama and vishama.[^746]

**3015.** *Thus [it is said] in Kohala:*
*Song is threefold: sama, ardhasama and vishama.*
*With four lines of equal number of matras [it] is called sama.*

**3016.** *The song whose first and third lines are equal, and the second and fourth equal too,*
*is named ardhasama.*

**3017.** *If all four lines of [a song] differ in their number of matras,*
*that song the sages Bharata and the others call vishama.*

**3018.** What further particulars there are in song — what do others know of that?
In the pastimes of the Sri rasa Krishna reveals everything.

**3019.** At the singing of the sakhis Krishna, rejoicing at heart,
praising [them] much, embraces the sakhis.

**3020.** From the embrace of the sakhis comes Radhika's great happiness.
What do others know of the delight that grows in song?

**3021.** What can be said — the qualities of song are of many kinds,
all those that shine in the Sri rasa-mandala.

**3022.** *Now, the qualities of song:*
The qualities of song the knowers of song have proclaimed:
graha, laya, yati, variety of mana,[^747]

**3023.** repetition of dhatu, and also newness,
manifold meaning of the matu words, pleasingness of the raga,

**3024.** gamaka, clarity of meaning, the charming
combination of tenna, patha and svara in various ways.

**3025.** Know: these are the qualities of song — graha and the rest, nine;
in this there are further varieties of many kinds.[^748]

**3026.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*Now the qualities of song: graha, laya and yati, variety of mana;*
*repetition of dhatu and newness, manifold meaning*
*of matu, pleasingness of the raga, then gamaka, clarity of meaning,*
*and the combination of tennas, svara and patha in various ways.*

**3027.** *Moreover:*
*Of all these qualities the most necessary is this:*
*the inclusion of speech possessed of excellences, ornaments and rasa.*

**3028.** Graha and the rest — all the qualities that [here] are defined —
the wise expound in many ways.

**3029.** *Among them, on graha:*
Graha is anagata, sama, atita — these three;
anagata graha and the rest — such are the three names.[^749]

**3030.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*Tala makes the course of song even; its grahas are three,*
*named anagata, sama and atita — so it is held everywhere.*

**3031.** *On anagata:*
When the tala is taken before the beginning of the song,
all call this the anagata graha.

**3032.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*When before the beginning of the song two syllables are uttered*
*[and then] the tala is set, this is called anagata graha.*

**3033.** *(The meaning here is this: the syllable taken in excess at the beginning of the song is "anagata": it never enters within the tala.)*

**3034.** *On sama:*
If tala and song arise at the same time,
then the wise call it the sama graha.

**3035.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*When the tala coincides [with the song] from the very utterance of the song,*
*then, because they arise at one time, this is called sama graha.*

**3036.** So also the atita graha; its kinds are many:
the knowers of sangita have set [them] forth in various ways.

**3037.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*If the kala that will fall later is placed at the beginning*
*and [thus] the tala is sung, then this is called tala graha.*[^750]

**3038.** *Now, laya:*
Laya — [when] the evenness of the actions of graha and the rest is manifest;
it has three kinds: druta, vilambita, madhya.[^751]

**3039.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*The mutual evenness of the actions — of song, instruments and the placing of steps —*
*or else [the evenness] of action and tala: this the wise call laya.*
*So [says] Vachaspati.*[^752]

**3040.** *Harinayaka, however, [says]: "The pause between actions is called laya."*
*The wise have called it threefold: druta, madhya and vilambita.*

**3041.** *Druta is of one matra; madhya has a pause twice [that of] druta;*
*vilambita is double [madhya]; all of these belong to all the talas.*

**3042.** Some by this define tala;
but in all views laya is a particular form of singing.

**3043.** *On yati:*
The rule for carrying the laya is yati;
its three kinds: srotovaha, sama, gopuchchhika.[^753]

**3044.** In these three particular [kinds] of pause are defined;
this the wise expound in many ways.

**3045.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*Yati is indeed the rule for carrying the laya;*
*it is threefold: srotovaha, sama and gopuchchhika.*

**3046.** Srotovaha, sama, gopuchchhika — the three [kinds] of laya;
[their] characteristics are easy; know: the shastras expound [them].

**3047.** *On mana:*
The action of the tala that gives a pause is called mana;
one avarta bears the name vardhamana,

**3048.** the second avarta is hiyamana — [so] it is determined;
the characteristics of these two, know, are known [as] easy.[^754]

**3049.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*The action of the tala that gives a pause is here called mana;*
*since it gives the tala a pause, mana completes the tala.*

**3050.** *If it falls on the second kala in the dhruva, then*
*the avarta in the tala is called vardhamana — so the knowers of tala hold.*

**3051.** *But if the mana falls on the last kala in the dhruva, then*
*the avarta is called hiyamana — so the wise have said.*

**3052.** *Now, repetition of dhatu:*
The way of repetition of dhatu the worthy describe:
the parts of the song are sung again and again — anew.

**3053.** *Manifold meaning of the matu words:*
The manifold meaning of the matu words they define thus:
in a turn of speech of one [and the same] sense [words] are not used.

**3054.** The characteristics of dhatu and matu [I] have made known before;
since [it is] easy, there was no detailed account.

**3055.** *On the pleasingness of the raga:*
The evident pleasingness of the raga destroys many sorrows;
the knowers of raga reveal [its] qualities: being pleasant to the ear and the rest.

**3056.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*Pleasant to the ear, abiding in yati, with angas, bringing happiness,*
*rich in [the registers] mandra, madhya and tara — [such is] the desired pleasingness of a raga.*

**3057.** *On gamaka:*
The nature of gamaka is the vibration of a svara;
in the hearts of the listeners it gives rise to great happiness.

**3058.** The kinds of gamaka are fifteen;
the characteristics of all, in order, beginning with tiripa, are set forth.

**3059.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*The vibration of a svara, bringing happiness to the listener's heart, is gamaka.*
*Its varieties are tiripa, sphurita, and also kampita,*

**3060.** *nila, andolita, vali, tribhinna, kuvala, ahata,*
*unnamita, plavita, hunkrita, and also mudrita,*
*namita and mishrita — thus fifteen are named.*[^755]

**3061.** *Now [it is told] of their characteristics:*
*Lovely in its imitation of the lightest vibration of the sound of the damaru,*
*with a speed of a quarter of a druta — [the gamaka] is called tiripa.*[^756]

**3062.** *With a speed made of a third of a druta, [the gamaka] is held to be sphurita;*
*with singing at half the measure of a druta, [the gamaka] is called kampita.*

**3063.** *Nila is with the speed of a druta, andolita with the speed of a laghu;*
*vali is joined with various curves — as the raga [requires].*

**3064.** *Tribhinna is a dense sound without pauses in the three registers;*
*kuvala is that same vali, knotted, soft to the throat.*

**3065.** *Ahata is when, striking the svara ahead, one turns back;*
*unnamita is said to be that which to svaras higher and higher*

**3066.** *passes in order; plavita is a vibration in prolonged singing;*
*hunkrita is held to be [the gamaka] filled with a charming sound "hum."*[^757]

**3067.** *Mudrita is the gamaka born of closed lips;*
*namita — so the knowers of sound call [the gamaka] from the lowering of the svaras.*

**3068.** *From the mixing of these comes mishrita; it has numerous varieties;*
*I have not named them, since they are unfit for use and unknowable.*

**3069.** *The way of practising this:*
*At night in the months of Magha and Pausha, only in the last watch,*
*standing in water, the practitioner should master these gamakas.*[^758]

**3069a.** These are the fifteen kinds of gamaka;
some, by the distinction of the seven svaras, name seven kinds.[^759]

**3069b.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*The vibration of a svara is gamaka; by the distinction of the svaras it is sevenfold.*
*(The meaning is this: by the distinction of the seven svaras there are seven kinds. This, moreover, is seen in the Narada-samhita.)*

**3070.** *Now, clarity of meaning:*
When on utterance all in the words is understood,
a faultless meaning full of rasa is called clarity.

**3071.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*When on the utterance of speech the meaning is fully grasped,*
*[and] the meaning is pleasant, faultless and with rasa — that indeed is clarity of meaning.*

**3072.** *The placing of tena, patha and svara with variety:*
*tena is used after patha and svara, and never at the beginning.*

**3073.** What fault arises in song from the absence of the qualities and the rest —
of that know something; this the knowers of song expound at length.

**3074.** Without tala — illness, without dhatu — loss of wealth;
a song without dhatu, matu and pada is an enemy.

**3075.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*Without tala — illness of the body, without dhatu — loss of wealth;*
*where there are no dhatu, matu and pada, that song is an enemy.*

**3076.** *Now [it is told] of the faults of song:*
Some say the faults of song are of many kinds;
others define [them] briefly: stumbling of speech and the rest.

**3077.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*The faults in songs are: stumbling of speech and the rest,*
*composition lacking tala and the rest,*
*impairment of dhatu, matu and the rest, harsh words,*
*loss of rasa and the rest, unpleasantness to the ear.*

**3078.** *Although in songs there are many such and similar faults,*
*they are not named [here]; if [one needs] to grasp them in knowledge, let one look into this and that [in the books].*[^760]

**3079.** One who sings a song is called a singer;
the characteristics of a singer are set forth in various ways.

**3080.** *[It is told] of the characteristics of a singer:*
Singers are of three kinds: the best, the middling, the lowest;
the characteristics of these three the shastras set forth plainly.

**3081.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*A singer is said to be of three kinds: the best, the middling, the lowest.*
*With a pure-sounding voice, with a good sharira, knowing the distinctions of many ragas,*[^761]

**3082.** *endowed with graha, mana and laya, knowing tala, having conquered fatigue,*
*with easy and graceful movement in gamakas touching the three sthanas,*[^762]

**3083.** *skilled in singing prabandhas, intent on careful performance,*
*master of his voice, knowing the sthayi, faultless, endowed with [good] retention —*

**3084.** *[such is] the best. The middling is named [one endowed] with some of these qualities;*
*but one who, though endowed with qualities, is full of faults is called the lowest.*

**3085.** There is also [a division] into five kinds — shikshakara and the rest:
one skilled in teaching [is called] a shikshakara; thus are known the shikshakara and the others.

**3086.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*Shikshakara, anukara, rasika, and also ranjaka*
*and bhavaka — thus the knowers of song have called the singer fivefold.*

**3087.** *One skilled in teaching without omission is held by the virtuous to be a shikshakara;*
*one who imitates the manner of others is called an anukara.*

**3088.** *One absorbed in rasa is a rasika; one who delights the listeners is a ranjaka;*
*one who imparts a special excellence to the song is called a bhavaka.*

**3089.** The wise further speak of singers of three kinds:
one, two, many — these are the plain characteristics.

**3090.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*The singer is of three kinds: ekala, yamala and vrinda.*
*One who sings entirely alone is an ekala singer;*
*one who has a second is a yamala; one who is with a group is a vrinda singer.*

**3091.** *[It is told] of the faults of a singer:*
The faults of a singer are of many kinds:
timidity, indistinct words and the rest are well known in the shastras.

**3092.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*Timid, with indistinct words, shaking his head, puffing, off-key,*
*with clenched teeth, closing his eyes, and also unsteady in the grama,*
*singing with a twisted neck, [now] scanty, [now] excessive in svaras, mixing ragas,*
*with trembling body, inattentive, making [the song] insipid, crow-voiced, hasty.*
*("Crow" means with a harsh cry.)*

**3093.** *Moreover:*
*falling out of tala, stretching the body of the song, frightful in appearance, goat-throated, unsteady,*
*with puffed-out cheeks, nasal — such indeed is a faulty singer.*

**3094.** *There are many other faults, not named for fear of prolixity;*
*these unnamed merits and faults are to be learned from other books.*[^763]

**3095.** Ragas, yakara and the rest — however many kinds [there are] —
the knowers of sangita have set this forth at length.[^764]

**3096.** These songs and the rest are transcendental: there is not a trace of fault in them;
in passing I have said a little, [only] to give an indication.

**3097.** Krishna, the Supreme Person, is free from [worldly] merits and faults;
whatever lila He performs captivates everyone.

**3098.** He is a Person not of this world, yet [His] lila is like the world's;
seeing and hearing [it], grass, wood and stone melt.

**3099.** Whoever describes it in whatever way,
freed from the poison of bad association, attains those feet.[^765]

**3100.** O Srinivasa, what shall I say of the delights of the rasa dance!
Krishna reveals all of this together with His beloveds.

**3101.** Nada, shruti, svaras and the rest — however many kinds [there are] —
even Bharata and the other munis find no end to them.

**3102.** That at which Brahma and the others are filled with the greatest wonder —
all such marvels [He] reveals in song.

**3103.** Songs in refined Sanskrit and in the languages of various lands
He sings in all those styles, describing [them].[^766]

**3104.** Now He sings alone, now together with Radhika —
who can describe the manner of singing of Them both?

**3105.** Now together with Lalita and the other sakhis
Radhika and Krishna sing with wondrous modulations.

**3106.** All those sounds of the voice are the essence of nectar;
in them is the wondrous movement of various gamakas.

**3107.** Hearing that singing, no one can remain composed;
there is nothing in the universe to compare it with.

**3108.** As the singing is, so are the various instruments — the greatest wonder;
the sound of the instruments steals away the composure of the three worlds.

**3109.** *Now [it is told] of instruments:*
Through instruments song and tala [gain] beauty; the instruments are of four [kinds]:
tata, anaddha, shushira and the one called ghana — so the shastras say.[^767]

**3110.** Tata is the vina and the rest, anaddha the muraja and the rest;
the vamshi and the rest are shushira, the kamsya-tala and the rest ghana.

**3111.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*Since without instruments neither song nor tala shines,*
*therefore here [the teaching on] the auspicious instrument is set forth by us.*

**3112.** *Tata, anaddha, shushira and ghana — thus [instruments] are of four kinds.*
*Tata is the instrument vina and the rest, anaddha the muraja and the rest,*
*the vamshi and the rest are shushira, the kamsya-tala and the rest ghana.*

**3113.** *In the Sangita-damodara:*
*Tata, shushira, anaddha, ghana — in this way [instruments] are of four kinds.*
*Tata is the instrument with strings, shushira the vamsha and the rest,*
*anaddha is that covered with hide, ghana is held to be the tala and the rest.*

**3114.** Of [these] four I make known a little, only the names;
the knowers of sangita reveal the characteristics of the instruments and the rest.

**3115.** *Tata, namely:*
The tata instruments are the alavani, the brahma-vina, and also
the kinnari, the laghu-kinnari and the rest — so it is known.

**3116.** *Thus [it is said] in the Sangita-damodara:*
*Alavani, brahma-vina, kinnari, laghu-kinnari,*
*vipanchi, vallaki, jyeshtha, chitra, ghoshavati, jaya,*

**3117.** *hastika, kubjika, kurma, sarangi, parivadini,*
*trishari, shatatantri, and also nakulaushthi and kamsari,*

**3118.** *audumbari, pinaki, nibandha, and also pushkala,*
*gadavaranahasta, rudra, then svara-mandala,*
*kavilasa, madhusyandi, ghona and the rest — [these] are tata.*[^768]

**3119.** *And likewise:*
*Another vina is the kachchhapi; it is sometimes [called] rupavati.*
*(That is, this same one is called rupavati.)*
*("Rudra" is the rudra-vina.)*

**3120.** *Anaddha, namely:*
Know the varieties of anaddha: [the one] called mardala, and also
the muraja, dhakka, pataha and the rest — so it is known.

**3121.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*The mardala, and also the muraja, dhakka, pataha, changava,*
*panava, kundali, bheri, ghata-vadya and jharjhara,*

**3122.** *damaru, tamaki, mantha, hudukka, maddu, dindima,*
*upanga, dardura and the rest — [these] are named anaddha.*[^769]

**3123.** The mardala is the best of the anaddha; its name is mridanga;
[it is] of two kinds: made of wood and of clay.

**3124.** This mardala is the best of all instruments; in union with it
all instruments gain beauty — it is known in the shastras.

**3125.** In the mridanga Brahma and the other gods ever reside;
[its sound] is supremely auspicious, captivating everyone.

**3126.** *Thus [it is said] in the Sangita-darpana:*
*Among the anaddha the mardala is the best.*[^770]

**3127.** *In the Sangita-damodara:*
*Mridangas are said to be made also of clay.*
*Thus the mardala is called the very best of all instruments;*
*having attained union with it, every instrument shines.*

**3128.** *In the Sangita-parijata:*
*In the middle part of the mridanga Brahma always dwells;*
*as the gods abide in his world, so they are present here too;*
*since the mridanga contains all the gods, it is all-auspicious.*

**3129.** The making of the mridanga, the varieties of instruments, [their] characteristics and the rest
the knowers of sangita describe in various ways.

**3130.** Some say the varnas arising from the instrument are twenty;
others say something of the fine rules for arranging the varnas.[^771]

**3131.** *Thus [it is said] in the Sangita-parijata:*
*The patha-varnas composed by Umapati are twenty, and so on.*[^772]

**3132.** The characteristics of a mridanga player are many:
"steady," "skilled in playing the instrument" and the rest — so some say.

**3133.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*Steady, skilled in playing the instrument, eloquent, clearly bringing out the syllables of the patha,*
*devoted to the practice of tala, able to display mature mastery in all the gamakas,*
*skilled in dancing with the turns of varied playing, well versed in the course of the song,*
*contented, playing with ease, quick-handed — such is the celebrated mridanga player.*

**3134.** All this the knowers of sangita have set forth at length.
The varieties of shushira instruments are a true elixir.

**3135.** *Now, shushira:*
Many varieties of shushira instruments are defined:
the vamshi, pavi, madhuri, tittiri, shankha and the rest.

**3136.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*The vamsha, and also the pavi, madhuri, tittiri, shankha, kahala,*
*dodahi, murali, bukka, shringika, svaranabhi,*

**3137.** *the shringa, the lapika-vamsha and, besides, the charma-vamsha —*
*these varieties of shushira were named by the ancient sages.*[^773]

**3138.** The characteristics of what is called the vamsha are many in the shastras:
graceful, straight, free from defects in the joints and the rest.

**3139.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*Graceful, straight, devoid of defects in the joints;*
*of bamboo, but it may also be of khadira, or of red sandalwood,*
*("Of bamboo" means a vamsha [of bamboo].)*

**3140.** *or of white sandalwood, or golden, or made of ivory;*
*it has an inner bore equal to the little finger — and so on.*

**3141.** The length of the vamshika is from six fingers
up to eighteen fingers: such is the view of the shastras.

**3142.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*This vamsha may be of five fingers; with an increase of one finger at a time*
*it receives the names "of six fingers" and so on — up to eighteen fingers.*

**3143.** By their measure in fingers the flutes have many names;
the excellent ones — Mahananda and the rest — the shastras define.[^774]

**3144.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*Mahananda, and also Nanda, Vijaya and Jaya —*
*these four best vamshas are approved by the muni Matanga.*[^775]

**3145.** *Of ten fingers is Mahananda, Nanda of eleven fingers,*
*of the measure of twelve fingers, it is declared, is Vijaya,*
*of the measure of fourteen fingers is what is called Jaya.*

**3146.** The merits, faults and the rest of the flute the wise reveal;
all this is known. [Now] I make known the ghana instruments.

**3147.** *Now, ghana:*
Among the ghana instruments are the karatala, the kamsya-vala, and also
the jaya-ghanta, the shuktika and the rest — of various kinds.

**3148.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*Karatala, kamsya-vala, jaya-ghanta, and also shuktika,*
*kampaka, ghata-vadya, ghanta-todya and gharghara,*

**3149.** *jhanjha-tala, manjira, kartari and uddhura —*
*these twelve are named by the lord of sages [instruments] called ghana.*[^776]

**3150.** The characteristics of the karatala and the rest are set forth in the shastras.
To the tata and other instruments the gods and others have their right.

**3151.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*Tata instruments are for the gods, saushira for the Gandharvas,*
*anaddha for the Rakshasas, and ghana for men, as is known.*[^777]

**3152.** Great good fortune dawned for all these instruments:
in the Sri rasa-mandala an incomparable beauty arose.

**3153.** O Srinivasa! How wondrous is the way of the rasa:
various instruments sound there that enchant Brahma and the others.

**3154.** The son of the king of Vraja, skilled in all instruments,
surrounded by His beloveds, enchants millions of Kandarpas.[^778]

**3155.** He plays the flute — with what wondrous grace!
In the three worlds there is no comparison to give for this beauty.

**3156.** In mandra, madhya and tara — a captivating alapa of svaras;
hearing the sound of the flute, Maheshvara is overwhelmed.

**3157.** Radha, the enchantress of the worlds, the embodiment of rasa,
plays the alavani instrument in the pure manner.[^779]

**3158.** Shadja, madhyama and gandhara — the three gramas:
as they are revealed in song, so She reveals [them] on the instrument.

**3159.** Lalita playfully plays the brahma-vina —
she who is skilled in revealing shrutis and the rest on the instrument.[^780]

**3160.** The beautiful Vishakha, with great sweet grace,
plays the kachchhapi-vina in various manners.

**3161.** The beautiful Suchitra plays the rudra-vina,
gracefully revealing the distinctions of svaras and jatis.

**3162.** Champakalatika joyfully plays the vipanchi,
surpassing all in revealing murchhanas, talas and the rest.

**3163.** Rangadevi gracefully plays the yantraka:
how wondrously the gamakas appear in it![^781]

**3164.** The beautiful Sudevi joyfully plays the sarangi:
in it the varieties of ragas and the prabandhas are revealed.

**3165.** Tungavidya delightedly plays the kinnari,
pouring a rain of nectar in the Sri rasa-mandala.

**3166.** Indulekha joyfully plays the svara-mandala,
effortlessly revealing the distinctions of svaras.[^782]

**3167.** All the hosts of Sri Radhika's sakhis, as many as there are,
are all absorbed in every way in playing all the instruments.

**3168.** Some play the mardala and the madal in every way,
revealing wondrous talas unheard of in the world.[^783]

**3169.** Some play the muraja and the upanga instrument,
at the sound of which no composure remains in the heart.[^784]

**3170.** Some play the damaru with the utmost skill;
the damaru is dear to Shiva — this is known in the world.

**3171.** *Thus [it is said] in the Sangita-parijata:*
*The damaru, let it be known, is two fists [in size], two-faced, slender in the middle;*
*its faces, one fist in measure, are covered with fine skin.*

**3172.** *It is played with two knots on cords attached to it;*
*this instrument ever shines in the hand of the Lord of Uma.*[^785]

**3173.** Some play karatalas and other instruments;
the Sri rasa-mandala is filled with the splendor of the instruments.

**3174.** All the hosts of Sri Radhika's sakhis, as many as there are —
who could tell how lovely they are with their various instruments?

**3175.** The sounds of all the instruments blend into one — how wondrous!
It is as if a rain of nectar falls in the Sri rasa-mandala.

**3176.** The heart of Sri Vrinda-devi is full of great joy:
she supplies wondrous instruments unknown to the shastras.

**3177.** Rai and Kanu, immersed in the rasa of the instruments,
dance with great jubilation in their hearts.

**3178.** Lalita and the other sakhis, in fitting bliss,
dance. I shall make known a little of the varieties [of dance].

**3179.** *Now [it is told] of dance:*
Nartana, in order, is natya, nritya and nritta — three [kinds];
these three are born of the Vedas — so the knowers of dance define.[^786]

**3180.** *Nartana is threefold: natya, nritya and nritta, in order.*

**3181.** *Of these, natya is thus:*
The various states of people's nature in all their kinds,
joined with abhinaya of the body — that is natya.[^787]

**3182.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*The nature of people, consisting of manifold states,*
*joined with abhinaya of the body, is called natya by the wise.*

**3183.** *And further:*
*That which is in drama is by nature abhinaya of the meaning of sentences and the meaning of words;*
*of these the first, endowed with rasa and bhava, was set forth by Bharata;*
*and surely the lords of sages employed it in dramas and the like.*[^788]

**3184.** *Now, nritya:*
That which is accepted by the custom of a country and rests on tala and the rest —
movement of the limbs with vilasa — is known as nritya.

**3185.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*That which is accepted by the custom of a country and rests on tala, mana and laya —*
*movement of the limbs with vilasa — the wise call nritya.*

**3186.** *Vilasa is thus:*
*A particularity in actions — in looking at the beloved (nayaka) and the like —*
*accompanied by amorous movements, is called vilasa.*

**3187.** *[It is told] of nritta:*
The mark of what is called nritta is that it is devoid of all abhinaya;
it is known as mere movement of the limbs and the like.

**3188.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*Mere movement of the body, devoid of all abhinaya,*
*[performed] in the manner set forth for angika, the knowers of dance call nritta.*

**3189.** Natya, nritya and nritta — all three are of two kinds:
the distinction of marga and deshi — this is well known in the shastras.

**3190.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*These three are declared twofold — by the distinction of marga and deshi.*

**3191.** *Of these, [it is told] of marga:*
*What was sought by Brahma and the others from Shambhu and employed by Bharata and the others —*
*song, instrumental music and dance — that is called marga.*
*(Margitam — that is, "sought.")*

**3192.** *[It is told] of deshi:*
*The song, instrumental music and also dance that in various countries*
*supremely delight kings and others the wise call deshi.*[^789]

**3193.** Twenty [kinds] of marga natya are defined by Kohala:
nataka, prakarana, bhana, prahasana and the rest.[^790]

**3194.** Some say marga natya is of ten kinds;
natika, prakaranika and the rest are [also] known.

**3195.** Dattila and others name sixteen [kinds] of deshi natya:
sattaka, trotaka, goshthi, vrindaka and the rest.[^791]

**3196.** Such are the manifold parts of natya, pleasing to the mind;
here I have only pointed the way — [they] are well known in the shastras.

**3197.** In nritya and nritta there are two [varieties]: tandava and lasya —
so say the knowers of dance; from them comes every happiness.

**3198.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*This pair is said to be twofold: tandava and lasya.*
*(The pair — that is, nritya and nritta.)*

**3199.** Tandava is a dance for the most part vigorous and the like;
for men and for women there are, respectively, tandava and lasya.

**3200.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*The performance, mostly vigorous, set forth by Tandu, they call tandava.*
*(Tandu is a particular [attendant] among the ganas of Shambhu.)*[^792]

**3201.** *In the Narada-samhita:*
*The dance of men is called tandava, the dance of women is called lasya.*

**3202.** Tandava is of two kinds: prerani tandava,
and also bahurupa tandava — this is commonly known.

**3203.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*Prerani and bahurupa — thus tandava is of two kinds.*

**3204.** *Of these, prerani is thus:*
*Where there is abundance of bodily movement and also absence of abhinaya,*
*that is called prerani; among people its name is deshi.*

**3205.** *Bahurupa is thus — in Damodara:*
*Where there is cutting and piercing, a manifold array of faces,*
*that is bahurupa tandava, vigorous, connected with speech.*[^793]

**3206.** Prerani and bahurupa are described in detail elsewhere.
Lasya, which increases [the power of] Kandarpa, is well known in the shastras.

**3207.** *[It is told] of lasya:*
The lasya dance is of two kinds: sphurita lasya,
and also yauvata lasya — these two are known everywhere.

**3208.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*Lasya, with tender movements of the body, increases [the power of] Makaradhvaja;*
*and it too is held to be twofold: sphurita and yauvata.*[^794]

**3209.** *[It is told] of sphurita lasya:*
*Where in the first [rasa], in abhinaya, with bhavas, rasas, embraces and kisses,*
*the nayika and the nayaka dance — that indeed is sphurita.*
*("In the first" — in the principal rasa; "with rasas" — with bhavas and movements that generate rasa; ashlesha — that is, embrace.)*[^795]

**3210.** *[It is told] of yauvata lasya:*
*Where actresses dance with sweet, well-knit pastimes,*
*resembling the science of enchantment — that lasya is held to be yauvata.*

**3211.** *Now [it is told] of nritta:*
Of nritta I shall give only the names: in it are three varieties —
vishama, vikata, laghu; the shastras set [them] out in detail.

**3212.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*Nritta too is declared threefold: vishama, vikata, laghu.*
*Vishama is said to be that in which there is whirling on a rope and the like;*

**3213.** *vikata is held to be that in which dress, limbs and actions are grotesque;*
*laghu is known as that endowed with few karanas — anchita and the rest.*
*(Anchita and the rest are particular karanas; they will be described. The particular kinds of nritya named by Kohala — dombika, bhanika and the rest — have already been mentioned.)*[^796]

**3214.** O Srinivasa! The ways of dance are manifold;
who has the power to tell of them completely?

**3215.** In the Sri rasa-mandala Krishna, the crown of rasikas,
reveals a dance beyond the reach of Shiva and Brahma.

**3216.** Who can keep composure at Krishna's wondrous dance?
Rai together with the sakhis floats in an ocean of happiness.

**3217.** In dancing with one another great merriment grows;
a supreme wonder is that abhinaya of the body.

**3218.** *Now, anga abhinaya:*
Abhinaya of the body is threefold: anga, upanga, and also
pratyanga; these three have many varieties.[^797]

**3219.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*Here, according to our understanding, a description of the angas, upangas*
*and pratyangas is given, in agreement with Sharngadeva and others.*[^798]

**3220.** The angas of abhinaya are the head, the shoulders, I say further,
the chest, the sides, the hands, the waist, the feet — so it is known.

**3221.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*The seven angas: head, shoulders, chest, sides, hands, waist, feet.*

**3222.** The pratyangas, know, are of nine beautiful kinds:
the neck, the arms, the shoulders, the wrists, the back, the belly,

**3223.** the thighs, and also the shanks, the knees, the ornaments — these are the nine;
the abhinaya of the pratyangas is defined by the knowers of dance.[^799]

**3224.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*The pratyangas are nine: neck, arms, shoulders, wrists,*
*back, belly, thighs, shanks, knees and ornaments.*

**3225.** The upangas are twelve; their abhinaya has its own kinds:
the crown, the gaze, the pupils, the brows, the face and the rest are known.[^800]

**3226.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*The twelve upangas: the crown, the gaze, the pupils, the brows, the face,*
*the two nostrils, the breath and the chin, the tongue, the cheeks, the teeth, the lips;*

**3227.** *and the color of the face Sharngadeva included among the upangas.*[^801]

**3228.** Some say: the angas are six, the pratyangas ten,
and the abhinaya of the upangas is of twenty-three kinds.

**3229.** All this is extensive; the chief in this are the angas;
something [of them] I shall make known, so as to attract all hearts.

**3230.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*Since among them the angas are chief, they are told of in brief.*

**3231.** *Of them, first [it is told] of the head:*
The actions of the head — dhuta, vidhuta, adhuta, and also
avadhuta and the rest — are of fourteen kinds.[^802]

**3232.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*Dhuta, vidhuta, adhuta, avadhuta and kampita,*
*akampita and udvahita, parivahita, anchita,*

**3233.** *nikunchita, paravritta, utkshipta and adhomukha,*
*and lolita — thus it should be known: the head is of fourteen kinds.*
*(Akampita means slightly trembling.)*

**3234.** *Of them, dhuta:*
A slow, slightly slanting shaking of the head is dhuta;
dhuta is prescribed for sorrow, wonder and the like.

**3235.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*The head slowly shaken sideways is called dhuta;*
*it occurs in refusal, in what is unwanted, in sorrow and in wonder.*

**3236.** Know the characteristics of vidhuta and the rest in the same way.
Likewise clear and well accepted is the abhinaya of the shoulders.

**3237.** *Now, the shoulders:*
The shoulders are of five [kinds]: ekochcha, lagna-karna, and also
uchchhrita, srasta, lolita — such are the known characteristics.

**3238.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*Ekochcha, lagna-karna, uchchhrita, srasta and lolita —*
*so are named the five kinds of shoulders, whose characteristics are clear from the name itself.*

**3239.** The abhinaya ekochcha is in blows with the fist and the spear;
likewise in the shastras [are described] the characteristics of karna-lagna and the rest.

**3240.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*The shoulders are called ekochcha in blows with the fist and the spear;*
*in embracing and in cold the shoulders are karna-lagna, in the opinion of the virtuous;*

**3241.** *uchchhrita in joy, pride and the like; srasta in grief, weariness, intoxication;*
*in fainting, and also in the dance of the vita, lolita should be done;*
*the knowers of dance name [it] in laughter and in playing the hudduka.*[^803]
*(Vita-nartana means the dance of a paramour.)*
*Thus the shoulders are of five kinds.*

**3242.** *Now, the chest:*
The abhinaya of the chest is of five [kinds]: sama, abhugna, and also
nirbhugna, kampita, udvahita — so it is known.

**3243.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*The chest is sama, abhugna, nirbhugna, prakampita*
*and udvahita — of five kinds; their characteristics we set forth.*

**3244.** *Of them, sama:*
Know: the abhinaya sama is [when] the chest is in saushthava and the like;
the characteristics of abhugna and the rest the knowers of the shastras define.[^804]

**3245.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*The chest established in saushthava, resting on the body in the chaturasra position,*
*remaining in its natural state, they call sama; sama is in the abhinaya of the natural state.*
*And so on.*

**3246.** *Now, the sides:*
The sides: vivartita, apasrita, prasarita,
nata, unnata — these five characteristics are known.

**3247.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*Vivartita, apasrita, prasarita, then nata*
*and unnata — so the wise have described the sides as of five kinds.*
*From the turning of the trika in turning round, there is vivartita.*
*And so on.*
*(Of the trika means of the back; "the trika is the lower part of the spine.")*

**3248.** *Now, the hands:*
The abhinaya of the hands is threefold: that called samyuta, and also
asamyuta and nritya-hasta — these three are known.

**3249.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*Asamyuta, samyuta and nritya-hastas — thus threefold*
*the hastakas are described by the knowers, by the distinction of ordinary and dance [gestures].*[^805]

**3250.** The action of abhinaya with one hand is asamyuta;
the action that [is done] with both hands is samyuta.

**3251.** [Gestures] that remain only in dance and express nothing,
with anga-hava, are called nritya-hasta.[^806]

**3252.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*Those whose actions [are done] with one hand are asamyuta;*
*those whose action is with both hands are samyuta.*

**3253.** *But those that remain only in dance and denote nothing,*
*accompanied by anga-hava, are held to be nritya-hastas.*

**3254.** The movement of the hands, the knowers of dance say, is threefold:
uttana, parshvaga, adhomukha — these three.

**3255.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*Uttana, parshvaga, and also adhomukha —*
*the movement of the hands of three kinds has been proclaimed by Bharata.*[^807]

**3256.** Some say fifteen; this too I accept.
In order, according to the teaching, I shall make known the asamyuta.

**3257.** *The asamyuta is told of:*
The asamyuta hastakas — pataka, and also
tripataka and the rest — are of twenty-four kinds.

**3258.** Some say there are four more than these;
some say thirty; this is well accepted.

**3259.** The asamyuta, by force of meaning, are accepted [also] as samyuta;
all this the learned define in detail.

**3260.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*Pataka, tripataka, [the gesture] named ardhachandra, kartarimukha,*
*arala-mushti, shikhara, kapittha, khatakamukha,*

**3261.** *shukatunda, kangula, padmakosha, then pallava,*
*suchimukha, sarpashiras, chatura, mrigashirshaka,*

**3262.** *hamsasya, hamsapaksha, bhramara, and also mukula,*
*urnanabha, samdamsha, tamrachuda, and one more — kavi:*
*these asamyuta hastas, it is said, are twenty-four.*[^808]

**3263.** *Upadhana, simhamukha, kadamba and nikunjaka —*
*these four besides, among the asamyuta, others have named.*

**3264.** *By Damodara these asamyuta hastas are stated as thirty;*
*and the asamyuta, by force of meaning, are also samyuta.*

**3265.** What the characteristics of all these hastakas are,
and in what matters they are applied — this is set forth in the shastras.

**3266.** The characteristics of the hastakas are very extensive;
here I show [only] the direction — how the abhinaya is.

**3267.** *Pataka is told of:*
The thumb bent and resting at the base of the forefinger,
and all the other fingers straight — so pataka is known.

**3268.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*That whose thumb, being bent, rests at the base of the forefinger,*
*and whose [other] fingers are straight [and] joined, is called pataka.*

**3269.** Abhinaya with pataka is in touching and in many [other] cases;
the knowers of dance explain it in various ways.

**3270.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*It [is used] in touching, in peta, in [showing] flags, clapping and the like;*
*in [showing] flames its fingers, raised upward, are spread apart and moving;*[^809]

**3271.** *in [showing] streams they are lowered; in [showing] the wings of birds it is at the hips;*
*in [showing] what is high it goes upward, but in pushkara and in seizing, downward;*

**3272.** *in the abhinaya of tossing up the hand goes upward from the region of the hips.*
*("From the region of the hips" means from the place of the hips.)*

**3273.** *In [expressing] facing [someone, the hand] comes from one's own side to the region of the face;*
*trembling at the side in refusal; in dividing, apart at the sides.*

**3274.** *And pataka [is used] slowly in rubbing, kneading, and also in cleaning;*
*in holding, pulling out and the like of large things — stones and so on —*
*these two [hands], turned toward each other, should be raised and lowered.*
*(Uchchhrita means raised upward.)*

**3275.** *[The hand] with palm and fingers raised, moving downward, in [showing] the force of wind and waves;*
*in pointing to a lake and a pond, having crossed as a svastika, they part.*
*(Saras, palvala means a small pond.)*

**3276.** *The two patakas, having taken the form of a svastika, should be separated*
*in cutting, concealing, [showing] a mirror, speaking and wiping.*
*(Pronchhana — in the [common] speech, ponchhana.)*

**3277.** *Making the hands with palms downward and upward, slightly extended,*
*one should show the shore, a hole, a crocodile, a house, a cave.*

**3278.** *Although the uses of the hands have been told without distinction, still [they] are to be applied only following the usage of people.*

**3279.** *About this it is said:*
*Observing the practice of people and relying on natya,*
*one should apply the hastakas according to this or that movement;*
*in rubbing, cutting, [showing] a mirror, dividing and the like this is indeed clear.*
*Thus is pataka.*

**3280.** So the knowers of dance define tripataka and the rest;
the wonder that is in this — could anyone else grasp it?

**3281.** *Thus are the asamyuta hastas. Now [it is told] of the samyuta, [coming next] in order:*

**3282.** The samyuta hastakas are defined as thirteen:
anjali, kapota, karkata, svastika and the rest.

**3283.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*Anjali and kapota, karkata, and also svastika,*
*dola, pushpaputa, utsanga, khataka-vardhamanaka,*

**3284.** *gajadanta and avahittha, nishadha, and also makara*
*and vardhamana — these samyuta hastas are thirteen.*[^810]

**3285.** *Here, anjali:*
Two patakas joined with the palms of both hands are anjali;
anjali is used in obeisance to the gods and the like.

**3286.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*If two patakas are joined at the palms, then it is anjali;*
*in obeisance to the gods it is said to be [held] above the head;*

**3287.** *but in obeisance to the gurus it should be at the face;*
*and in obeisance to brahmanas, the virtuous hold, at the heart;*

**3288.** *in others there is no rule, know this; [it] may be done in the three [ways] as one wishes.*
*Thus is anjali.*

**3289.** The characteristics of kapota and the other samyuta [hastakas] are many;
the knowers of dance expound [them] in various ways.

**3290.** *Now, the nritya-hastas:*
The nritya-hastas are suited only to dance;
they are of thirty kinds; some say thirty-two.

**3291.** Chaturasra, udvritta and the rest — thirty kinds;
the characteristics and so on of them all are well set forth in the shastras.

**3292.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*Two chaturasras, then two udvrittas, the hands named tenamukha, and so on.*[^811]

**3293.** The hastakas are endless; the learned [only] pointed out the direction,
and the other hastakas that [there are] they expounded by kinds.

**3294.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*These hastakas have been stated by me only to point out the direction;*
*because of the endlessness of what is portrayed, there are endless other [positions of the] hands.*
*Thus are the hands.*

**3295.** *Now [it is told] of the hips:*
The abhinaya of the hips is of five [kinds]: kampita, udvahita,
chhinna, vivrita, rechita — [such are] the known characteristics.

**3296.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*Kampita, udvahita, chhinna, vivrita, and also rechita —*
*so the hips are said to be of five kinds.*

**3297.** *Now, the feet:*
The feet: sama, anchita, kunchita, suchi and the rest —
thirteen kinds the knowers of dance define.

**3298.** *Thus [it is said]:*
*Sama, anchita, kunchita and suchi-agratala-sanchara,*
*mardita and udghatita, and also agraga, parshvaga and parshniga,*[^812]

**3299.** *tadita, udghattita, uchchhedha, udghatita — so in order;*
*the feet are said by the knowers of dance to be of thirteen kinds.*[^813]

**3300.** *When both feet stand naturally, [the foot] is called sama.*
*Thus are the seven angas.*

**3301.** What the abhinaya of the pratyangas and upangas is like —
this the knowers of dance have set forth in detail.

**3302.** And whatever other acts of natya they introduced in this [teaching] —
all of that I cannot set forth in detail.

**3303.** O Srinivasa! In the rasa the son of the king of Vraja
reveals what is hard to know [even] for Brahma and the others.

**3304.** There is no comparison for [His] abhinaya of the body;
[He] reveals various bhavas in endless poses.

**3305.** Sri Radhika and all Radhika's sakhis
reveal [such] poses — who could tell of [them], and how much?

**3306.** The supreme, wondrous beauty cannot be told;
amid the sakhis Rai and Kanu sport.

**3307.** What can be said of the captivating sweetness of the two of Them!
The best of the wise describe [it] in various ways.

**3308.** *Thus [it is said] in a song — of Sri Krishna, in a suitable raga:*[^814]
Shyama-raya, who delights in the rasa —
at [His] poses the world swoons.

**3309.** Crushed collyrium, a dense mass of clouds —
these the luster of [His] most tender body surpasses.

**3310.** On [His] head shines the eyed peacock feather;
[it] enchants the minds of the young women.

**3311.** A wondrous tilaka on [His] lovely forehead;
whom do the curling locks not bewitch?

**3312.** The two brows are Kama's bow;
in the corners of the eyes, arrows are aimed.

**3313.** Restless earrings at the ears;
a pearl swings at the nostril.

**3314.** The moon of [His] face, in all four directions,
rains nectar with a mere trace of a smile.

**3315.** The sweetness of [His] broad chest
steals the wealth of composure.

**3316.** On [His] neck the forest garland sports;
seeing [it], can a frail woman hold her heart?

**3317.** The curve of [His] arms steals life;
with [His] slender waist [He] maddens the world.

**3318.** [His] garments and ornaments are finely arrayed;
[His] thighs put to shame an upturned plantain trunk.[^815]

**3319.** Ankle bells ring on [His] reddish feet;
Narahari offers himself for Him.

**3320.** *[A song] in a suitable raga. Now, of Sri Radhika:*
Rai, who delights in the rasa, in the rasa
sports amid the sakhis at Shyama's left side.

**3321.** Ah, I die! What radiance of beauty!
It lights up the world, surpassing the whole array of comparisons.

**3322.** With [Her] face [She] destroys the moon's conceit;
with [Her] sweetest smile [She] steals the pride of nectar.

**3323.** [Her] two brows are a row of bees;
in the corners of [Her] lotus eyes — play of many kinds.

**3324.** In [Her] nose a lovely pendant is set;
how fresh the dot of sindura in the middle of the forehead!

**3325.** In [Her] ears, charming earrings;
the beauty of [Her] cheeks puts a golden mirror to shame.

**3326.** On [Her] hands bracelets and bangles shine;
[Her] breasts, covered with a bodice, enchant Kanu's mind.

**3327.** [Her] slender waist is girdled with kinkini bells;
the wondrous garment She wears clings to [Her] body.

**3328.** Graceful are [Her] hips and thighs;
did the one who fashioned [them] keep even a trace of composure?

**3329.** Jeweled ankle bells on [Her] feet;
Narahari offers himself for the rays of [Her] lovely nails.

**3330.** Rai and Kanu with the sakhis, in various ways,
fulfil the desire of Sri Vrinda-devi.

**3331.** What joy arises in the Sri rasa-mandala!
The mridanga and many [other] instruments sound in one accord.

**3332.** Shyama-raya, the crest-jewel of rasikas, dances;
with what longing the poet sings the sweetness of that dance!

**3333.** *Thus [it is said] in a song (raga Kedara):*
The Nagara of Vraja dances, ocean of rasa, abode of happiness.
The anklets on [His] feet jingle; in various gaits [He] keeps the tala,
stealing the [whole] weight of composure; [His] abundant poses are incomparable. (Refrain.)

**3334.** The host of fair maidens is seized with delight; hastakas of many kinds,
new abhinaya of the bowed head — the peacock feather tilted to the left.
Lovely are [His] face and lips — [they] drive away the conceit of the moon and the dawn;
[His] kunda-jasmine teeth flash; [His] sweet smile conquers Kama.

**3335.** How many lovely pathas [He] calls out: "Dha dha dhiki dhiki taka tata,
thai thai thai thai di drimiki, drimikata didi drama!
Tatta taka thok thoga, thavi kukudha dhilanga,
dhikata dhidhi kata, dhidhi dhilli lili" — how charming![^816]

**3336.** Delightful is the sound of the ornaments at [His] waist; a flower garland hangs on [His] chest;
the rows of [His] locks sway — fine, charming on [His] forehead.
The jewels of the earrings flash in [His] ears; surpassing restless young khanjana birds
is the glance of [His] lotus eyes; Ghanashyama offers himself [for this].[^817]

**3337.** *Again, raga Kedara:*
Shyama, full of rasa, shines amid the rasa-mandala in beautiful poses;
in lovely attire, playful, supremely skilled in ever new music.

**3338.** Relishfully [He] displays jatis, shrutis, svaras, gramas, murchhanas, tanas;[^818]
"Thodita kata thaita thai thai," [He] says, smiling ever so gently.

**3339.** The lovely moon of [His] face flashes, breaking the weight of Madana's pride;
the glance of [His] restless lotus eyes delights the hearts of the young women.

**3340.** On [His] feet shine the anklets that sound "jhana nana nana";
Narahari offers himself for the sweet dance; the mridanga sounds: "drimi drimi."

**3341.** *Again, raga Bhupali:*
Shyama-raya the rasika dances;
seeing [Him], whose heart does not find solace?

**3342.** In what sweet manner [He] smiles gently!
[He] destroys the composure and dharma of the young women.[^819]

**3343.** The earrings swing at the base of [His] ears;
whom does the radiance of [His] cheeks not bewitch?

**3344.** How much abhinaya [He] performs,
by which Madana is defeated!

**3345.** From the corners of [His] restless long eyes
what rasa [He] pours out — who knows?

**3346.** With [His] lotus feet [He] keeps the tala;
the sound of the ankle bells steals life.

**3347.** "Ta thai ta thai thai thaiya" —
with what grace [He] says it, lingering, lingering!

**3348.** Drimi drimi sounds the madal;
Narahari offers up [his] life [for this].

**3349.** O Srinivasa, Rai's dance is wondrous;
the poets describe [something of it] but find no end [to it].

**3350.** *Thus [it is said] in a song (raga Kedara):*
Radha dances, shattering the weight of composure, with the gait of an elephant.
Auspicious, stainless, soft is the bank of the Kalindi;
blessed, blessed, blessed are the pure, splendid bank [and] night, full of rasa! (Refrain.)

**3351.** Ever so softly sounds the mridanga: "Dhigi dhigi dhigi taga dhilanga,
dha drinda drinda jhendrang drimi, drimi drimi drimi dramini."
Jhunu nunu — the sound of the ankle bells on [Her] feet; the kinkini bells at [Her] waist — jhini nini nini;
the bangles on [Her] hands tinkle: jhanana, jhanana — [She is] exceedingly charming.

**3352.** The lotus of [Her] face in bloom, [Her] garment, the rows of teeth, the graceful laughter;[^820]
"Taka thai thai, thai taka," [She] utters, the abode of happiness.
Most lovely is the host of jeweled ornaments; [She] sways [Her] neck in dense delight;
restless are the corners of [Her] eyes [and Her] brows; the host of [Her] locks adorns [Her].

**3353.** Stealing the pride of gold, with a body of supreme sweetness,
veiled by the fluttering end of [Her] garment, the Incomparable One shines.
[She] makes hastakas of many kinds; beauty, a mass of rasa, pours [from Her];
Narahari, beholding, offers himself again and again [for Her]; the celestial women are put to shame.

**3354.** *Again, raga Karnata:*
Radha, who delights in the rasa, dances;
the mridanga sounds: "dhik dhik dha dha."

**3355.** The rays of [Her] body flash, stealing the mind;
the smile of [Her] moon-face — as if nectar were pouring.

**3356.** [She] calls out: "Thai thai dhiki taka dhenna,
ai ati ai ati oia tenna."

**3357.** The movement of [Her] lotus eyes crushes the khanjana birds;
[Her] hands, performing abhinaya, are adorned with bracelets.

**3358.** [Her] slender waist is girdled with jingling kinkini bells;
the garment She wears is light [and] clings to [Her] body.

**3359.** Jhanana — the jeweled ankle bells flash on [Her] feet;
Narahari offers himself for the grace with which [She] steps.

**3360.** *Again, raga Kamoda:*
Rai dances, the jewel among women;
on [Her] feet the ankle bells ring, at [Her] waist the kinkini.

**3361.** [Her] braid, surpassing a serpent, swings on [Her] back;
what a curve of the neck — in waves of rasa!

**3362.** What sweet abhinaya [She] performs!
Saying "tathaia tathaia thaiya," [She] keeps the tala.

**3363.** Destroying with [Her] face the moon's conceit,
with [Her] smile [She] rains nectar — heaps upon heaps!

**3364.** Abhinaya of the eyes — in how many ways!
[She] intoxicates King Madana, the Moon of Vraja.

**3365.** What comparison can Narahari give?
The beauty of [Her] body lights up the world.

**3366.** O Srinivasa, with what delight Rai and Kanu
perform a wondrous dance together with Lalita and the others!

**3367.** *Thus [it is said] in a song (raga Kedara):*
Today the play of the rasa is supreme; Shyama shines, full of the highest rasa;
Radhika's lotus hand is with the Upholder of the mountain, who delights [all] with [His] steps.[^821]
With smiling face [He] calls out lovely pathas: "Thai tathai thai tathai tatatho, didi digan" —
the abhinaya of [His] hands breaks the weight of Madana's pride.

**3368.** The jewel of women sees the face of her beloved, her very life, and deep joy grows in [her] heart;
[her] whole body thrills abundantly, [her] soles keep the tala in restless movement.
It sounds: "Drimi drimiki drimidha, thai tathai tata thai tathai tha,
thung nung nung"; from the corners of [her] restless eyes a wealth of rasa rains down.

**3369.** Beholding the beauty of the Couple, delighted, offering themselves for the incomparable cloud and lightning,
Lalita and her friends, absorbed in the dance, gently, gently sway their necks in lovely poses.
In sweet voices, in many ways, [they] utter: "Thai tathai thai drimiki drimi tatho,
dig dig dig dig thai tathai" — with surpassing skill, in fine music.

**3370.** Beautifully attired, Vishakhika dances on [her] side, again and again crying: "Tadhik dhigiti,
dhigiti dhigi dhigi dhik dhaikata, dha dhi ni ni ni nini dhinnina."
"Drimiki drimi drimi" — the sound of the mardala steals composure; Ghanashyama says unceasingly:
"Tiai aiti aia, aiati aia tinnina."

**3371.** *Again, raga Kedara:*
What a fresh full-moon night it is today!
The bank of the Yamuna flashes in the rasa; the moon lights up the quarters.

**3372.** Rai and Kanu — in what a sweet manner
the two of Them dance, leaning against each other, resting [Their] arms on [each other's] shoulders!

**3373.** Every moment, what delight in [Their] hearts!
Both play the flute, bringing [Their] faces together — what comparison could be given for that?

**3374.** Eyes in lovely eyes,
lips touch lips, [tasting] rasa; in rapture the heart rejoices.

**3375.** With both hands, the left and the right,
They display so many kinds of abhinaya, stealing Madana's composure.

**3376.** "Ta ta tathai tathai," They say;
ceaseless is that sound — what a stream of nectar flows from the moon of [Their] faces!

**3377.** Drimi drimiki — the mridanga sounds;
on the ground the feet keep the tala — how finely the new ankle bells adorn [them]!

**3378.** Lalita and the others, seeing that beauty,
sing in various ways with dancing gestures — [a beauty] that entices Narahari's mind.

**3379.** O Srinivasa, the pastimes of the rasa dance the poets
describe in detail — in them is endless bliss.

**3380.** Hearing all this destroys many misfortunes;
the lotus feet of Radha and Krishna are attained without effort.

**3381.** Krishna, the Enjoyer of the Sri rasa dance, the Enchanter of the worlds,
sports in the water of the Yamuna for a long while.

**3382.** Who can describe the merriment in that?
In this kunja dwelling [They] array Themselves in wondrous attire.

**3383.** The Two lie down here in great joy;
at the end of night the sakhis wake [Them].

**3384.** The Two, with the sakhis, go each to Their own home;
in separation from each other, the Two cannot keep hold of life.

**3385.** The sakhis console the Two in many ways;
the Two, lying down each in Their own home, meet in dreams.

**3386.** *Thus [it is said] in a song:*
Rai and Shyama-raya with the sakhis —
abundant play in the rasa dance, jubilation in the heart.

**3387.** To sport in the water,
entering the waters of the Yamuna, [They] make so many graceful movements.

**3388.** [They] shower water on each other;
the garments get wet, clinging to the bodies, full of beauty.

**3389.** Abashed, the lovely girl, glancing toward Shyama,
hides in the deep water, in a forest of lotuses.

**3390.** That Dark One, beside Himself with prema,
kisses a lotus, mistaking [it] for Rai's face.

**3391.** Lalita and the other sakhis all around,
seeing Shyama's antics, laugh ever so softly.

**3392.** Rai, receiving a sign from the sakhis,
stands leaning Her body against Shyama's body.

**3393.** Moment by moment the merriment grows;
having sported in the water, [They] come up onto the bank of the Yamuna.

**3394.** Intoxicated by Madana, They drink honey;
in the joy of the battle of love the heart overflows.

**3395.** At the end of night, from the nikunja,
[They] go with wary steps by unseen paths.

**3396.** The Two, having gone each to Their own home,
lie down, distraught with the pain of separation.

**3397.** In dreams [They] meet, with joy in the heart;
Narahari offers himself up for this love of the Two.

**3398.** Coming again, [They] sport in this kunja dwelling;
the poets describe this in order, in various ways.

**3399.** *Thus [it is said] in the Govinda-lilamrita, in the first canto, the fourth verse:*[^822]
*He who at the end of night goes from the kunja into the cowherd village, in the morning and in the evening performs the pastimes of milking, eating and the like,*
*in the sangava, grazing the cows, sports with His friends,*
*at midday and then at night verily enjoys with Radha in the forest, in the afternoon*
*goes to the village, and at pradosha delights His dear ones — may that Krishna protect us.*

**3400.** *Thus [it is said] in a song:*
At the end of night, lying in a new kunja,
the ornaments of Vraja, Shyama and Gori, in fresh love,
waking playfully, in fear of the stern elders,
go very swiftly and lie down again at home.

**3401.** In the morning, busy with bathing and so on, the lovely girl,
