[^115]: Bahulavana is the fifth of the twelve forests of Vraja (cf. 464), southwest of Mathura.
[^116]: Sankarshana-kunda and Mana-sarasi (in the verse at 466, Manasa-saras) are ponds of Bahulavana.
[^117]: Madhu is the month of Chaitra (March–April).
[^118]: Dakshina-grama — "the southern village." A play on words in 473: dakshina ("southern," "right," "compliant") — in the classification of heroines (nayika-bheda), the heroine who cannot hold to her sulking (mana) for long and readily yields to her beloved, as opposed to the "contrary" one (vama).
[^119]: The sense of the line is unclear: probably that [the domain] of Nanda extended as far as Shashthikara and Raval. Raval is a village on the Yamuna, by tradition the birthplace of Radha. "Ral" follows the 1912/13 ed. (rāla); the 1960 ed. is corrupt here.
[^120]: Tosha-sthana — apparently the village of Tosh; tosha means "satisfaction" (a play on words with "in great satisfaction").
[^121]: Arita (from Arishta, cf. 478, 509) is a village near Govardhana where Radha-kunda and Shyama-kunda are located (the present-day village of Radhakund).
[^122]: Arishta is the asura in the form of a bull slain by Krishna (*Bhag.* 10.36).
[^123]: The night bath in Radha-kunda is taken on the Bahula-ashtami, the eighth lunar day of the dark fortnight of the month of Kartika (cf. 504).
[^124]: The *Stavavali* is a collection of prayers by Raghunatha Dasa Gosvami; the *Vraja-vilasa-stava* is one of them.
[^125]: In the 1960 ed., here and in 496, "kundas" (kuṇḍa) instead of "kunjas" (kuñja); cf. 497 and 498, where both editions have kunjas.
[^126]: So in A and C (by the scans): «সারস লস হংস লাখ» (sārasa lasa haṃsa lākh); lasa is understood as "sport, show off their beauty" (lasat). The 1960 edition reads mārasa-lana-haṃsa.
[^127]: The festival of lamps (Dipotsava, Dipavali) falls on the new moon of the month of Kartika.
[^128]: Prabodhini is the ekadashi of the bright fortnight of Kartika, when Vishnu awakens from His sleep (Utthana Ekadashi).
[^129]: On Mahaprabhu's discovery of Radha-kunda and Shyama-kunda in the rice fields of Arita, see CC Madhya 18.
[^130]: Kali ("the dark one") and Gauri ("the fair one") — apparently an allusion to Shyama-kunda and Radha-kunda. On the Lord's discovery of the kundas in two rice fields at Arit-grama see CC Madhya 18.
[^131]: Badarikashrama (Badrinath) is a place of pilgrimage in the Himalayas; "the Lord" and "Narayana" (538) here are the Deity of Badri-Narayana.
[^132]: Yudhishthira is the eldest of the five Pandava brothers, the heroes of the *Mahabharata*. Cf. 5.600: at the Manasa-pavana-ghat the Pandavas abide in the form of trees.
[^133]: The Manasa-pavana-ghat is a ghat on the northern bank of Shyama-kunda, by Subala's kunja (5.599), dear to Radha (5.600).
[^134]: Chaurasa — "square," with even sides. So in the 1888 and 1960 editions (নহিল "did not become"); the 1912/13 edition (by the scan of A) reads রহিল "remained," apparently a misprint (cf. "শ্যামকুণ্ড বক্র" in 547: the kunda is crooked).
[^135]: Sakhisthali is a village near Govardhana, the place of Chandravali, Radha's rival (see 572). The palasha is a tree with large leaves (Butea monosperma).
[^136]: A sadhaka is a practicing devotee; a siddha is a perfected one. The sense: though Raghunatha abides in the body of a sadhaka, his acts are those of an eternal associate devoted to Radha.
[^137]: "In his mind" (মানসে): in mental service (manasi-seva).
[^138]: Sri Vallabha is Vallabha Bhatta (Vallabhacharya), the founder of the Pushti-marga; his son Vitthalanatha served the Deity of Sri Nathaji at Govardhana. Not to be confused with Vallabha (Anupama), the brother of Rupa and Sanatana.
[^139]: Sri Kunda is Radha-kunda. The stone from Govardhana (shila) and the necklace of gunja berries were given to Raghunatha Dasa by Mahaprabhu (CC Antya 6).
[^140]: Malyahari (Malyaharini)-kunda is a pond near Radha-kunda. The pastime with the pearls is described in Raghunatha Dasa's *Mukta-charita* (586).
[^141]: Shivakhora and Bhanukhora are ponds near Radha-kunda.
[^142]: So in the 1888 and 1912/13 editions (শুষ্ক "withered"; checked against the scans); the 1960 edition reads শুদ্ধ, "pure."
[^143]: The subject is not named in the original (তেঁহো "he"); probably Srinivasa bowed to Krishnadasa Kaviraja as was fitting.
[^144]: The Pandavas are the five princely brothers, heroes of the *Mahabharata*. The Manasa-pavana ghat is at Shyama-kunda (see 599).
[^145]: Sri Kunda is Radha-kunda. Mukharai is a village named after Mukhara (605).
[^146]: Kusuma-sarovara ("the lake of flowers") is a pond at Govardhana, not far from Radha-kunda; here Radha and her friends gathered flowers and met Krishna.
[^147]: Narada wished to see Krishna's pastimes with the gopis; Vrinda (Vrinda-devi) is the goddess of Vrindavana, the arranger of Radha and Krishna's meetings.
[^148]: Shankhachuda was a servant of Kuvera who tried to abduct the gopis; Krishna slew him and gave his jewel to Balarama (*Bhag.* 10.34).
[^149]: Palika is one of the yutheshvaris, the leaders of bands of gopis.
[^150]: "yamunā ata grāmete" in all copies (G, A, B, C; checked against the scan of A); taken as the name of a village (Yamuna-ata; possibly Yamunavata). It could also be understood as "far away is the Yamuna, in the village of Ata."
[^151]: Indradhvaja-vedi is "the altar of Indra's banner." Krishna persuaded Nanda to replace the yearly worship of Indra with the worship of Govardhana (*Bhag.* 10.24; cf. 634).
[^152]: Rina-mochana means "releasing from debts," Papa-mochana "releasing from sins."
[^153]: Sankarshana is Balarama.
[^154]: Parasauli is a village near Govardhana, the place of the spring rasa; Chandra-sarovara (620) is there.
[^155]: The *Stavavali* is a collection of hymns by Raghunatha Dasa Gosvami; *Govardhanashraya-dashaka* means "Ten [Verses] on Taking Shelter of Govardhana."
[^156]: Rai is an affectionate name of Radha.
[^157]: Paiṭhe means "enters" (as the 1912/13 edition also explains); hence the name Paitha.
[^158]: Gauri-tirtha is a tirtha at Govardhana named after Gauri (Parvati).
[^159]: The nipa is the kadamba tree.
[^160]: Cf. *Bhag.* 10.24: Krishna assumed a huge form, saying, "I am the hill," and ate the cowherds' offerings.
[^161]: Aniyor comes from the Braj "āniyo aur," "bring more."
[^162]: The Annakuta ("mountain of food") is the offering to Govardhana of a mountain of foodstuffs; this is the place of that offering.
[^163]: The *Vraja-vilasa* (*Vraja-vilasa-stava*) is a hymn in Raghunatha Dasa Gosvami's *Stavavali*. The Enemy of Agha (Aghari) is Krishna, who slew the demon Aghasura; the best of the lords of Vraja is Nanda. The author does not explain the words "tricks Radha with a boon" (vareṇa rādhāṃ chalayan).
[^164]: After Krishna lifted Govardhana, Indra and the celestial cow Surabhi performed His abhisheka (ceremonial bathing), and He received the name Govinda (*Bhag.* 10.27).
[^165]: Shakra is Indra. Tarpana is the offering of water to the gods and ancestors.
[^166]: Dana-nivartana means "the ending of the [play of the] toll" (the dana-lila; see 661, 668).
[^167]: The Deity of Gopala, hidden in the forest at Govardhana, appeared to Madhavendra Puri bringing him milk and was then installed by him on the hill (CC Madhya 4).
[^168]: Ganthuli is a village near Govardhana; according to CC Madhya 18, Gopala, fearing raids, would move there, and there Mahaprabhu saw Him.
[^169]: Dhak is the local (Hindi) name of the palasha tree; Shyamadhak means "Shyama's (Krishna's) palasha."
[^170]: The speaker is Raghava Pandita, who lived at Govardhana (see BR 4.392).
[^171]: Airavata is Indra's elephant.
[^172]: Surabhi is the celestial cow who took part in Govinda's abhisheka (641).
[^173]: Kadamkhandi means "a grove of kadambas."
[^174]: Danaghati (Danaghat, 667) is the place of the dana-lila: Krishna, posing as a toll collector, stops the gopis carrying dairy goods to sell.
[^175]: The Sanodiya brahmana of Mathura who accompanied the Lord through Vraja (CC Madhya 17–18).
[^176]: Gandharva (Gandharvika) is Radha. Madana is the god of love; Krishna poses as a toll collector for King Madana.
[^177]: The *Dana-keli-kaumudi* is a one-act play (bhanika) by Rupa Gosvami on the dana-lila.
[^178]: The guardians of the worlds (lokapalas) are the gods who protect the quarters: Indra (east), Yama (south), Varuna (west), Kuvera (north); see 672–673.
[^179]: Manasa-jahnavi ("the Ganga of Manasa") is Manasi-ganga. Gandharvika is Radha; the Crusher of Mura is Krishna. "At the foot of the serpent": *bhogi-mūle*, so in A, B and C (checked against the scan of A); "serpent" (*bhogī*) follows the 1960 edition's gloss; the word also means "enjoyer."
[^180]: The Yadavas here are the cowherds of Vraja: Nanda's clan is counted as a branch of the line of Yadu.
[^181]: Balai (Balaichand) is an affectionate name of Balarama; Nityananda is Balarama Himself.
[^182]: The 1960 edition has "ascetic" (*yati*) instead of "form" (*mūrti*).
[^183]: Not in the 1960 edition.
[^184]: Cf. CB Antya 5–6: later, in Bengal, Nityananda wore rich ornaments.
[^185]: The Chakra-tirtha at Govardhana is not the one in Mathura (5.303); Sanatana Gosvami lived here (728–740).
[^186]: As glossed in the 1960 edition: Sankarshana-kunda (the Plough-bearer is Balarama), Brahma-kunda, Kadamkhandi, Kusuma-sarovara (*sumanas*, "flower"), Rudra-kunda, Apsara-kunda, Gauri-tirtha, Chandra-sarovara (*jyotsnā*, "moonlight"), Rina-mochana and Papa-mochana (*mokṣaṇa*, "release"), Malyahari-kunda, Arishta-kunda (the Enemy of the gods is the asura Arishta), Indradhvaja-vedi; the divine hill is Govardhana, and the Ratna-pitha is the Ratna-simhasana.
[^187]: So in the text: the command comes from Chakra-tirtha itself; it can also be understood as "[there was] a command to live here, at Chakra-tirtha."
[^188]: According to tradition, this Govardhana stone bearing Krishna's footprint is kept in Vrindavana, in the Radha-Damodara temple.
[^189]: Manasa-jahnavi is Manasi-ganga (see 675). The surety (*pratibhū*): Govardhana as the witness and guarantor of the pastimes of the young couple, Radha and Krishna.
[^190]: The name apparently comes from Hindi "made [Him] swear" (*saũh karāī*). A reads «সোঁকরাই» (sõkarāi) in the scan; the 1960 edition "saukarāi"; the line is missing in C.
[^191]: According to the *Bhagavata-mahatmya* of the Skanda Purana, Uddhava, who dwells as grasses and creepers at Govardhana, appeared to Krishna's queens from Dvaraka, who had come to Vraja with Vajranabha, and told them about Krishna.
[^192]: Govinda-ghat is a ghat on Radha-kunda. The speaker is Raghava Pandita.
[^193]: Rupa and Raghunatha (Raghunatha Dasa Gosvami) were then living at Radha-kunda; Sanatana, at Govardhana.
[^194]: "The hood of a female serpent" is vyālāṅganā-phaṇā (A/B "ফণা"; the 1960 edition has "ফণী," "serpent"). Radha's braid is implied; the author does not quote the verse itself.
[^195]: The author does not name the girls; it is implied that they were Radha and Her friends.
[^196]: The *Lalita-madhava* is a drama by Rupa Gosvami; vipralambha is love in separation (here, the separation of Radha and Krishna).
[^197]: Nirmanchhana is a rite in which something precious is waved over a beloved person (as a sign of readiness to give it up for him) to ward off misfortune. "Coming out from Govardhana" probably means coming out from under the hill that Krishna lifted; compare verse 780, where the gopis perform nirmanchhana even with their sons. The author derives the name Nimgrama from this rite.
[^198]: The patala (pāṭala) is a tree with fragrant pink flowers (Stereospermum).
[^199]: Dera means "camp, halting place." Nanda and all the cowherds were moving from Shashthighara to Nandishvara.
[^200]: So in A (by the scan): "এই কুঞ্জে নবাগ্রাম" (C "নবা গ্রাম"); the sense is unclear: possibly "কুঞ্জ-নামা গ্রাম," "the village named Kunja," which agrees with the name Kunjera in 784. Sri Kunda is Radha-kunda.
[^201]: A (by the scan) reads "মোয়নাখ্যা" ("called Moyana"), the 1960 edition "মোরনাখ্যা" ("called Morana"); translated after the 1960 edition, the reading is unclear.
[^202]: The source of the verse has not been identified (the text has only "তথাহি"). Surya is the father of the Yamuna.
[^203]: "Keu na ai" (keu nā āi) is Braj-bhasha for "Why has [She] not come?"
[^204]: "ভদায়র" (bhadāyara): so in A (by the scan); the 1960 edition apparently has "ভদ্রায়র". Bhadra is one of the leaders of the groups of gopis (yutheshvaris).
[^205]: The name is explained by the words "mag hera" (mag herā), "they gazed at the road."
[^206]: Phagua is the gift claimed at the Holi festival; phagu (gulal) is the red powder people throw on one another at Holi. The author derives the name Ganthuli from "ganthi," "knot."
[^207]: So in the text: Vitthala (Vitthalanatha, 805; Vitthaleshvara, 815) served a Deity of Sri Krishna Chaitanya.
[^208]: The Lord did not climb Govardhana, regarding it as nondifferent from Krishna; Gopala came to Ganthuli on the pretext of the danger of a raid (CC Madhya 18).
[^209]: Madhavendra Puri made two Bengali brahmanas his disciples and entrusted them with the service of Gopala (CC Madhya 4).
[^210]: After Krishna lifted Govardhana, Indra together with the celestial cow Surabhi came to beg His forgiveness (*Bhag.* 10.27).
[^211]: Devashirsha means "head of the gods"; munishirsha (823), "head of the sages." G writes "দেবশীর্ষস্থানকুণ্ড" as a single name.
[^212]: Pramodana is from pramoda, "joy, delight" (see 826).
[^213]: Kandara means "cave, ravine," setu "bridge, dam"; A (by the scan) reads «সেতু কন্দরা» (setu kandarā), the 1960 edition as one word; the sense of the name is unclear.
[^214]: The 1960 edition reads gandhaśilā basiyā ("being set"; so translated); A (by the scan) has «গন্ধশিলা রসিয়া» (rasiyā, possibly "full of rasa"); the reading is unclear. Gandha-shila means "the fragrant stone."
[^215]: According to tradition, Nanda and the cowherds wished to travel as pilgrims to Badri-narayana (Badarikashrama in the Himalayas); Krishna revealed Narayana to them here — hence Adi-Badri (827).
[^216]: Kanva is the sage in whose hermitage Shakuntala grew up (*Mahabharata*).
[^217]: Kamyavana is the fourth of the twelve forests of Vraja (see 835), in the western part of Vraja; today Kaman.
[^218]: Shiva Kameshvara is the guardian of Kamyavana.
[^219]: According to the *Mahabharata*, the Pandavas lived in exile in the Kamyaka forest; tradition connects it with Kamyavana.
[^220]: Manikarnika is the famous ghat in Kashi (Varanasi), and Vishvanatha is Shiva, the lord of Kashi; here they are the holy places of the same names in Kamyavana (cf. 855).
[^221]: In imitation of Rama, who built a bridge across the ocean to Lanka.
[^222]: Lukalukani is the game of hide-and-seek, michali that of blind man's buff (local words).
[^223]: Kashi, Gaya, Prayaga, Pushkara, Gomati and Dvaraka are great tirthas of India; in Kamyavana there are ponds bearing their names.
[^224]: Ghosharani, "the queen of the cowherd settlement," is a usual epithet of Yashoda. Who is called "the king of the cowherds" is not specified in the text.
[^225]: Shyama-kunda, Lalita-kunda and Vishakha-kunda (862) here are ponds of Kamyavana, not those at Radha-kunda.
[^226]: Mohini is the enchanting female form in which Vishnu distributed the nectar of immortality to the gods after the churning of the ocean (*Bhag.* 8.8–9).
[^227]: The 1960 ed. has "Mohini-kunda" (as in 864).
[^228]: Pichhalini-shila means "the slippery stone": one slides down from it (868).
[^229]: Bajana means "playing musical instruments"; Bajana-shila is "the stone of music."
[^230]: Santana: so in A (by the scan: «সন্তনকুণ্ড») and in the 1960 edition. Prithudaka is also the name of a famous tirtha at Kurukshetra.
[^231]: The asura Vyoma, taking the form of a cowherd boy, during a game of "thieves and sheep" carried the boys off into a cave; Krishna slew him (*Bhag.* 10.37).
[^232]: Dhulauda means "[the place where] the dust flies up."
[^233]: Uddhava is Krishna's friend and counselor, sent by Him from Mathura to console Nanda, Yashoda and the gopis (*Bhag.* 10.46–47).
[^234]: Eight praharas (watches) means all day and night.
[^235]: This Kadamkhandi ("grove of kadambas") is not the one near Govardhana (5.660). Chaturmukha ("four-faced") is Brahma.
[^236]: Svarnahara means "golden necklace"; sona (Hindi) means "gold."
[^237]: Varshana is the village of King Vrishabhanu, Radha's father, at the foot of the hill; see 890–894.
[^238]: Sankari-khor is the narrow path between the two hills of Varshana (893). Garh means "fort"; the hilltops are so called. Dana, mana and vilasa are the play of the toll (891), Radhika's sulking and the play (892).
[^239]: The verse number follows the 1912/13 ed. (by the scan of A: "উদ্দীপনে বয়ঃসন্ধৌ ৬ শ্লোকঃ"); in the 1960 ed. the number is garbled by OCR (possibly "৮").
[^240]: A play on words: guṇī means "girded with a cord" and "full of merits"; upacaya "fullness" and "rise"; valibhiḥ "with the folds [of the belly]" and "with the strong [allies]." Subjects greet a new king with music and gifts.
[^241]: The 1960 ed. has "in this kunda."
[^242]: The verse number in the editions is unclear: in the 1912/13 edition (by the scan of A) it is "12," the same as for 901; in the 1960 edition it is garbled by OCR ("১২৭"). The number is omitted.
[^243]: Chiksauli is a village next to Varshana; its former name Chitrashali is connected with Rai's wondrous (chitra) attire.
[^244]: Gahvara-vana ("the forest in the gorge") is a grove on the hill of Varshana.
[^245]: The pastime of sowing pearls is described in the *Mukta-charita* of Raghunatha Dasa Gosvami.
[^246]: Vrishabhanupura is Varshana. This Bhanukhora is a pond at Varshana; not to be confused with the Bhanukhora near Radha-kunda (587).
[^247]: All copies read jiyāla (checked against the scan of A) (the jiyala tree); possibly an error for piyāla (cf. Piyala-sarovara, 915).
[^248]: Pilu is a tree (Salvadora) with edible berries.
[^249]: Prema-vaichittya is a state of love in which the beloved, even while near her Lover, experiences separation from Him (UN, section on separation).
[^250]: Sanketa ("signal, place of tryst") is a village between Varshana and Nandagrama, where the sakhis arranged the secret meetings of Radha and Krishna.
[^251]: Purva-raga is attraction before meeting (see 926); the "brief meeting" (sankshipta-sambhoga) in UN is the union that follows purva-raga.
[^252]: Nandishvara is the hill at Nandagrama where Nanda's house stood; see 931–940.
[^253]: The words of the women of Mathura who saw Krishna and Balarama in Kamsa's arena. In the 1960 edition, the thirteenth verse; in the standard numbering, *Bhag.* 10.44.13. Giritra is Shiva; Rama is Lakshmi.
[^254]: Pavana-sarovara is the main lake of Nandagrama, at the foot of Nandishvara.
[^255]: Probably the *Mathura-mahatmya* of Rupa Gosvami, a compilation of Puranic verses on the Mathura-mandala. The 1960 edition lacks the introductory line.
[^256]: Mahaprabhu saw the Deities of Nanda, Yashoda and Krishna in a cave on Nandishvara hill (CC Madhya 18); see 942–953.
[^257]: Parjanya is the father of Nanda and the grandfather of Krishna; Devamidha is his father.
[^258]: Giridhara ("the Holder of the hill") is Krishna. The name Kshunahara is connected with Parjanya's fast ("giving up food"); A (by the scan) reads «ক্ষুণ্ণাহার» in both places (kṣuṇṇāhāra: kṣuṇṇa "cut down," āhāra "food"), the 1960 edition kṣuṇāhāra.
[^259]: Paurnamasi is the venerable ascetic of Vraja, an incarnation of Yogamaya and the grandmother of Madhumangala; she arranges the meetings of Radha and Krishna (see 969).
[^260]: Mana is the heroine's loving pique; abhisara is her secret going out to the tryst.
[^261]: Nandimukhi is the granddaughter of Paurnamasi and the daughter of Sandipani Muni; she helps arrange the meetings of Radha and Krishna. Avanti (Ujjain, 972) is the city where Sandipani lived.
[^262]: The *Bhakti-rasamrita-sindhu* is Rupa Gosvami's treatise on bhakti-rasa; the Dakshina-vibhaga ("Southern Division") is its second part, on the components of rasa; a lahari ("wave") is a chapter. There Krishna's age is treated as an uddipana (a stimulus of feeling). Kaumara is early childhood, pauganda boyhood, kaishora early youth.
[^263]: The Pashchima-vibhaga ("Western Division") of the BRS deals with the five principal rasas; its third lahari is on the rasa of friendship (sakhya).
[^264]: The verse number is omitted: the 1912/13 edition (by the scan of A) has "৫৪", apparently a misprint for 24 (cf. 23 in 983 and 25 in 989); the 1960 edition has "verses 23–24".
[^265]: "Dark curling locks" follows the 1888 and 1912/13 editions (*nīlālaka*). The 1960 edition reads *līlālaka*: "locks playfully [arranged]".
[^266]: The narma-sakhas are Krishna's most intimate friends, confidants of His heart's secrets.
[^267]: Ujjvala-rasa ("the radiant rasa") is madhura-rasa, the love of the gopis for Krishna.
[^268]: Kandarpa is Kamadeva, the god of love.
[^269]: "Absorbed": rata, so in the 1960 edition and apparently in A (by the scan; the letters are smudged); the 1888 edition has "rada".
[^270]: Skt. *vayaḥ-sandhi* ("the meeting of ages"), *navya*, *vyakta*, *pūrṇa*.
[^271]: Karela-kunda is a pond near Nandishvara (Nandagrama); karil (*karīla*) is a thorny shrub of Vraja (*Capparis decidua*).
[^272]: Madhusudana ("the slayer of Madhu") is a name of Krishna; the same word also means a bee ("drinker of honey").
[^273]: Panihari (Braj) means "water-carrier".
[^274]: Rohini is Vasudeva's wife, the mother of Balarama, who lived in Vraja in the house of Nanda.
[^275]: By custom, after eating one walks a hundred steps and then rests.
[^276]: Which goddess is meant is not said; possibly Paurnamasi (Yogamaya; cf. 968–969 and 1027).
[^277]: Sahasi is from *sāhasa*, "boldness, daring".
[^278]: Krishna sowed pearls, and pearl-bearing creepers grew from them; this pastime is described in the *Mukta-charita* of Raghunatha Dasa Gosvami.
[^279]: Akrura is Krishna's uncle of the Yadu clan, sent by Kamsa to bring Krishna and Balarama to Mathura; his coming separated the gopis from Krishna (*Bhag.* 10.38–39).
[^280]: *Krūra* means "cruel". Cf. the gopis' reproach in the *Bhagavatam* (10.39): he who is called Akrura ("not cruel") is cruel.
[^281]: Uddhava is Krishna's friend and counsellor in Mathura; sent by Him to Vraja to console Nanda, Yashoda and the gopis, he gave the gopis a message about yoga and knowledge (*Bhag.* 10.46–47). Yogiya-sthana means "the place of yoga".
[^282]: Udho is the popular form of Uddhava's name; Udho-kriya-sthana means "the place of Uddhava's doings".
[^283]: Kanu is Krishna. Kusumabana ("whose arrows are flowers") is Kamadeva. "Playing" is *khuralī* (A, by the scan: «মুরলি খুরলি»; the 1960 edition *muralī buvali*).
[^284]: Govindadasa is the poet Govindadasa Kaviraja, a disciple of Srinivasa Acharya; his name is the signature of the pada.
[^285]: Gendukhora (*gendu*, "ball"; *khor*, "hollow") is a place of ball games near Nandishvara. Reading after B (*gedu-khor*; cf. «গেদু», "ball," in 1055); A (by the scan) and the 1960 edition have «গেছুখোর» (*gechhu-khor*).
[^286]: Jiva Gosvami's *Gopala-champu* has two parts, the Purva (first) and the Uttara (second); its chapters are called purana ("filling").
[^287]: A difficult passage: *asmat-tṛṇ-mātra-da-paricaraṇaḥ*; translated after the anvaya of the 1960 edition ("whose service gives us only thirst").
[^288]: So in the 1960 edition (*Bhag.* 10.15.14); the 1912/13 edition (by the scan of A) has «১৫ অধ্যায়ে ১৩ শ্লোকঃ», the thirteenth verse.
[^289]: Gupta-kunda means "the secret pond".
[^290]: Meheran is a village near Nandagrama. Abhinanda is Nanda's elder brother.
[^291]: Javata (*Yāvaṭa*) is a village not far from Nandagrama where Radha lived in the house of Her father-in-law; Yaograma (*yāo-grāma*) is its local name.
[^292]: Abhimanyu is the cowherd regarded as Radha's husband; Jatila is his mother, Kutila his sister. Mukhara (1076) is Radha's grandmother.
[^293]: The *Gitavali* is a collection of songs by Rupa Gosvami within the *Stavamala*. Sanatana in the last line is the poet's signature: the name of his elder brother, Sanatana Gosvami. The 1960 edition does not name the raga; the 1912/13 edition (by the scan of A) has «ধানাশ্রীঃ। ৬৩৬ পৃং। ১—৬।», i.e. raga Dhanashri, "p. 636, [verses] 1–6".
[^294]: Pivana (*pīvana*) means "drinking" (cf. 1087). The word *nadī* ("river") is in all copies; the sense is unclear — perhaps a stream by the kunda.
[^295]: Ladili (*lāḍilī*, Braj) means "darling", an affectionate name of Radha.
[^296]: Amrita-hasta means "nectar-handed": whatever Radha cooks becomes like nectar. This boon is usually attributed to the sage Durvasa; here, at Narada-kunda, the sage is not named.
[^297]: Iya (*iyā*), aiya (*āiyā*) are sung syllables; "ta ta thira mira…" (1097, 1100, 1102) are the syllables of the dance rhythm (bols), given in transliteration; checked against the scan of A (which has «বুদ্ধ ঝিক্ ঝিক্ ঝিক্», «থুন্না দ্রমকট», «কিটিঝক্»).
[^298]: Shyamaghana ("dark cloud") is Krishna (cf. Ghanashyama).
[^299]: Manasija ("born of the mind") is Kamadeva.
[^300]: There follow padas about how Krishna in various guises makes His way into Javata to Radha; "once", "and once" are their headings in the text.
[^301]: A malini is a woman of the gardener caste who strings and sells garlands; here Krishna is in her guise.
[^302]: Vinodini ("she who is skilled in play") is a name of Radha.
[^303]: Gaja-moti is a pearl believed to be born in an elephant's head; here, Radha's ornament.
[^304]: Rama is Lakshmi.
[^305]: Ghanashyama ("dark as a rain cloud") is a name of Krishna and also the second name of the author, Narahari Chakravarti; here and in 1143 and 1155 it is the poet's signature in the song (bhanita), playing on this coincidence.
[^306]: "And at another time" — kvacic ca (Skt.): so begins the account of another of Krishna's tricks in Javata; Gauri is the name of the raga to which the song is sung.
[^307]: Kundalata is the wife of Subhadra, a cousin of Krishna, a daughter-in-law in Nanda's household; she helps the meetings of Radha and Krishna. Yashoda sends her to bring Radha.
[^308]: A and C read śyāmā-vadhū ("the young wife Shyama," "the dark young wife"), the 1960 edition śyāma-vadhū ("the young wife Shyama[sundara]"); in either case Krishna in disguise.
[^309]: Brother-in-law (devara) — the husband's younger brother. Kundalata's husband Subhadra is Krishna's cousin, so Krishna is her devara.
[^310]: Dvijaraja — "king of the twice-born": both "the best of brahmanas" and the moon. Hence the joke about worshiping the moon (1146, 1151).
[^311]: The Padyavali is an anthology of verses about Krishna compiled by Rupa Gosvami. The bold old woman is Jatila; koli is the jujube (badari, Ziziphus).
[^312]: Kokilavana ("the forest of cuckoos") — a forest to the west of Javata (see 1161).
[^313]: Ajanak — a village near Javata and Nandagrama; its name is explained from añjana, "kohl for the eyes" (see 1170–1176).
[^314]: Vidyudvari (Bijoari) — a village nearby; its name is explained from vidyut, "lightning" (see 1185–1186).
[^315]: Parasho — a village nearby; its name is explained from paraśvaḥ (Beng. paraśu), "the day after tomorrow" (see 1189).
[^316]: Shi — a village near Parasho; its name is explained from śīghra, "soon" (see 1193).
[^317]: Kamai and Karala (now Karahla) are neighbouring villages of Vraja not far from Varshana and Nandagrama.
[^318]: Chandravali is the leader of a group of gopis, Radha's rival; her husband is the cowherd Govardhana Malla (1204).
[^319]: More precisely, her cousin: her father Chandrabhanu is a younger brother of Vrishabhanu, Radha's father (1202–1203).
[^320]: Padma is Chandravali's closest friend. The group leaders (yutheshvaris) are the chief gopis, each with her own group of friends.
[^321]: Piyaso is from Hindi *pyās*, "thirst".
[^322]: Sahara (now Sahar) is a village of Vraja, the abode of Upananda, Nanda's elder brother; hence Krishna is to him "his brother's son" (1208).
[^323]: Subhadra is Upananda's son, Krishna's cousin; not to be confused with Subhadra, Krishna's sister.
[^324]: A (by the scan) reads «শ্যামঃ সূক্ষ্মমতির্বাগতিমধুরো জ্যোতির্বিদামগ্রণীঃ» (*sūkṣma-matir vāg-ati-madhuraḥ*), which confirms the translation; the 1960 edition is garbled. The Lord of Speech (Gishpati) is Brihaspati, the preceptor of the gods.
[^325]: Kundalata is Subhadra's wife, a friend of Radha; by Yashoda's order she brings Radha to cook for Krishna (1213).
[^326]: A (by the scan) reads «সাঁখি» (sā̃khi); the 1888 edition sakhi, the 1960 edition sīkhi.
[^327]: Talao (Hindi *tālāb*, *tālāo*) means "pond".
[^328]: Madhumangala is a brahmana, Krishna's jesting friend, the grandson of Paurnamasi.
[^329]: Chhatrapati means "possessor of the umbrella": the royal umbrella (*chhatra*) is the sign of kingship.
[^330]: Umrao (Persian *umarā*, "nobles") means a nobleman, a high dignitary; hence the name of the village Umrao (1258), where Kishori-kunda is (cf. BR 1.325).
[^331]: In the 1960 ed.: "Sri Madhumangala, without saying anything, ran away."
[^332]: A and C (by the scans) read «এবে কৃষ্ণ দেহ রাজ্যে কর অধিকার», understood as *kṛṣṇa-deha-rājye* ("in the kingdom of Krishna's body"); it could also be taken as "now give Krishna (?), rule the kingdom." In the 1960 ed.: "Now take Krishna, rule the kingdom."
[^333]: Kishori-kunda is a pond at the village of Umrao (1258) near Chhatravana; Kishori ("the young girl") is Radha, the daughter of Vrishabhanu. On Lokanatha Gosvami's life here and on the Deity Radha-vinoda, see BR 1.325–338.
[^334]: Nari and Semari are neighbouring villages of Vraja near Chhata, where a goddess is worshipped (cf. 1276); according to Narahari, their names go back to the former Kinnari and Shyamari (1271–1275). A and C (by the scans) read «নরীসেমরী» (narīsemarī); the 1960 edition nirīsemarī.
[^335]: Shyama-sakhi is Krishna in the guise of a gopi friend named Shyama.
[^336]: A kinnari is a celestial musician woman (of the race of the kinnaras).
[^337]: Which Devi is meant is not said: perhaps Radha (cf. 1275) or the goddess worshipped in this village (on it, see 1270–1271).
[^338]: Chhatravana — from *chhatra*, "royal umbrella": here Krishna was proclaimed king; today the town of Chhata.
[^339]: Radha-sthali — "the place of Radha": here Radha was anointed to the throne of Vrindavana (see verse 1280).
[^340]: The Creator (Vidhi) is Brahma; Savitri is a goddess, Brahma's consort; Manasi-ganga is at Govardhana. The last words of the verse read "সোন্মত্তরাধাস্থলী" (sonmatta-rādhāsthalī) in the 1912/13 and 1960 editions; even in the scan of A the letters are not quite clear (possibly «সাস্মাংস্তু», sāsmāṁs tu); translated after the anvaya of the 1960 edition (sā asmān — "that… us").
[^341]: Khadiravana is the seventh of the twelve forests of Vraja; *khadira* is the catechu acacia.
[^342]: *Sangama* — "meeting, union".
[^343]: Kadamba-khandi — "a grove of kadambas".
[^344]: Baka is the demon in the form of a huge crane, sent by Kamsa and killed by Krishna (*Bhag.* 10.11).
[^345]: Dvadashi is the twelfth lunar day of each half of the month; there are twenty-four in a year.
[^346]: Sri Uddhava Dasa and Madhava are Vaishnavas who were there; Uddhava Dasa is not Uddhava, Krishna's friend.
[^347]: Baithan (Braj, "the place where one sits"; cf. Hindi *baiṭhnā*, "to sit") — today two villages, Chhota Baithan and Bara Baithan ("Little" and "Big" Baithan).
[^348]: Rupa Gosvami is the younger brother of Sanatana Gosvami; the Vrajavasis called the brothers by one joint name (see 1352).
[^349]: Nipa is the kadamba tree; Nipavana is "the forest of kadambas".
[^350]: Kuntala means "locks of hair". So in the 1888 and 1912/13 editions; the 1960 edition has "Kundala-kunda" (*kuṇḍala*, "earrings").
[^351]: Bedokhor (*beṛokhor*): the meaning of the name is not explained in the text; in Vraja place names "khor" is a narrow passage between hills (cf. Sankari-khor).
[^352]: Pahari means "hill"; Charana-pahari is "the Hill of the Feet" (the name is explained in 1397).
[^353]: The name Haroyal is apparently connected with *hārā*, "to lose" (see 1399). This Krishna-kunda is a different one from that in 1389.
[^354]: A (by the scan) reads «সাতোঙা» (*sātoṅā*), as in 450: the same village of Satoha, the place of austerities of the sage Shantanu (5.450).
[^355]: Kupa means "well"; Vadya-shila is "the stone of musical instruments".
[^356]: The name is explained by the word *pāi(la)*, "She found".
[^357]: Chalana-shila is "the stone of walking".
[^358]: The name Kamari is derived from *kāma*, "desire".
[^359]: Bichhor (*bichhoṛ*) means "separation" (see 1410). The Moon-faced One (*candramukhī*) is apparently Radha.
[^360]: Kadamba-khandi is "the kadamba grove". A play on words: Tiloyar — *tilek* ("not for a moment").
[^361]: Shringaravata is "the banyan of adornment".
[^362]: A (by the scan) reads «বাসোনী» (*bāsonī*), the 1960 edition *vāsosī*; the name is connected with *suvāsa*, "fragrance".
[^363]: Paya is from *payaḥ*, "milk".
[^364]: Kotara means "a hollow in a tree". Kotavana is apparently present-day Kotvan, near the northern border of Vraja.
[^365]: Sheshashayi ("He who lies on Shesha") is a place near the northern border of Vraja with a Deity of Vishnu lying on the serpent Shesha (Ananta); Kshira-samudra ("the Ocean of Milk") is a pond there.
[^366]: Goshtha means "a cowherd settlement"; here it is Vraja.
[^367]: A (by the scan) has «খম্ব হরে খামী গ্রাম» (*khamba hare khāmī grām*), the 1960 edition *khakhahare "khānigrām"* (only *khānigrām* marked as a name). The division into village names is conjectural.
[^368]: Banachari is a village on the northern border of Vraja (today Banchari).
[^369]: A (by the scan) has «খররো» (*khararo*), the 1960 edition *thararo* (in quotation marks as a name). The name seems to be connected with *khair*, "welfare".
[^370]: Ujani is from *ujāna*, "against the current".
[^371]: Khelanavana means "the forest of games"; Khela-tirtha, "the holy place of games". Khelana-vata means "the banyan of games"; so in the 1912/13 edition; the 1960 edition has "of this Khelanavana".
[^372]: Rama-ghat is a place on the bank of the Yamuna where Balarama performed the rasa-lila with the gopis during His visit to Vraja (*Bhag.* 10.65).
[^373]: Balarama's visit from Dvaraka to Vraja is described in *Bhag.* 10.65. Chaitra and Vaishakha are the spring months (March to May).
[^374]: The *Sri Krishna-chaitanya-charita* is the Sanskrit biography of Sri Chaitanya composed by Murari Gupta (the so-called *Kadcha* of Murari Gupta); it is divided into four *prakramas* (parts).
[^375]: Madhu and Madhava are the months Chaitra and Vaishakha. In the standard numbering, *Bhag.* 10.65.17.
[^376]: Velavali is the name of the raga. The Silver Mountain is Kailasa.
[^377]: The king of birds is Garuda. "Surpassing the moon": so in the 1912/13 edition (*jini*); the 1960 edition has "like the moon" (*jani*).
[^378]: Ghanashyama is the author's second name; here it is the poet's signature (*bhanita*). "Offers himself in sacrifice": *nichani*, the custom of waving oneself around the beloved, giving oneself for him.
[^379]: A single earring is a traditional feature of Balarama's appearance.
[^380]: "Neck": *kaṇṭha*; the scan of A clearly reads «ত্রিবলি বলিত কণ্ঠ সুললিত কক্ষ» (in the OCR copies a syllable is lost).
[^381]: Malakosha is the name of the raga. *Madhu-yamini* means "spring night" (Madhu is the spring month Chaitra).
[^382]: So in A (by the scan: «বহু পিয় চউতোর সকল»): *cauṭora*, apparently "on all four sides"; *bahu piya*, "many beloveds". The Lord (*nāha*) is Baladeva.
[^383]: So in A (by the scan: «মগন ঘন ভণ তন আয়য়ে»): *ghana bhaṇa tana āyaye* is translated conjecturally ("flocks with a dense humming"); *paraśa pararasa* (B: *para rasa*), "the supreme rasa of touch" (possibly "the rasa of mutual touch"). Narahari is the poet's signature.
[^384]: In the standard numbering, *Bhag.* 10.65.18.
[^385]: The text has one introductory line for both verses. In the standard numbering, *Bhag.* 10.65.19–20.
[^386]: Varuni is the goddess of intoxicating drink, the daughter of Varuna; according to tradition, she appeared at the churning of the ocean together with the nectar. In the *Harivamsha* (1498) she speaks these words to Balarama.
[^387]: Mridanga: so in A (by the scan: «মৃদঙ্গ পিনাক বীণা») and in the 1960 edition; "স্বর্গ" in the OCR of copy B is a recognition error. The pinaka is a stringed instrument.
[^388]: Shankarabharana is the name of a raga. "Da drimi…", "taka…" here and further in the songs are syllables of dance and drum rhythm (bols), given in transliteration; checked against the scan of A (in 1504: «ধা দৃগু দৃগুধ বিথুঙ্কট»).
[^389]: Ghanashyama — here, in 1510 and 1513 — is the poet's signature (the second name of Narahari Chakravarti).
[^390]: Kedara is the name of a raga; triputa is the name of a tala (rhythmic cycle). Ratipati ("the husband of Rati") is Kamadeva. A (by the scan) reads «বাজে ঝিগ ঝিগ ঝিগ ঝেন্দ্রাং» ("jhig").
[^391]: "অলি যোর" (ali yora) in all copies (checked against the scan of A); the sense is unclear; translated "the bees hum in swarms."
[^392]: "Offers himself" — nichhani: a rite in which one "offers oneself" for the beloved, warding off misfortune from him; the usual formula of the poet's signature (likewise in 1534).
[^393]: Cf. *Bhagavatam* (10.65): Balarama, coming to Vraja, spent two spring months with the gopis, drank varuni and, when the Yamuna did not come at His call, drew her with His plough.
[^394]: "Having sat": basiyā, so in the 1960 edition and apparently in A (by the scan: «বসিয়া রমণীগণ সঙ্গে»); the OCR of A/B has "বগিয়া".
[^395]: Deshapala is the name of a raga. Stanzas 1538–1539 are in Sanskrit. Halayudha ("armed with the plough") is a name of Balarama.
[^396]: Nrihari is the same as Narahari: the poet's signature. "Sa ri ga ma pa dha ni" are the names of the svaras (notes); "dhiki dhikata dheya", "tenna" are rhythmic syllables (bols), given in transliteration.
[^397]: Shri is the name of a raga. Revati is the wife of Balarama. "Re" is a singing vocative. "He frees from the burden of composure" (dhṛti-bhara-mocana) — that is, He deprives [the lovely women] of their composure.
[^398]: A (by the scan) reads «নরহরিমব» (naraharimaba), the 1960 edition «নরহরিমধ»; the sense is unclear; translated "[Lord of] Narahari" (the poet's signature). What follows are rhythmic syllables; G "অইনব", A/B "অইঅ".
[^399]: A verse of Raghunatha Dasa Gosvami. Krishna (kṛṣṇā, "the dark one") is here the Yamuna; the Plough-bearer (hali) is Balarama; Rama-ghatta is Rama-ghat.
[^400]: On Nityananda's pilgrimage to the tirthas before meeting Sri Chaitanya, see CB Adi 9.
[^401]: Shakata (śākaṭa) is a kind of tree; by the sense, one that grew from the tooth-stick Nityananda had used.
[^402]: The Lord of Navadvipa is Sri Chaitanya. Nityananda awaits His manifestation as the Lord in Navadvipa.
[^403]: Kachchhavana (kaccha-vana; kacchapa, "turtle") is a forest near Rama-ghat.
[^404]: Bhushanavana — "the forest of ornaments."
[^405]: Bhandiravata is the banyan in Bhandiravana, on the eastern bank of the Yamuna, where Balarama killed the asura Pralamba (*Bhag.* 10.18); Akshayavata ("the undecaying banyan") is its present popular name (1567).
[^406]: The Slayer of Baka (*bakabhid*) is Krishna; Madana is Kamadeva, the god of love; Bhandiraka is Bhandira. The verse number follows the 1960 edition (৯৩); B reads "৯৬," apparently an OCR error (96 is the number of the verse at 1601).
[^407]: Munjatavi and Ishikatavi mean "the thicket of munja grass" and "the thicket of ishika reeds"; here Krishna swallowed the forest fire (*Bhag.* 10.19; see 1585).
[^408]: Bhandari: so in A (by the scan: «ঐ যে ভাণ্ডারী গ্রাম») and in the 1960 edition (*bhāṇḍārī*).
[^409]: Tapovana means "the forest of austerity": here the daughters of the cowherds kept a vow to the goddess Katyayani in order to obtain Krishna as their husband (*Bhag.* 10.22).
[^410]: Chir-ghat means "the ghat of the garments," the place where Krishna stole the gopis' clothes (*Bhag.* 10.22; see 1590–1594). Katyayani is a name of the goddess Durga.
[^411]: The story of Nanda's abduction by Varuna's servant is in *Bhag.* 10.28.
[^412]: Bhaya means "fear."
[^413]: Vatsavana means "the forest of the calves"; Brahma's theft of the calves and cowherd boys is in *Bhag.* 10.13.
[^414]: Balihara: so in A (by the scan: «এই বলিহারা নাম গ্রাম») and B; the name is associated with *bāla-hara*, "the stealing of the boys." The 1960 edition reads *bālahārā*, and for "the boys and the rest" (*bālakādi*) has *bālakāri*.
[^415]: The name is derived from *parīkṣā*, "test."
[^416]: Sei (*sei*) means "that [very one]"; the name is explained at 1607.
[^417]: A (by the scan) reads «এচোমুহা গ্রামে» (*ecomuhā*), as does the 1960 edition; understood as *e comuhā*, "this [village of] Chomuha." The name apparently comes from *catur-mukha*, "the Four-faced One."
[^418]: Bhiru-chaturmukha means "the Frightened Four-faced One." "Together with [its] Lord" (*seśam*) follows the 1960 edition's anvaya (*īśa-sahitam*).
[^419]: Sarpa-sthali means "the place of the serpent"; Aghavana, "the forest of Agha"; Aghasura is the demon in the form of a huge serpent who swallowed the cowherd boys (*Bhag.* 10.12). Sapoli follows A (by the scan: «অঘবন নাম লোকে কহয়ে সপৌলী», *sapaulī*) and B (*sapolī*).
[^420]: The Conqueror of Mura (*murajit*) is Krishna. The aranis are the two wooden sticks with which fire is kindled by friction; "the fire at the edge of the aranis" (*araṇyos taṭa-pāvaka*) is a tentative understanding. The 1960 edition gives no anvaya for this verse.
[^421]: The name Jayet is derived from *jaya*, "victory."
[^422]: A (by the scan) reads «অঘাসুর বধে এসিয়ান। তেঞি এসেয়ানো গ্রাম সেহোনা আখ্যান» (*e siyān*, *e seyāno*); the 1960 edition has *soyāno*; Seyano is adopted, from *seyānā*, "clever, shrewd." Sehona (*sehonā*) reads the same in all copies.
[^423]: So in the 1912/13 edition (*grām*); the 1960 edition has "names given by the cowherds in ancient times" (*nām*).
[^424]: The hill by Krishna-kunda is *kṛṣṇakuṇḍa-ṭīlā* (*ṭīlā*, "hill, mound"); this Krishna-kunda is different from those at 1389 and 1398.
[^425]: Maghera: so in A (by the scan: «দেখ মঘেরা এ গ্রাম। পূর্ব্বে জানাইল মঘহেরা হয় নাম»); the 1960 edition has *aghērā*. This is not the village of Maghera at 792–793. The words *pūrve jānāila* can also be understood as "earlier [I] told [you]" (cf. 792–793).
[^426]: A (by the scan) reads «এ আটস্থ গ্রামে» (*āṭastha*), the 1960 edition *āṭastu*; Atas is adopted. The name is apparently connected with Ashtavakra. Ashtavakra is a sage whose body was bent in eight places.
[^427]: Shakra-sthana means "the place of Shakra" (Indra). Indra's downpour upon Vraja and Krishna's lifting of Govardhana are in *Bhag.* 10.24–25.
[^428]: The name Varahar is derived from *varāha*, "boar."
[^429]: The name Harasali is apparently derived from *rāsa-sthalī*, "the place of the rasa dance."
[^430]: "Lovely": *valgu*, so in A (by the scan: «বৈদগ্ধ্যোজ্জ্বলবল্গুবল্লববধূবর্গেণ»; two syllables, as the metre requires); the 1960 edition has *bandhura* (same meaning).
[^431]: *Bhakti-rasamrita* is the *Bhakti-rasamrita-sindhu* of Sri Rupa Gosvami. The mangalacharana is the auspicious verses at the beginning of a book.
[^432]: The eastern country (*pūrva deśa*) is Bengal, Jiva's homeland.
[^433]: A digvijayi ("conqueror of the quarters") is a scholar who travels about defeating everyone in debate. A jaya-patri ("certificate of victory") is a written admission of defeat given by the loser to the victor.
[^434]: Surukhuru (*surukhuru*) is a village on the eastern bank of the Yamuna, where Krishna showed favour to the gods (1672).
[^435]: "O Vasudha": *vasudhe*, so in A (by the scan: «তত্র গত্বাচ বসুধে»); the OCR copies have *vandhe* (Varaha addressing the Earth, as in other verses of the Adi-varaha Purana).
[^436]: Vasudeva (*vāsudeva*) is a name of Krishna ("the son of Vasudeva"), here the Deity of Bhandira; not to be confused with His father Vasudeva (*vasudeva*).
[^437]: The name Chhaheri (*chāheri*) is explained by the word "shade" (*chāyā*, 1684).
[^438]: A math (*māṭh*) is a large clay vessel (see 1687). Mathgrama is probably present-day Mant near Bhandiravana.
[^439]: Mana-sarovara ("the lake of sulking") is, by tradition, the place where Radha remained in mana (sulky anger towards Krishna).
[^440]: In the verse Lohajangha is the guardian of the forest; in the Bengali text (1697) he is a demon slain by Krishna.
[^441]: In the Gaudiya Vaishnava understanding, Krishna was also born of Yashoda in Gokula-Mahavana; cf. the birthplace in Mathura (5.186–224).
[^442]: Brihat-kanana ("the Great Forest") is Mahavana. *Nava-gavī-lakṣe dve* is understood as in the 1960 edition: "twice nine lakhs" (1,800,000) of cows; "two lakhs of young cows" is also possible. A prastha is a measure of grain.
[^443]: *Sanātana-mūrti* — "the eternal form" [of Krishna]; the word carries the poet's signature, the name of Sanatana Gosvami (cf. 5.1080).
[^444]: Sanatana is the poet's signature: in the songs of the *Gitavali* Rupa Gosvami puts the name of his elder brother, Sanatana Gosvami (see 5.1083). Manasa is a Himalayan lake, the home of swans.
[^445]: Garga (Gargacharya) is the priest of the Yadu dynasty; at Nanda's request he secretly, fearing Kamsa, gave names to Rama (Balarama) and Krishna (*Bhag.* 10.8).
[^446]: The verse numbers of the *Padyavali* here and below are as in the editions of the *Bhakti-ratnakara*. Gorochana is a bright yellow fragrant pigment obtained from cows; tilaka is drawn with it.
[^447]: The 1960 ed. has "কংসের আবাসে" (kamsera avase, "in Kamsa's abode"), the 1912/13 ed. (by the scan of A) "কংসের আরামে" (kamsera arame, "in Kamsa's garden / for Kamsa's pleasure"). Translated by the sense: Trinavarta is a demon sent by Kamsa.
[^448]: Brahmanda-ghat ("the ghat of the universe") is a ghat on the Yamuna near Mahavana (Gokula).
[^449]: Dhanishtha is one of the gopis, a maidservant in Nanda's house.
[^450]: Shambhu is Shiva; the Lotus-born is Brahma; the enemy of Krauncha is Karttikeya (Skanda); the lord of the gods is Indra; the lord of wealth is Kubera. "Thoo-thoo" is the spitting with which the mother wards off the evil eye and evil spirits.
[^451]: Saumitri is Lakshmana, the son of Sumitra. Hearing His mother's story, Krishna remembers His own pastime as Rama.
[^452]: The verse begins in A (by the scan) «শ্যামোচ্চন্দ্রা স্বপিষি» (*śyāmocchandrā*: "the night (*śyāmā*) with the moon risen high"); the 1960 ed. reads «যামোচ্চন্দ্রা» (*yāmocchandrā*), which it explains as "the night is almost over". Either way the sense is late night. Narahari ("man-lion") is Nrisimha, who tore apart the Danava Hiranyakashipu.
[^453]: Yamalarjuna-bhanjana-tirtha ("the tirtha of the breaking of the twin arjuna trees") is the place where Krishna, tied to the mortar, dragged it between two arjuna trees and felled them, freeing Kuvera's sons Nalakuvara and Manigriva (*Bhag.* 10.10).
[^454]: Gopishvara is Mahadeva (Shiva) in Mahavana.
[^455]: Sapta-samudrika ("[the well] of the seven seas") is a well in Mahavana. Pinda is a memorial offering to the ancestors.
[^456]: The eighth in the count of the twelve forests of Vraja (see 5.111–147). Lord Varaha is speaking.
[^457]: The 1960 ed. reads "জন্মোৎসব-স্থান" — "seeing the place of the birth festival".
[^458]: Madana-gopala (below, in 1784, Madanamohana) is the Deity served by Sanatana Gosvami; later He was installed in Vrindavana.
[^459]: Ramanaka-valu ("the charming sand") is a sandy bank of the Yamuna near Gokula, the place of Krishna's childhood play (now Ramana-reti).
[^460]: Gopa-kupa ("the well of the cowherds") is a well in Gokula. Gokula (Mahavana) is Nanda's village on the eastern bank of the Yamuna, where Krishna spent His infancy.
[^461]: Agravana is a forest, apparently near present-day Agra. Jamadagni is a sage, the father of Parashurama.
[^462]: Renuka is a village by the Yamuna (probably present-day Runakta near Agra), named after Jamadagni's wife, the mother of Parashurama.
[^463]: Rajagrama is a village on the way from Renuka to Gokula.
[^464]: The *Sri Chaitanya-charita* is the Sanskrit poem of Murari Gupta (*Sri Krishna-chaitanya-charitamrita*). "In the second sarga" follows the 1912/13 ed.; in the 1960 ed. the introductory line has only "in the fourth prakrama". Sri Madhava is the Deity of Veni-madhava at Prayaga (cf. CC Madhya 17).
[^465]: Akshaya-vata ("the imperishable banyan") is the sacred banyan at Prayaga; the Triveni is the confluence of the Ganga, Yamuna and Sarasvati there.
[^466]: The 1960 ed. reads "জামদগ্ন্যো মহাত্মা চ" — "the great-souled son of Jamadagni", i.e. the lord of warriors is Parashurama himself.
[^467]: Sakarauri — a village whose name is derived here from "সঙ্কলয়ে" ("they gathered together"). The 1912/13 ed. (by the scan of A) reads «সকরৌরী» (sakaraurī); the 1960 ed. «সকরেলী» (sakarelī).
[^468]: In the verse Vrishabhanu is called vṛṣa-ravi (ravi = bhānu, "sun"). Kirtida is Radha's mother; Gandharva is Radha.
[^469]: Krittika (kṛttikā; so also in A by the scan: «কৃত্তিকা উদরে») — here the name of Radha's mother; in verse 1811 she is Kirtida.
[^470]: Subhanu is Vrishabhanu's brother (cf. 5.1203). Suchandrabhanu (sucandrabhānu) — possibly Chandrabhanu, Vrishabhanu's younger brother, with the prefix su- ("beautiful").
[^471]: Vasudeva (vāsudeva) — here Krishna, the son of Vasudeva (vasudeva), the father spoken of in the second line.
[^472]: Ambika-kanana ("Ambika's grove", Ambikavana) — a place near Mathura where the cowherds of Vraja worshipped Shiva and the goddess Ambika and where Krishna freed Nanda from the serpent (*Bhag.* 10.34). Here it is on the bank of the Krishna-ganga; Gokarna is the Shiva worshipped there (cf. 5.302–332).
[^473]: Deva-yatra (deva-yātrā) — a festival, a pilgrimage to worship the gods; cf. *Bhag.* 10.34.1.
[^474]: Sudarshana — a Vidyadhara who was cursed by the sages of Angira's line for mocking them and became a serpent (*Bhag.* 10.34).
[^475]: Akrura-tirtha (Akrura-ghat) — a place on the Yamuna between Mathura and Vrindavana where Akrura, taking Krishna and Balarama to Mathura, saw Them in the water, and then Vishnu upon Shesha (*Bhag.* 10.39).
[^476]: Cf. CC Madhya 18: because of the crowds that flocked to Him in Vrindavana, Mahaprabhu stayed at Akrura-tirtha.
[^477]: The brahmanas of Mathura were performing the Angirasa sacrifice (*Bhag.* 10.23); Krishna sent the cowherd boys to them for food, but the brahmanas did not answer them, and their wives themselves brought the food.
[^478]: Bhojana-sthala ("the place of the meal").
[^479]: The four kinds of food — that which is chewed, sucked, licked and drunk. In the 1960 ed. "যাজ্ঞিকবিপ্র" — "of the sacrificing brahmanas"; in the 1912/13 ed. "যাজ্ঞিকবিজ্ঞ" — "of the learned sacrificers".
[^480]: So in the 1912/13 ed. (dvādaśamam); the 1960 ed. reads dvādaśakam ("[of the] twelve"). Vrindavana is the twelfth in the Adi-varaha Purana's list of the twelve forests of Vraja.
[^481]: *Bhag.* 10.11.28 — the number as in the 1960 ed.; the 1912/13 ed. (by the scan of A) has «১১ অধ্যায়ে ১২ শ্লোকঃ», apparently a misprint. The words of Upananda, who proposes that the cowherds move from Gokula to Vrindavana.
[^482]: *Bhag.* 10.21.10 — the number as in the 1912/13 ed. (by the scan of A: «২১ অধ্যায়ে ১০ শ্লোকঃ»); the 1960 ed. has "১৭". The words of the gopis.
[^483]: The Sushumna is the principal subtle channel (nadi) of the body in yoga teaching: since the forest is Krishna's body, the Yamuna (Kalindi) is called its Sushumna.
[^484]: A yojana is four kroshas, so five yojanas are twenty kroshas; in 1905 Narahari resolves the difference from the popular "sixteen kroshas" by the inconceivable potency of the abode.
[^485]: Pratima (pratimā) — a temple Deity: worldly people see in Govinda only the Deity in the temple, while His intimate ones see Krishna Himself.
[^486]: Mauna-mudra — "the seal of silence": the Deity of Govinda is silent and motionless (cf. 1950).
[^487]: The 1960 ed. adds in brackets "(গোপালতাপন্যাং)" — "in the Gopala-tapani"; the 1912/13 ed. has only "in the Atharva-veda". The source of the passage has not been identified.
[^488]: Gaudiya Vaishnavas understand "without qualities" and "formless" as "without material qualities and material form": Govinda's form is eternal and spiritual.
[^489]: Hava and bhava are the first of the "ornaments" (alankaras) of the loving heroine, bodily manifestations of her love (*Ujjvala-nilamani*).
[^490]: The Yogapitha (literally "the seat of union") is the centre of Vrindavana, the place of the eternal presence of Radha and Govinda; the site of Sri Govinda-deva's temple in Vrindavana is also so called.
[^491]: So in the 1912/13 ed. (divyaṁ vraja-vayo-rūpam); the 1960 ed. reads divya-vrajyā-vayo-rūpam ("divine in gait, age and form"); likewise in 1935.
[^492]: Prakritis — here, Krishna's feminine energies, His principal beloveds (Lalita and the others, 1939–1945).
[^493]: The eight friends around the Yogapitha: in front (in the 1960 ed.'s anvaya, in the west) Lalita, in the north-west Shyamala, in the north Madhumati, in the north-east Dhanya (in the anvaya, Dhanishtha), in the east Vishakha, in the south-east Shaibya, in the south Padma, in the south-west Bhadra. The 1960 ed.'s anvaya takes "Hari's beloved" (haripriyā) with Vishakha; it may also be a separate name (cf. Haripriya in 1944).
[^494]: Reading unclear: the 1960 ed. has sarvādhikā ("surpassing all"; in the anvaya, sarva-sādhikā, "accomplishing all that Krishna desires"); in A (by the scan, 500 dpi) the print is blurred: «সর্ব্ব[স]াধিকা», apparently sarva-sādhikā, as in the anvaya; translated after the text of the 1960 ed.
[^495]: So in the 1912/13 ed. (by the scan of A: «ষোড়শী প্রকৃতিশ্রেষ্ঠা»); the 1960 ed. reads ṣoḍaśa-prakṛti-śreṣṭhā ("the best of the sixteen prakritis").
[^496]: In the 1960 ed. the number 1946 is skipped: 1947 follows directly after 1945; no text is missing in the 1912/13 ed.
[^497]: In the standard numbering, *Bhakti-rasamrita-sindhu* 1.2.239; both editions have "১১১". Keshi-tirtha is a ghat on the Yamuna in Vrindavana where Krishna killed the demon Keshi.
[^498]: "Exceedingly enchanting" follows the 1912/13 edition (*dvibhujāti manohara*, "two-armed, exceedingly…"); the 1960 edition reads *dvibhujādi*: "two-armed and so on — enchanting."
[^499]: The *Gopala-tapani* is the *Gopala-tapani Upanishad*, an Upanishad on Krishna as Gopala; the Purva-vibhaga is its first part. The verse number follows the 1912/13 edition (B: "১১"); the 1960 edition has "১৩শ" (the thirteenth).
[^500]: The line is made up of the endings of three mantras (verses 37, 41, 43). Govardhana is here a name of Krishna (literally "He who increases the cows").
[^501]: The number 1959 exists only in the 1960 edition: it marks the Bengali translation of quotation 1958, and no text of the poem stands under it; in the 1912/13 edition this couplet follows quotation 1958 directly.
[^502]: The *Urdhvamnaya-tantra* (*Urdhvamnaya*) is a tantra in the form of a dialogue between Shiva and Parvati; below, 1961–1997, is its nineteenth patala (chapter).
[^503]: The 1960 edition explains: Krishna Himself marvels at Govinda's beauty.
[^504]: "Whose form is a stone full of consciousness and bliss" follows the 1912/13 edition (*cidānanda-śilākāraḥ*: of the Deity of Govinda); the 1960 edition reads *cidānanda-mayākāraḥ*, "whose form is full of consciousness and bliss." The stealing of the garments and the vow refer to the pastime with the gopis who kept the vow to Katyayani (*Bhag.* 10.22). Kaishora (1965) is early youth.
[^505]: Vrinda and Vira are gopi messengers (dutis). The 1960 edition takes the whole couplet as about Vrinda alone: "Vrinda, His bodily splendour, a heroine skilled in all means, named Lilavati, always stands to the east of the Yogapitha."
[^506]: Shyama is a friend of Radha; Bhagini means literally "sister"; Siddheshi (1971), "mistress of perfections."
[^507]: According to the 1960 edition: the Five-faced is Shiva, the Ten-faced Sankarshana, the Four-faced Brahma, the Thousand-faced Ananta.
[^508]: Madanonmadini: "she who maddens [even] Madana." The tree, according to the 1960 edition, is the wish-fulfilling tree (kalpa-vriksha). Mana is the jealous pique of the beloved.
[^509]: An obscure verse; translated according to the anvaya of the 1960 edition. The husband of Rati is Kamadeva (Kama, Manmatha); the Pair is Radha and Govinda; the sapphire Hari is Krishna, like a blue gem.
[^510]: The syllable of Manmatha is the kama-bija, a "seed" syllable of a mantra; two such syllables, "shri-krishnaya," "govindaya" and "svaha" make twelve syllables. Purva-anuraga is love that flares up before meeting; the 1960 edition takes *pūrva* as "supreme."
[^511]: The rishi, the metre (chhandas), the devata, the shakti and the shadanga (sixfold nyasa, touching parts of the body with the mantra) are the required elements in the tantras' description of a mantra.
[^512]: A lakh is a hundred thousand. A homa is a fire sacrifice; ghee is clarified butter. Kahlara (1981) is the white water lily.
[^513]: Dhiroddhata ("steady and haughty"), dhirodatta ("steady and noble") and dhira-lalita ("steady and playful") are types of the hero-lover (nayaka) in poetics. Gopikanatha ("lord of the gopis") is Gopinatha.
[^514]: The text of 1983–1987 is difficult; translated according to the 1912/13 edition (checked against the scan of A: «মাধুর্য্যং গোবিদাং পতৌ», «বাল্যতঃ সমতিক্রান্তঃ»), with the help of the anvaya of the 1960 edition. According to the 1960 edition, the Lord of the cowherds (*govidāṁ pati*) is Gopinatha, who abides in the ravine of Govardhana; it is Govinda who has gone beyond kaishora (1986–1987). Kandarpa is the god of love; here, amorous feeling.
[^515]: Sangava is the time after early morning, about three muhurtas (2 h 24 min) after sunrise.
[^516]: Ratna-mandapa: "jewelled pavilion"; Shringara-: "pavilion of amorous adornment"; Saubhagya-: "of good fortune"; Maha-madhurya-: "of great sweetness"; Samrajya- (1996): "of sovereignty"; Ananda-: "of bliss"; Surata-: "of amorous delights."
[^517]: So in the 1912/13 edition (by the scan of A): «সাম্রাজ্যমণ্ডপং নাম তথা সুরতমণ্ডপং। আনন্দমণ্ডপং নাম তথা সুরতমণ্ডপং» — "Surata-mandapa" is named twice (the reading «মবলত» in the OCR copies is a distortion of «সুরত»). The 1960 edition lacks the half-verse with "Ananda-mandapa." The eight names are reached by counting also the epithets of 1994 ("Unassailable for Chandravali," "Temple of Radha's Good Fortune").
[^518]: A patala is a chapter of a tantra.
[^519]: Bhojana-tila: "the hill of the meal"; apparently the hill by Bhojana-sthali, where the brahmanas' wives fed Krishna, and from where Raghava spoke of Vrindavana and the Yogapitha.
[^520]: Saubhari was a sage who performed austerities in the waters of the Yamuna; because of his curse Garuda could not fly here, and so the serpent Kaliya took refuge here from Garuda (*Bhag.* 10.17).
[^521]: Gonarakha follows the 1912/13 edition (by the scan of A: «গোনরখ নাম গ্রাম জানে সর্ব্বজন»); the 1960 edition has "Sanarakha" (সনোরখ); cf. the present-day village of Sunrakh near Vrindavana, associated with Saubhari.
[^522]: Kaliya-hrada is the pool in the Yamuna at Vrindavana where the many-headed serpent Kaliya lived, subdued by Krishna (*Bhag.* 10.16); below it is also called Kaliya-tirtha, Kali-tirtha, Kali-hrada. The kadamba of play (*keli-kadamba*, 2002) is the tree by the pool from which Krishna leapt into the water.
[^523]: Ramanaka-dvipa is an island in the ocean, Kaliya's former home (*Bhag.* 10.16–17).
[^524]: The verse number follows the 1960 edition (*Bhag.* 10.16.62); the 1912/13 edition has "৫৫" (the fifty-fifth). Krishna speaks, addressing Kaliya.
[^525]: Literally "destroys tirtha-sins" (*tīrtha-pāpa*): sins committed at holy places are considered especially grave.
[^526]: Sayujya, "union," is one of the kinds of liberation; the Gaudiya Vaishnavas reject it as a goal. The 1960 edition renders it in its translation as "service to Krishna."
[^527]: The Dvadashaditya-tirtha ("tirtha of the Twelve Adityas") is the hill by Kaliya-hrada where the twelve Adityas (suns) warmed Krishna, chilled after subduing Kaliya (2020–2022).
[^528]: The day of the Sun is Sunday; sankranti is the day the Sun enters a new sign of the zodiac.
[^529]: Sanatana Gosvami's place is by the Dvadashaditya hill, where the temple of Madanamohana was later built.
[^530]: Praskandana ("outflowing") is a tirtha and ghat on the Yamuna in Vrindavana, next to Dvadashaditya-tirtha described above; the name is explained in 2032–2034.
[^531]: The text of the verse is corrupted in the OCR copies; the second half is translated from the scan of A: «তত্তৎকোমলসান্দ্রসুন্দরতরশ্রীমৎসদঙ্গোচ্ছলদ্গন্ধৈর্হারি সুবারি সুদ্যুতি» (tat-tat-komala-sāndra-sundaratara-śrīmat-sad-aṅgocchalad-gandhair hāri suvāri sudyuti).
[^532]: The 1960 edition reads "śrīkṛṣṇacaitanyābhinna" — "non-different from Sri Krishna Chaitanya". Advaita Acharya, the closest associate of Mahaprabhu, lived in Shantipura.
[^533]: "Mādhavendra-purīśvara" — "the master Madhavendra Puri"; it can also be understood as "Madhavendra Puri [and] Ishvara [Puri]".
[^534]: Shrihatta is present-day Sylhet (Bangladesh). Kuvera Pandita (in PV, Kuvera Acharya) is Advaita's father.
[^535]: Shantipura is a town on the Ganga not far from Navadvipa; later Advaita lived here.
[^536]: As the story implies, the Purusha shining like gold is Lord Chaitanya (Gaura), the second is Advaita; the "elder brother" in 2051 is Nityananda (Balarama), cf. 2059.
[^537]: Cf. *Bhag.* 7.9.38: in the Kali age the Lord comes hidden (channa).
[^538]: Kamalaksha ("lotus-eyed") is Advaita's first name.
[^539]: The source of the verse is not named. The Madhva-sampradaya is the line of disciplic succession from Madhvacharya, to which the Gaudiya Vaishnavas trace Madhavendra Puri.
[^540]: Advaita-vata ("Advaita's banyan") is a banyan on the bank of the Yamuna in Vrindavana, by the Praskandana-ghat.
[^541]: Apparently Imli-tala ("[the place] under the tamarind") in Vrindavana; cf. CC Madhya 18: in Vrindavana Mahaprabhu sat under an old tamarind tree.
[^542]: Advaita called the Lord with loud cries (hunkaras), worshipping Him with Ganga water and tulasi leaves (CC Adi 3).
[^543]: The four Vaishnava sampradayas (lines of disciplic succession) are those of Sri (Lakshmi, Ramanuja), Brahma (Madhva), Rudra (Vishnusvami) and Sanaka, i.e. the Kumaras (Nimbaditya). "Madhva" is the reading of the 1888 and 1912/13 editions (শ্রী মাধ্ব রুদ্র সনক); the 1960 edition here, in 2112 and in 2131 reads "Sri, Brahma, Rudra, Sanaka".
[^544]: Kavi Karnapura also quotes this verse in the *Gaura-ganoddesha-dipika* (below, 2150). It apparently has not been found in the extant recensions of the *Padma Purana*.
[^545]: Ramanuja (Ramanujacharya, 11th–12th c.) is the acharya of the Sri sampradaya; his commentary on the *Brahma-sutra* is known as the *Sri-bhashya*.
[^546]: Madhva (Madhvacharya, 13th c.) is the founder of Dvaita Vedanta; from him comes the Madhva sampradaya, to which Narahari traces Mahaprabhu (2136–2148).
[^547]: Couplets 2123a–2123b are in the 1888 and 1912/13 editions; they are absent from the 1960 edition.
[^548]: Vishnusvami is the acharya of the Rudra sampradaya; Narahari also places Vallabhacharya in his line (2134).
[^549]: Hamsa ("the swan") is the avatara in which the Lord instructed Sanaka and the other Kumaras (*Bhag.* 11.13).
[^550]: Nimbaditya is Nimbarka, the acharya of the Sanaka (Kumara) sampradaya.
[^551]: Ramanandacharya is an acharya in Ramanuja's line, the founder of a northern branch whose followers are called Ramanandi.
[^552]: The *Anubhashya* is Vallabhacharya's commentary on the *Brahma-sutra*; the "Vallabhi" are Vallabhacharya's followers.
[^553]: Here begins the chain of teachers (guru-parampara) of the Madhva sampradaya down to Mahaprabhu; the same chain is given in the verses of the *Gaura-ganoddesha-dipika* (2149–2162) and of Gopala-guru (2169–2172). In the 1888 and 1912/13 editions the teachers in 2137–2148 are numbered in the text from 1 (Brahma) to 20 (Gaurachandra); Vishnu Puri (2144) has no number. Paravyoma is the spiritual sky, Vaikuntha.
[^554]: Narahari (Tirtha) is an acharya of the Madhva line, not the author of the *Bhakti-ratnakara*. In 2169 he is called Nrihari.
[^555]: Vishnu Puri is the compiler of the *Bhakti-ratnavali*, a collection of verses of the *Bhagavatam* on bhakti.
[^556]: Which work entitled *Vishnu-samhita* is meant has not been established.
[^557]: "Madhva" in 2149–2150 is the reading of the 1912/13 edition (শ্রীমাধ্ব-রুদ্র-সনক); the 1960 edition reads "Sri, Brahma, Rudra, Sanaka" (cf. 2110). In 2150 Kavi Karnapura quotes the verse of the *Padma Purana* (cf. 2111).
[^558]: জ্ঞানাবরোধনাৎ (*jñānāvarodhanāt*), literally "because of the restraining of knowledge"; the sense of the phrase is unclear.
[^559]: The *Shata-dushani* ("A Hundred Refutations") is, according to Karnapura, Madhva's work against the doctrine of a Brahman without qualities; the known work of this title belongs to Vedanta Deshika (of Ramanuja's school).
[^560]: Prita, preyas, vatsala and ujjvala are the rasas of dasya, sakhya, vatsalya and madhurya (so also the 1960 edition explains).
[^561]: A play on words: "Nimai" + "ananda" ("bliss"). The name "Nimanandi" is usually applied to the followers of Nimbarka (Nimbaditya, 2129).
[^562]: Gopala-guru Gosvami was a disciple of Vakreshvara Pandita in Puri. Nrihari is the Narahari of 2141 and 2155.
[^563]: The 1960 edition reads "in the *Atharva-veda*." The verse is known as *Mundaka Upanishad* 3.1.3, which belongs to the Atharva-veda.
[^564]: In the 1912/13 edition the statement of the source stands after the quotation ("iti tu…"); the 1960 edition moves it to the introductory line. The passage is known only from quotations in Vaishnava works. "A god on earth" (*bhū-gīrvāṇa*) is a brahmana; the Alakananda is the Ganga. অস্যাং (*asyām*, "upon her") probably means "upon the earth"; the sense is unclear.
[^565]: The Purusha-bodhini is a passage ascribed to the Atharva-veda, known from Vaishnava quotations. The syntax of the phrase ("ity anena") is unclear; the translation follows the explanation in 2196a. স্বমনু (*svamanu*) is taken as "His own mantra" (*manu*, mantra).
[^566]: Not in the 1960 edition.
[^567]: The *Vishnu-sahasra-nama* ("The Thousand Names of Vishnu") of the *Mahabharata* (Anushasana-parva); the 1960 edition adds "(*Mahabharata*, Dana-dharma) *Sri Vishnu-divya-sahasra-nama-stotra*." So also in 2209.
[^568]: The words of Garga to Nanda (*Bhag.* 10.8.13). The verse number follows the 1960 edition; the 1912/13 edition (by the scan of A) has «৮ অধ্যায়ে ৯ শ্লোকঃ».
[^569]: *Bhag.* 11.5.32. The verse number follows the 1960 edition; the 1912/13 edition (by the scan of A) has «৫ অধ্যায়ে ২৯ শ্লোকঃ».
[^570]: The 1960 edition reads "Who can tell of the pastimes of the Lord?"
[^571]: Akrura is Akrura-tirtha (see 1857). Cf. CC Madhya 18: because of the crowds in Vrindavana Mahaprabhu stayed at Akrura-tirtha, took His meals there and sat beneath a tamarind (Imli-tala).
[^572]: Krishnadasa, a Rajput, became Mahaprabhu's servant in Vraja and accompanied Him to Prayaga (CC Madhya 18).
[^573]: Rarh is the region of Bengal west of the Ganga; Ekachakra (Birbhum) is the birthplace of Nityananda; Hadai Pandita (Hadai Ojha) is His father (cf. BR 4).
[^574]: Hado Pandita (in 2253, Hado Ojha) is Hadai Pandita, the father of Nityananda, of Ekachakra; "Ojha" is here an honorific title of a brahmana. The CB (Adi 9) also tells of the sannyasi who asked the father for Nityananda.
[^575]: Balarama's pilgrimage to the tirthas in the Dvapara-yuga is described in the *Bhagavata Purana* (10.78–79).
[^576]: Pandurpura is Pandharpur on the river Bhima (Maharashtra). Sri Vitthalanatha here is the Deity of Krishna as Vitthala (Vithoba) in Pandharpur, not the son of Vallabhacharya (5.577).
[^577]: Madhava Puri is Madhavendra Puri (cf. 2271–2272); a fellow disciple (*satirtha*) is a disciple of the same guru, Lakshmipati (2268).
[^578]: Ranga Puri was a disciple of Madhavendra Puri; the CC (Madhya 9) tells of his meeting with Mahaprabhu in Pandharpur.
[^579]: Utkala is Orissa.
[^580]: That is, it did not occur to the brahmana to tell his guru that Nityananda was staying with him.
[^581]: The single earring and the horn are marks of Balarama's form (Balarama is called Ekakundala, "the one with a single earring"); the horn (*shringa*) is a cowherd's horn.
[^582]: That is, Lakshmipati does not want to separate Krishna from Balarama and serve Krishna alone as distinct from Baladeva.
[^583]: The initiation of Nityananda by Lakshmipati is told in the *Bhakti-ratnakara*; the CC and CB do not mention it.
[^584]: The source of the verse is not named.
[^585]: Pandura (Pandurapura) is Pandharpur, where Lakshmipati gave Nityananda initiation (above). A (by the scan) reads «পাণ্ডুরগ্রামীর ভক্তি কহনে না যায়» (pāṇḍura-grāmīr, "of the people of Pandura"); garbled in the OCR of the 1960 edition.
[^586]: Pratichi-tirtha — lit. "the western tirtha"; the place has not been identified. On Nityananda's travels and His meeting with Madhavendra Puri see CB Adi 9.
[^587]: Couplets 2333 and 2334 are from CB Adi 9 (chapter not verified).
[^588]: Setubandha is the site of Rama's "bridge" in South India; Rameshvara is the Shiva-linga installed there by Rama.
[^589]: Ishvara is Ishvara Puri (cf. 2335); śrī-mādhava-purīśvarādika can also be understood as "the master Madhava Puri with his disciples." The Sarayu is the river on which Ayodhya stands.
[^590]: CB Adi 9 (chapter not verified).
[^591]: Hastinapura — the capital of the Pandavas (near modern Meerut).
[^592]: Shringaravata, which is also called Nityananda-vata (see above): here Raghava tells about Nityananda.
[^593]: Kanai is an affectionate name of Krishna.
[^594]: Chir-ghat in Vrindavana, on the Yamuna (not the one near Bhandiravana, 1589). Chayana-ghat — lit. "the ghat of gathering."
[^595]: Nidhuvana is a grove in Vrindavana; nidhuvana means "amorous play," hence the name.
[^596]: The Vraja-vilasa-stava of Raghunatha Dasa Gosvami. The Destroyer of Baka is Krishna.
[^597]: Dhira-samira — "the gentle breeze": a place on the Yamuna in Vrindavana.
[^598]: The Gita-govinda of Jayadeva; the sakhi-messenger tells Radha of Krishna pining in the kunja.
[^599]: Ibid., the refrain of the song. In Jayadeva dhīra-samīre means "where the gentle breeze blows"; Narahari sees here the name of the place.
[^600]: Mana is the jealous, offended pride of the beloved. Manikarnika is here a ghat in Vrindavana (namesake of the ghat in Kashi).
[^601]: Vamshivata — "the banyan of the flute": from here Krishna called the gopis with His flute to the rasa dance.
[^602]: The text does not say which Gosvami.
[^603]: Krishna's meeting with the gopis, His questions and His advice to return home (2389) — Bhag. 10.29.
[^604]: অসমতা (asamatā; so in A by the scan: «রাসারম্ভে অসমতা দেখি গোপীগণে») — lit. "inequality" (or "disagreement"); the sense is unclear: perhaps the pride of the gopis (Bhag. 10.29) or the inequality between Radha and the other gopis. Krishna's disappearance, the search, the gopis' song and His return (2390–2398) — Bhag. 10.29–32.
[^605]: The rasa dance — Bhag. 10.33.
[^606]: A kalpa is a day of Brahma, an immense span of time. Vyasa describes the rasa in the *Bhagavatam* (Tenth Canto, chapters 29–33).
[^607]: *Bhag.* 10.33.2–10 (here 2403–2411). Modes (svara-jāti) are melodic series; dhruva is a kind of song and its measure (tala).
[^608]: The Purva-prabandha is the first part of the *Gopala-champu* (cf. the *Purva-gopala-champu* in 5.1058). "In a suitable raga" is yathā-rāgaḥ (so the 1960 edition also understands it); no raga is named.
[^609]: A difficult passage (nayana-paṇa-sacita-vaśīkṛta-pūrte); translated according to the anvaya of the 1960 edition.
[^610]: A play on words: sā śrī-rādhā valitārādhā — "she is Sri Radha, who has accomplished worship (ārādhā)." The anvaya of the 1960 edition takes it as "honored by Sri Radha."
[^611]: The younger brother of Rama is Krishna, the younger brother of Balarama.
[^612]: বর্ণিব কি আনে (varṇiba ki āne) can also be understood as "what else shall I describe?"
[^613]: The beloved (kāntā) and "her" in 2436 (anayā) are Radha; so the anvaya of the 1960 edition explains.
[^614]: anaya-care — literally "who goes imprudently" (at dawn, when they may be seen); the anvaya of the 1960 edition explains it as "who had never before gone outside [with her]."
[^615]: Radiance — dhāman ("abode," "light"; the anvaya of the 1960 edition: "the form of Krishna"). The word sanātana ("eternal") may also be a signature name — an allusion to Sanatana Gosvami, Jiva's teacher.
[^616]: The *Natya-shastra* is the treatise on drama and dance ascribed to the sage Bharata.
[^617]: Sangita is music in the broad sense: song, instrumental music and dance (see 2494–2497). From 2486 Raghava begins an exposition of the theory of music.
[^618]: Isha is Shiva; Nandi is Shiva's attendant; Bharata is the sage to whom the *Natya-shastra* is ascribed; Kohala is an ancient teacher of music and drama; Dashasya ("the Ten-faced") is Ravana; Rambha is an apsara.
[^619]: The *Sangita-parijata*, *Sangita-shiromani*, *Sangita-sara*, *Sangita-damodara* and *Sangita-muktavali* (2496–2532) are Sanskrit treatises on music; the editions give no verse numbers. No source is named for the verses 2489, 2491 and 2493.
[^620]: *Tat tiryaṅ-nara-devādi-manohāri*: so in A (by the scan: «তত্তির্য্যঙ্‌নরদেবাদিমনোহারি প্রকীর্ত্তিতং»).
[^621]: Nada is sound as the basis of music; svara is a musical tone (note); raga is a melodic mode.
[^622]: Anjaneya ("the son of Anjana") is Hanuman, to whom tradition ascribes one of the schools of Indian music. So in A (by the scan: «আঞ্জনেয়ঃ») and B; G has *āgneya*. The gourd is the resonator of the vina with which Sarasvati is depicted.
[^623]: A play on words: *nāda* = *na* + *da*. In the original fire is called *havya-vāhana* ("the bearer of oblations").
[^624]: Mandra, madhya and tara are the low, middle and high registers of the voice.
[^625]: The trivarga is the three aims of life: dharma, artha and kama; the chaturvarga is the four, with moksha added.
[^626]: In the treatises dhatu is usually the musical (melodic) side of a song and matu its words; here (2531, 2533) matu is called "raga and the rest."
[^627]: Shrutis are the finest distinguishable degrees of pitch; svara is a tone; grama is a basic scale; murchhana is a sequence of svaras, ascending and descending.
[^628]: All copies read *tālākhya* ("called tala"); the 1960 edition's paraphrase has "tala, or tana." In classical theory the tanas (*tāna*) are derived from the murchhanas.
[^629]: Graha-svara is the initial tone of a melody, amsha-svara the chief (predominant) tone, nyasa-svara the final tone; the jatis are ancient melodic types.
[^630]: Nadis are the channels of the subtle body.
[^631]: Shadja, rishabha, gandhara, madhyama, panchama, dhaivata and nishada are the seven svaras of the Indian scale, abbreviated sa, ri, ga, ma, pa, dha, ni (see 2576–2577).
[^632]: The names of the shrutis (2557–2565) are translated according to A/B/C. The 1960 edition has Chalanika instead of Kandalika (in rishabha), Sarasa instead of Saragha (in gandhara), Madhavi instead of Magadhi (in madhyama), Jaya instead of Mata (Yata) (in dhaivata), and Matra instead of Vijaya (in nishada).
[^633]: In 2561 A/B/C have *mātā* (Mata), in the verse 2564 *yāta-rasāmṛtā* (Yata); the 1960 edition has *jāyā* (Jaya) in both places. The readings of A are checked against the scan («মাতা রসা অমৃতে ধৈবতে», «যাতরসামৃতা চেতি»).
[^634]: So in A/B/C (*brahmāṇḍa*, "the universe"; in A by the scan: «ব্রহ্মাণ্ড না জানে»); the 1960 edition has *brahmāo* ("even Brahma"); cf. the verse 2569.
[^635]: Yoga-rudha is a word whose meaning is both derived from its components (yoga) and fixed by usage (rudhi). "Manifesting" follows A/B/C (*sphuran*); the 1960 edition has *svanan* ("sounding"). In the last line the scan of A reads «যস্মাদ্রঞ্জয়ন্তি» (*yasmād rañjayanti*, "delight"; garbled in the OCR copies).
[^636]: A play on words: *ṣaḍ-ja*, "born of six."
[^637]: Damodara is an authority on music, the author of a treatise; which one is not stated in the text. A nadi is a channel of the subtle body (see 2547).
[^638]: *Ṛṣabha* also means "bull." A (by the scan), B and C have *ṛṣabhasyeti*, the 1960 edition *vṛṣabhasyeva* ("like a bull's"); translated according to the 1960 edition (the sense is clearer).
[^639]: "In the throat and in the ear": so in A (by the scan: «বায়ুর্গলে শ্রোত্রে চ চালয়ন্») and B; the 1960 edition has *gandhaṁ…* ("fragrance").
[^640]: Prana, apana, samana, udana and vyana are the five vital airs (pranas) of the body.
[^641]: A play on words: madhyama is "the middle one" (from "the middle place," 2589); panchama is "the fifth," from the five pranas (2590); dhaivata echoes *dhāvan* ("running," 2592); nishada comes from *niṣīdanti* ("they sit down").
[^642]: The chataka is a bird believed to drink only rainwater; the krauncha is a heron.
[^643]: The vadi is the predominant svara of a raga, the samvadi the one consonant with it, the vivadi the dissonant one, and the anuvadi the remaining, subordinate ones (see 2601–2611). Cf. the verse 2608: gandhara and nishada are vivadi to rishabha and dhaivata, or the other way round (2603–2604).
[^644]: Dattila is an ancient theorist of music, the author of the treatise *Dattilam*.
[^645]: That is, in the *Sangita-parijata* (cf. 2621).
[^646]: A play on words: *saṃmūrcchita* means "thickened, unfolded, swelling"; hence the name murchhana. *Bharatāḥ* (plural) is Bharata and his school. The 1960 edition has *yadā* ("when") for *yatra* ("where") and *nāmnā* for *iti*; the sense is the same.
[^647]: So in A and C (*sthāna-traye*); the 1960 edition has "in the three gramas." The sthanas are the three registers of the voice (mandra, madhya and tara; see 2518–2520). The twenty-one murchhanas are seven in each of the three gramas.
[^648]: Lalita, Madhyama, Chitra, etc. (2637–2640) are names of murchhanas, not of the sakhis. The list differs from the list of murchhanas in the *Natya-shastra*.
[^649]: The Moon-crested One (*candramauli*, "with the moon on his brow") is Shiva.
[^650]: So in A and C (*śirāgre*, "at the crown of the head"); the 1960 edition has *śivāgre*, "before Shiva." The crown of the head is the place of the high register (tara, see 2518). The source of the verse is not identified.
[^651]: All copies read *tāla* here and below (2645–2657); the 1960 edition adds "(tanas)" to the heading and has *tāna* in the verses 2650–2652. In content these are the tanas, melodic sequences derived from the murchhanas (cf. 2538–2539); the kuta-tanas (*kūṭa-tāna*) are composite tanas obtained by permuting the svaras.
[^652]: Damodara is a theorist of music; the source of the quotation is not identified.
[^653]: The Agnishtoma is a Vedic soma sacrifice; in the treatises on music the pure tanas bear the names of Vedic sacrifices. "Becomes auspicious" is *śivo bhavet*, a play on words: after praising Shiva one "becomes shiva (auspicious)."
[^654]: So in A (by the scan: «সন্তি প্রয়োগবৈধুর্য্যান্ন ময়া তাঃ প্রকীর্ত্তিতাঃ»; *prayoga-vaidhuryān na*, "owing to unfitness for practice — not"); the 1960 edition has *vaidhuryāya*, C *vaidhuryām*.
[^655]: Varna is the character of melodic movement: sthayi, arohi, avarohi and sanchari (see 2659–2665).
[^656]: *Sthāyy-anugatārtha*: so in A (by the scan: «স্থায্যনুগতার্থ»), B, C; understood after the verse 2664: the names arohi ("ascending") and avarohi ("descending") speak for themselves.
[^657]: Alankara (lit. "ornament") is a melodic figure, an ordered combination of svaras; below (2674–2693) examples are given for each kind of varna.
