[^658]: The couplet 2679 has "Prachchhadana"; the verse has *sandhi-pracchādana* (A, B, C *sandhiḥ pracchādanam*).
[^659]: A (by the scan: «আদ্যং স্বরং চতুর্ব্বারং দ্বিবারঞ্চ তৃতীয়কং») and B read *dvivāraṃ ca tṛtīyakam* ("the third twice"); the 1960 edition has *ādyaṃ svaraṃ dvivāraṃ ca caturvāraṃ dvitīyakam* ("the first twice, the second four times"). Translated according to the sense of the couplet 2681 and the example.
[^660]: "The lion's backward glance" (*siṃhāvalokana*) is a turn in which the melody, having moved forward, returns to its beginning, like a lion looking back over the way it has come.
[^661]: The vadi ("the sounding one") is the chief tone of a raga; here it is identified with the amsha-svara.
[^662]: Not in the 1960 edition. The *Gita-prakasha* is a treatise on music.
[^663]: So in the 1912/13 edition. In the 1960 edition the attributions are transposed: verse 2705 is assigned to the *Sangita-parijata*, and 2706 is introduced with "And further."
[^664]: Shuddha means "pure," vikrita "modified," sankirna "mixed."
[^665]: The words "Others [say]: 'Thus [the jatis] are twofold'" are not in the 1960 edition. Harinayaka is the author of a treatise on music. The eighteen are the seven shuddha and the eleven vikrita (2722–2725).
[^666]: A (by the scan: «গান্ধারী পঞ্চমান্ধ্রীচ ষড়্জাপি চবতী তথা») and B read *ṣaḍjāpi cavatī*, the 1960 edition *ṣaḍjāpi dhaivatī* ("and also Shadja and Dhaivati"); the reading is corrupt. Translated as *ṣaḍjodīcyavatī*: Dhaivati has already been named among the shuddha (2722), and in the treatises Shadjodichyavati is among the eleven vikrita.
[^667]: So in all copies (*kārmāvarī*, *gāndhārodīccarā*, *madhyamodīccarā*); the treatises usually have Karmaravi, Gandharodichyava, Madhyamodichyava.
[^668]: The 1960 edition's paraphrase takes *śuddhā-siddhā-vikṛtānām* as "the shuddha, siddha and vikrita"; cf. 2726, where the vikrita are derived from the union of shuddha.
[^669]: So in the 1960 edition (*sahasra*, cf. the verse 2738); the 1888 and 1912/13 editions have *sahaja* ("natural"), probably a misprint. Meru is the world mountain at the centre of the universe.
[^670]: The *Narada-panchama-sara-samhita* is a treatise on music ascribed to Narada; the 1960 edition has *Narada-panchama-samhita*.
[^671]: Raginis are female ragas, the "consorts" of the male ragas.
[^672]: In the 1888 edition this verse is preceded by a further half-verse: "Only six ragas are named, and thirty raginis."
[^673]: So in the 1912/13 edition (*bhāṣā*); the 1960 edition has Vibhasha.
[^674]: The names in 2751–2757 follow the 1912/13 edition (checked against the scan of A); the 1960 edition has Sindhuda (2752), Purabi, Kanada, Koda (2753), Veloyari, Vairagi (2754), Pahida (2756).
[^675]: The 1960 edition's paraphrase takes *vīṇayā* as "she who holds the vina," that is, Sarasvati.
[^676]: Sampurna ("full") is a raga of seven svaras, shadava one of six, audava one of five.
[^677]: So in the 1888 and 1912/13 editions (*nāṭa-ghaṇṭāravo*); the 1960 edition has "Nata, Ghanta-raga." *Bhūpatī* is the reading of G and C; B has *bhūpakṣī*.
[^678]: The *Kohaliya* is a treatise ascribed to Kohala (see 2493).
[^679]: Here and below are names of ragas. Dhannasika is the raga Dhanashri. *Deśākhyāśāvarī* is taken, as in the 1960 edition's paraphrase, as two ragas: Desha (*deśākhyā*, "called Desha") and Ashavari.
[^680]: Harinayaka is a medieval theorist of music (cf. 2789, 2827, 2842).
[^681]: *Tārā-ṣālaga-gauḍakaḥ*: so in A (by the scan: «শ্রীকণ্ঠশ্চৈব ভৌলীচ তারাষালগগৌড়কঃ») and the 1960 edition; taken as three ragas: Tara, Shalaga, Gaudaka.
[^682]: Audava is a raga of five svaras, as shadava is a raga of six (2774–2780).
[^683]: That is, the raga Pratapa-saindhavi (the 1960 edition's paraphrase has "Pratapasindhu"); "the second" is apparently a second variety of Saindhavi.
[^684]: The copies read *turaska* (G), *turakṣa* (A, B, C); the usual form is *turuṣka*, Turushka.
[^685]: Sankirna ("mixed") ragas are those arising from the union of two or more ragas (2790).
[^686]: So in A (by the scan: «সমুৎপন্না মুখাবরী»), B, C (*mukhāvarī*); the 1960 edition has Sukhavari (*sukhāvarī*).
[^687]: So in G (*raṅga-bhūmau*, "on the stage"); the 1912/13 edition (by the scan of A: «বঙ্গভূমৌ») has *vaṅga-bhūmau* ("in the land of Banga"), apparently a misprint.
[^688]: A danda is a measure of time of 24 minutes; ten dandas are four hours.
[^689]: Anibaddha ("unbound") is free singing not held together by bandha (construction from parts, metre and words); nibaddha ("bound") is song built from dhatus and angas (2838). Alapa is the unhurried unfolding of a raga without tala.
[^690]: Varnalankara is ornamentation with syllables: meaningless syllables ("a-ta-na-ri" and the like) or the names of the svaras (sa, ri, ga, ma, pa, dha, ni); see 2824, 2828.
[^691]: Hunkara is the sound "hum," an inarticulate utterance of the voice. Hara is Shiva; Gauri is his consort.
[^692]: A gamaka is a melodic ornament (oscillation, sliding of the tone).
[^693]: The Queen of Vrindavana (Vrindavaneshvari) is Radha.
[^694]: Angas are the component parts of a song. Shuddha ("pure"), chhayalaga ("bearing the shadow [of another]") and kshudra ("small") are the three kinds of nibaddha. So in G (*chāyālaga*); A (by the scan: «শুদ্ধা, ছায়া নগ ক্ষুদ্র», «ছায়া নগং»), B, C have "ছায়া নগ" (*chāyā naga*), apparently a misprint of the old editions.
[^695]: Salaga (in the treatises usually *sālaga*) is the same as chhayalaga; sankirna here is kshudra (2844).
[^696]: A play on words: *prabandha* — *prakṛṣṭa bandha*, "excellent construction."
[^697]: Prabandha, vastu and rupaka are three names for a song composition (nibaddha): prabandha has four dhatus and six angas, vastu three dhatus and five angas, rupaka two dhatus and two angas (see 2850–2852). The angas are the constituent elements of a song (see 2872–2884).
[^698]: Harinayaka is the author of a Sanskrit treatise on music, to whom Narahari refers many times. Nibaddha ("bound") is a song bound by a fixed text, metre and tala. In 2851 there is a play on the word "prabandha": *prakṛṣṭa bandha*, "an excellent binding."
[^699]: Here dhatu is a section (part) of a composition, not the melodic side of a song as in 2530–2535.
[^700]: Udgraha ("taking up") is the opening section of a song, melapaka ("the joining one") the transition, dhruva ("the constant one") the recurring refrain, abhoga ("completion") the concluding section; antara ("the intermediate one," 2861–2862) is the section between dhruva and abhoga.
[^701]: This explanatory line is in the 1912/13 edition (B); it is absent from the 1960 edition.
[^702]: The song 2866–2871 (in Brajabuli) illustrates the dhatus of a prabandha; the names of the sections are given in the text itself. All copies read পঠমঞ্জরী (*paṭhamañjarī*); the usual name of the raga is Patamanjari (*paṭamañjarī*).
[^703]: Ghanashyama is the author's second name, Narahari's; according to 2863–2864, the poet's name is given in the abhoga.
[^704]: The five dhatus are udgraha, melapaka, dhruva, antara and abhoga.
[^705]: Svara is the notes (sa, ri, ga…); biruda is laudatory epithets naming qualities; pada is meaningful words; tenaka (tena) is the auspicious syllables "tena"; patha is syllables imitating the sound of instruments (drums); tala is the rhythmic cycle.
[^706]: Chachchatputa, yati and adi (2881) are names of talas.
[^707]: Here jati is a class of prabandha by the number of its angas (compare the other sense of the word in 2541–2543). The muni, according to the 1960 edition, is Bharata.
[^708]: All copies (A by the scan: «তালেনৈকেন বাদ্যাভ্যাং ত্রিভির্বা») read *vādyābhyām* ("with two instruments"); from the sense (compare "prabandhas with many talas" in 2896) *dvābhyām* ("with two [talas]") is possible.
[^709]: The shuka is a parrot, the shari his mate (a female parrot or myna), and the cuckoo (pika) the Indian koel. The birds sing songs of the medini (2903–2907, 2908–2911) and nandini (2912–2914) types, which contain the angas: words, biruda epithets, svaras ("sa ri ga"), tena ("tenna"), patha ("dha dha"); syllables without meaning are rendered by their sound.
[^710]: The Daughter of the Ocean is Lakshmi.
[^711]: The syllables are given after G and B, checked against the scan of A («ঝান্তা ঝাং ঝান্তা তাথা বিত কতো থুন্না দৃমিকি ত্রিগও তকতা তা থৈয়া»); G "বান্তা বাং ঝান্তা তাখা বিতকতো থুন্না দৃমকি ত্রিগও তকতা তা থৈয়া," B "ঝাসন্তা ঝাং ঝান্তা তাখা বিত কতো থুন্না দুমিকি…".
[^712]: The sense of the word "shakti" (*śakti*, "power, capacity"; in the 1960 edition শাক্তি) is unclear here; translated literally.
[^713]: Ela is one of the classical prabandhas (complex song forms); next (2918–2927) twenty-six prabandhas are listed.
[^714]: The names of the prabandhas are given as in the text; the readings of several are unclear (OCR): স্বরাদ্যকরণং (Svaradya-karana); বর্তন্যথ — B বদ্ধন্যথ, G বত্মস্যিথ, C বর্মন্যথ, understood as *vartany atha* ("then Vartani"); মাতিলক (possibly Umatilaka); সিংহনীল (possibly Simhalila). Angacharini (*aṅga-cāriṇī*) may be not a separate name but a qualifier of Varnasvara (compare its four varieties in 2918–2919).
[^715]: So in A (by the scan: «লাহড়ী চ প্রকীর্ত্তিতা … তথা সরভনীলকঃ»): "লাহড়ী" (*lāhaḍī*, Lahadi); "সরভনীলক" (*sarabhanīlaka*; possibly Sharabhalila — cf. the name of a tala in 2968). Here the list of the twenty-six prabandhas (2921–2927) ends.
[^716]: Svarartha ("meaning [from] the svaras") is a prabandha in which the desired meaning is expressed by the syllables of the svaras alone (sa, ri, ga, ma, pa, dha, ni), see 2931; shuddha means "pure," mishra "mixed."
[^717]: The indication of the raga ("রাগঃ কেদার") is in B; the 1960 edition does not have it. The song 2933–2936 is an example of the svarartha prabandha sung by the bee (2929–2932).
[^718]: The second half of the line follows the scan of A: «ব্রজরমণীমণি-মুখপদ্ম পরিমল লুব্ধ বঙ্ক রতন সমা» (*lubdha* "greedy, enamoured," *baṅka* "graceful," *ratana-samā* "like a jewel"; the OCR of copy B has «নিব … সগা»). In the OCR of the 1960 edition this half and the first half of 2936 are lost.
[^719]: Ghanashyama is the author's second name, Narahari. The svara syllables follow the 1960 edition ("পমগমম্মগরি মপধনিপধনিধা"); in B the line is corrupt.
[^720]: Shuda (in the treatises *sūḍa*) is a song form woven of many talas (2941–2942).
[^721]: All copies read "সংজ্ঞা রসালগ" (*saṃjñā rasālaga*); understood as *saṃjñār sālaga* ("the term… salaga"), cf. 2944. Salaga is the same term as in 2843.
[^722]: Dhruvaka, manthaka and the rest are kinds of salaga shuda. "বাসক" (*vāsaka*): so in A (by the scan: «বাসকঃ প্রতিতালশ্চ», «চত্বারো বাসকাঃ»), C and the 1960 edition; possibly the *rāsaka* of the treatises.
[^723]: The nine kinds (2946) and ten more give nineteen. The 1960 edition reads "ভুবি" ("on earth") instead of "ভুরি" ("a multitude").
[^724]: So in A (by the scan: «আদির্যতির্নসারুশ্চাড্ডতালস্ত্রিপুটস্তথা»): "নসারু" (*nasāru*; G "ন্সারু"); "চাজ্ঞতাল" is understood as *cāḍḍatāla* ("and also addatala"); the 1960 edition's translation has "অজ্ঞ" (*ajña*).
[^725]: The 1960 edition reads "গতিশুদ্ধি" ("purity of movement") instead of "গীতশুদ্ধি" ("purity of song").
[^726]: Isha ("the Lord") is Shiva; Girija ("Daughter of the Mountain") is Parvati, Shakti. Ghañ is a suffix of Sanskrit grammar.
[^727]: Sharajanma ("born in the reeds") is Kartikeya, the son of Shiva; Maruta is the god of the wind. "A" is the long vowel in the word *tāla*.
[^728]: Vachaspati is an author whom Narahari cites; his treatise has not been identified.
[^729]: The names of the talas follow A/B/C; in the 1960 edition a number of readings differ: "চচ্চৎপুট" (A/C "চঞ্চৎপুট"; the usual chachchatputa is adopted, cf. 2876), "রঙ্গলীল", "হংসলীল", "বর্ণলীল" (A/B/C "-নীল"). Many names are distorted by OCR.
[^730]: A (by the scan: «জয়তালঃ সুদুর্ব্বলঃ»), B, C read "সুদুর্বলঃ" (*sudurbala*); the 1960 edition reads "কুড়ুককঃ" (*kuḍukkaka*, kudukkaka).
[^731]: A (by the scan: «সান্দীকুন্দমুকুন্দকৌ»), B and the 1960 edition read "সান্দীকুন্দ" (*sāndīkunda*), C "নান্দীকুন্দ" (*nāndīkunda*).
[^732]: Not in the 1960 edition: there the second half of 2975 is "তস্মাদুপরি বিজ্ঞেয়ঃ পার্বতীলোচনস্তথা" (the text between the two occurrences of "বিজ্ঞেয়" is skipped). Text from A (by the scan): «… রাজনারায়ণস্তস্মাদ্বিদ্বদ্ভিঃ পরিকীর্ত্তিতঃ» (*vidvadbhiḥ parikīrtitaḥ*, "declared by the learned"); «বিদ্বপ্তি» in the OCR of copy B is a distortion, not the name of a tala.
[^733]: A matra is a unit of time. Anudruta, druta, laghu, guru and pluta are the five angas (measures) of tala: a quarter matra, half a matra, one, two and three matras (2982).
[^734]: The signs in A (by the scan): «( আকারো যথা—, ।, ৬, । । ।, )» — apparently some of the signs did not print; the 1960 edition has "লঘু (), গুরু (৬), পুত (0)"; the Bengali translation of the 1960 edition gives laghu "|", guru "৬", pluta "||".
[^735]: "সাবধিক" (so in A by the scan: «এতেষাং সাবধিকঘাতস্থানমাহ») — meaning unclear; understood as *sāvadhika* ("with measure").
[^736]: A (by the scan) reads «দ্রুতাশ্রয়স্তু কথিতং চতুরঙ্গুলমুচ্ছ্রিতং» (*drutāśrayas tu*, "for druta, however"); translated by the sense ("for the one called druta").
[^737]: The 1960 edition reads "চানুদ্রুতঃ" ("and anudruta"), which agrees with the beginning of the verse, where druta is of four fingers.
[^738]: A sounded (*saśabda*) stroke is a clap; a soundless (*niḥśabda*) one is a movement of the hand without sound.
[^739]: The 1960 edition reads: "in pluta, one sounded, six soundless in it" ("ছয়" instead of "দ্বয়"); the Sanskrit verse 2991 confirms two soundless strokes.
[^740]: The meaning of the second line is unclear; translated from the reading "সোঽপ্যর্ধং যাতি চ লঘোরর্ধনাদাদ্ দ্রুত ইতি".
[^741]: Chitrapada, chitrakala, dhruvapada and panchali are the four kinds of kshudra song (the third kind of nibaddha, see 2840–2845); the characteristics of the first two are given in 3005–3008.
[^742]: All copies read চিত্রকলাখ্যা ("called chitrakala"); from verse 3006 and its closing "Such is chitrapada" chitrapada is meant, while chitrakala is described in 3007–3008; translated by the sense.
[^743]: Line — *pāda*, a quarter of a verse (not the pada words of 3005–3006). Matra is a unit of duration.
[^744]: The vernacular (*bhāṣā*) is a common (non-Sanskrit) language. Divya ("divine") is a song in Sanskrit, manusha ("human") one in Prakrit or a regional language, divya-manusha one in a mixed language (3011–3012).
[^745]: Anga, Vanga and Kalinga are ancient countries of eastern India (Anga — the east of present-day Bihar, Vanga — Bengal, Kalinga — Orissa).
[^746]: Sama ("equal"), ardhasama ("half-equal"), vishama ("unequal") — by the number of matras in the lines (3015–3017).
[^747]: Graha ("seizing") is the entry of the tala relative to the beginning of the song (3029–3037); laya is tempo (3038–3042); yati is the order in which the tempo is carried (3043–3046); mana is the concluding action of the tala (3047–3051).
[^748]: নয় means "nine" or possibly a negation ("not [only]"); eleven qualities are listed in 3022–3024 and 3026; the sense is unclear.
[^749]: Anagata ("not yet come"), sama ("simultaneous"), atita ("past").
[^750]: All copies (A by the scan: «বিন্যস্য গীয়তে তালস্তদা তালগ্রহঃ স্মৃতঃ») read তালগ্রহঃ (*tālagrahaḥ*, "tala graha"); from the sense (the definition of atita graha) one would expect *tadātītagrahaḥ* ("then — atita graha"). Kala is a beat (unit of time) of the tala.
[^751]: The last word of the first line is, by the scan of A, apparently «স্ফুরিতে» (*sphurite*, "is manifest"; the OCR copies have স্থরিতে, হরিতে, C স্রিতে). Druta ("fast"), vilambita ("slow"), madhya ("middle") are the kinds of tempo.
[^752]: Vachaspati is a theorist of music whom Narahari quotes; his treatise is not named.
[^753]: Yati here is a quality of song, the order in which the laya (tempo) is carried, not the name of a tala as in 2881. Srotovaha ("flowing as a stream"), sama ("even"), gopuchchhika ("[like] a cow's tail").
[^754]: Avarta is a cycle (round) of the tala; vardhamana ("increasing"), hiyamana ("decreasing"). In A (by the scan: «দ্বিতীয় আবর্ত্ত বর্দ্ধমানাখ্য নির্দ্ধার») and B, 3048 has vardhamana, as in 3047; translated after the 1960 edition and verse 3051 — hiyamana.
[^755]: The names of the gamakas are given as in the text; in other treatises several of them are known in the forms lina (nila), kurula (kuvala), ullasita (unnamita).
[^756]: The damaru is a small hourglass-shaped drum. Druta and laghu are measures of duration in the tala (a laghu is twice as long as a druta).
[^757]: Prolonged singing — *pluta*, an overlong sound.
[^758]: Magha and Pausha are winter months (December to February).
[^759]: Couplets 3069a–3069b are not in the 1960 edition; they are in the 1912/13 edition (A, B).
[^760]: So in A (by the scan: «নোক্তাস্তে চেদ্গ্রহস্তেষাং জ্ঞানে তত্ত্বিলোক্যতাং») and B: জ্ঞানে (*jñāne*, "in knowledge"); the 1960 edition has গানে ("if they appear in singing, let them be noticed").
[^761]: Sharira (*śārīra*) in music theory is the natural quality of the voice, the capacity to manifest a raga without training; the 1960 edition understands *suśārīra* as "with a handsome body."
[^762]: Graha (here the point at which the song enters relative to the tala), mana (measure) and laya (tempo) are terms of the theory of tala. The sthanas are the three registers of the voice.
[^763]: "Merits and faults" (*guṇa-doṣakāḥ*) follows B (1912/13 edition); the 1960 edition reads *gāna-doṣakāḥ*, "faults of singing."
[^764]: রাগ যকারাদি (*rāga yakārādi*): so in A (by the scan), B and the 1960 edition; the meaning of "yakara" has not been established.
[^765]: "From the poison of bad association" (*duḥsaṅga-viṣa-mukta*) follows B; the 1960 edition reads "freed from bad association" (*duḥsaṅga-vimukta*).
[^766]: Or: "Finely wrought songs in the languages of various lands" (*susaṃskṛta*).
[^767]: Tata ("stretched") are stringed instruments, anaddha ("covered") drums, shushira ("hollow") wind instruments, ghana ("solid") percussion of metal and the like. The muraja is a drum, the vamshi (vamsha) a flute, the kamsya-tala bronze cymbals.
[^768]: The names of the instruments are given as in the text, after the 1912/13 edition (checked against the scan of A: «ত্রিশরী শততন্ত্রী চ … গদাবারুণ হস্তশ্চ রুদ্রোহথ স্বরমণ্ডলঃ। কবিলাসো…»); the 1960 edition has shatachandri (for shatatantri), shara-mandala (for svara-mandala), kapilasa (for kavilasa). গদাবারণহস্ত (B গদাবারুণ হস্ত) may be two names: gada and varanahasta.
[^769]: The names follow A and C; the 1960 edition has ghanta-vadya (for ghata-vadya), and its Bengali translation chandu (for changava). The readings মন্থ (mantha) and মড্ডু (maddu) are confirmed by the scan of A («ডমরুষ্টমকির্মন্থো হুড়ুক্কা মড্ডুডিণ্ডিমৌ»).
[^770]: The *Sangita-darpana* is a Sanskrit treatise on music (Damodara Mishra, 17th century).
[^771]: Here the varnas are the syllables by which drum strokes are rendered (patha-varnas; compare 3131, 3133).
[^772]: Umapati ("the husband of Uma") is Shiva.
[^773]: Following A and C; the 1960 edition has pari (for pavi, 3135–3136) and todahi (for dodahi).
[^774]: A (by the scan) and C read অঙ্গুলী ন্যূনেতে ("by the decrease of fingers"), the 1960 edition অঙ্গুলি-নানেতে; translated by the sense as "by their measure in fingers" (*māne*).
[^775]: Matanga is an ancient muni, author of the treatise on music *Brihaddeshi*.
[^776]: A (by the scan) reads «কর্ত্তর্য্যুদ্ধুর এবচ» (*kartary uddhura*, "kartari and uddhura"), which confirms the Bengali translation of the 1960 edition, where the lord of sages is also identified as Bharata. Kampaka follows A and C (the 1960 edition কম্পিকা, kampika).
[^777]: The four classes of instruments: tata — stringed, saushira (sushira) — wind, anaddha — covered with skin (drums), ghana — solid (cymbals, bells and the like). "For men" follows B (*mānavānām*); the 1960 edition has *kinnarāṇām* ("for the Kinnaras") in the text and gives *mānavānām* as a variant.
[^778]: "In all instruments" follows the 1888 edition (সর্ব) and the 1960 edition; the 1912/13 edition reads স্বর বাদ্য, "in svaras and instruments." Kandarpa is Kamadeva, the god of love.
[^779]: "The enchantress of the worlds" follows the 1888 and 1912/13 editions (ভুবনমোহিনী); the 1960 edition reads "the enchantress of Govinda." The alavani (*alāvanī*) is probably a vina with a gourd resonator (compare the alapini vina of the music treatises); so in A (by the scan: «বাজায়েন অলাবনী যন্ত্র»).
[^780]: The brahma-vina, kachchhapi-vina (the "tortoise" vina), rudra-vina, vipanchi, kinnari, sarangi and svara-mandala (3159–3166) are kinds of vina and other stringed instruments.
[^781]: Yantraka (*yantraka*, literally "instrument") — which instrument is meant has not been determined; যন্ত্রক বিলাস is understood as "on the yantraka with grace (*vilāsa*)."
[^782]: Lalita, Vishakha, Suchitra (Chitra), Champakalatika, Rangadevi, Sudevi, Tungavidya and Indulekha (3159–3166) are the eight principal sakhis of Radha.
[^783]: The mardala is a drum. "Madal" is by the sense: the 1888 and 1912/13 editions read মাদক (*mādaka*), probably for মাদল (madal, a drum); the 1960 edition reads মৃদঙ্গ (mridanga); A by the scan has «মর্দ্দল মাদক».
[^784]: The muraja is a kind of drum (mridanga); the upanga is an accompanying instrument, its exact kind not determined.
[^785]: The Lord of Uma is Shiva.
[^786]: Natya is dramatic performance with speech and abhinaya; nritya is dance that expresses feelings (bhava); nritta is pure dance without expressing meaning. In the 1888 and 1912/13 editions the words নৃত্য (nritya) and নৃত্ত (nritta) are usually not distinguished in spelling; the distinction follows the 1960 edition and the sense. On the arts being born of the Vedas compare 2490.
[^787]: Abhinaya is expressive acting (by gesture, facial expression, speech); abhinaya of the body (angika) is acting by movements of the body.
[^788]: "Of these the first" follows the 1888 and 1912/13 editions (*tatrādyam*); the 1960 edition reads *tac cādau* ("and this was set forth by Bharata in the beginning").
[^789]: "Supremely" follows the 1960 edition (*param*); the 1888 and 1912/13 editions read নরং (*naram*), probably an error.
[^790]: Nataka, prakarana, bhana and prahasana are kinds of drama (rupaka) in Sanskrit dramaturgy; natika and prakaranika (3194), sattaka, trotaka, goshthi and vrindaka (3195) are kinds of "minor" drama.
[^791]: Dattila follows the 1960 edition (দন্থিলাদি, i.e. দত্তিলাদি); the 1888 and 1912/13 editions (A by the scan) read দন্তিনাদি (*dantinādi*), apparently a misprint.
[^792]: Tandu is an attendant of Shiva who, according to tradition, passed the tandava on to Bharata; the dance is named after him.
[^793]: The 1960 edition reads Sangita-damodara. The sense of the words "cutting and piercing" (*chedanam bhedanam*) and "connected with speech" (*vāṇīgatam*) is unclear; translated literally.
[^794]: Makaradhvaja ("he whose banner bears the makara") is Kamadeva.
[^795]: The gloss in parentheses follows the 1912/13 edition (by the scan of A: «আদ্যে প্রধানে রসে। রসজনকৈর্ভাবৈশ্চেষ্টিতৈরিতং … আশ্লেষ আলিঙ্গনমিত্যর্থঃ»); the 1960 edition lacks it. The first rasa is shringara, the amorous rasa.
[^796]: A karana is a unit of dance movement. "Dombika" follows the 1888 edition (ড়ন্বিকা) and A (by the scan: «কোহলোক্তনৃত্যবিশেষাড়াম্বিকা», ḍāmbikā); the 1960 edition reads আরম্ভিকা (*ārambhikā*).
[^797]: The angas are the principal parts of the body, the upangas the minor ones (eyes, brows, lips and the like), the pratyangas the secondary ones (neck, shoulders and others).
[^798]: Sharngadeva is the author of the treatise on music and dance *Sangita-ratnakara* (13th century).
[^799]: "Nine" is said, but ten are named (counting the "ornaments"); translated as in the text, and likewise in the verse 3224.
[^800]: Abhinaya is the expressive portrayal (of feelings, objects, actions) by movements of the body in dance and drama. The upangas are the minor members (parts of the face), as distinct from the angas (head, shoulders, chest, sides, hands, hips, feet) and the pratyangas (3221–3224).
[^801]: Sharngadeva is the author of the treatise on music and dance *Sangita-ratnakara* (13th century).
[^802]: The names of the kinds of abhinaya here and below are given in transliteration; they are descriptive Sanskrit words (for example, kampita, "trembling"; adhomukha, "turned downward"; uttana, "turned upward"). Dhuta means "shaken"; the 1960 edition has ধৃত (dhrita, "held").
[^803]: The hudduka is a kind of drum.
[^804]: Saushthava is the correct, balanced posture of the dancer; chaturasra (3245) is the "four-cornered," stable position of the body.
[^805]: A hastaka (hasta) is a hand gesture in dance and abhinaya.
[^806]: So in all copies (অঙ্গহাব, *aṅga-hāva*, "expressive movement of the body"), also in the verse 3253; possibly to be read *aṅga-hāra* (angahara, a sequence of dance movements). Checked against the scan of A («অঙ্গ হাব সহ নৃত্য হস্তা», «অঙ্গহাবেন সহিতা»).
[^807]: In all editions the name of Bharata carries a footnote: "Bharata is the compiler of the sutras of the natya-shastras."
[^808]: The names of the gestures are given in transliteration (pataka, "flag"; ardhachandra, "half-moon"; hamsasya, "swan's beak," and so on). The count of twenty-four results if, as in the 1960 edition, arala-mushti is one name and kavi a separate one; in the *Natya-shastra* arala and mushti are two gestures, and then তাম্রচূড়োহপরঃ কবিঃ (so in A by the scan; the OCR of copy B has কপিঃ) may mean something else.
[^809]: Verses 3270–3277 are rules for the use of pataka; the text is corrupt in places in all copies: পেটে (*peṭe*, "peta") and পুষ্করে (*puṣkare*, "pushkara" — a lotus, a pond or a drum) are given by their sound (so also in A by the scan: «এষ স্পর্শে চ পেটে চ», «পুষ্করে গ্রহণে স্বধঃ»).
[^810]: Thirteen results if khataka-vardhamanaka is one name (as in the *Natya-shastra*); the 1960 edition divides the compound otherwise: pushpaputa-utsanga as one name, khataka and vardhamanaka as two.
[^811]: তেন মুখাভিধৌ (*tena mukhābhidhau*); in the *Natya-shastra* the gesture after udvritta is talamukha — possibly *tālamukhābhidhau*; so also in A by the scan («হস্তৌ তেন মুখাভিধৌ»).
[^812]: Suchi-agratala-sanchara is one name, as in the 1960 edition; since suchi is named separately in 3297, a division is possible: suchi, agratala-sanchara. The list continues in 3299.
[^813]: So in all copies: udghatita is named twice (here and in 3298); uchchhedha (*ucchedha*) is possibly *utsedha* ("raising"); so also in A by the scan («তাড়িতোদ্ঘট্টিতোচ্ছেধ উদ্ঘাটিত»). The count of thirteen results if suchi-agratala-sanchara is one name (see 3298).
[^814]: "In a suitable raga" is yathā-rāgaḥ (likewise in 3320); no raga is named.
[^815]: The 1912/13 and 1960 editions have a footnote: if a plantain trunk is turned with its root upward, such are the thighs.
[^816]: Pathas are syllables of dance and drum rhythm (bols); given after the 1912/13 edition (checked against the scan of A), the 1960 edition differs in places.
[^817]: "Offers himself" is niramanchhana (*niramañchana*), the same as nichhani (cf. 1523). Ghanashyama is the poet's signature (cf. 1507).
[^818]: A tana is a melodic figure, a run elaborating the raga.
[^819]: The 1888 edition has ধৈরজ ধন, "the wealth of composure" (cf. 3315).
[^820]: Stanzas 3352–3353 are checked against the scan of A («প্রফুল্লিত মুখকঞ্জ বসন, দশনাবলি ললিত হসন … লোল লোচন চঞ্চল ভরু»). বসন (*vasana*, "garment") is unclear in this line, possibly বদন ("face"); ভরু (G; B ভর) is taken as ভুরু ("brows").
[^821]: মহীধর (*mahīdhara*, "upholder of the mountain") is taken as a name of Krishna, who lifted Govardhana; the sense of the line is unclear. দি দি দিগণ (so in A by the scan: «রাধিকা করকঞ্জহি মহিধর চরণ রঞ্জনা … দি দি দিগণ হস্ত অভিনয়») is taken as rhythm syllables.
[^822]: The *Govinda-lilamrita* is Krishnadasa Kaviraja's poem on the daily pastimes of Radha and Krishna; this verse lists the eight periods of the day (ashta-kaliya-lila), and the song 3400–3407 follows them in order. Sangava is the time after morning, before midday; pradosha is the beginning of night, dusk. The introductory line naming the source is absent in the 1960 edition.
[^823]: সাবান সময়ে (*sābāna samaye*, 1960 edition), B সবে সময়ে (*sabe samaye*); A by the scan reads «সাঁঝহু সময়ে» (*sā̃jha-hu samaye*, "at evening time"), which confirms the translation (cf. *sāyam* in verse 3399).
[^824]: Literally "the place of the play of [the month of] Phaguna": the 1960 edition has ফন্তুন, B ফন্তণ (*phantuna*) — probably ফাগুন (*phāguna*) or ফল্গুন (*phalguna*); Phalguna (February–March) is the month of the Holi festival; reading unclear (OCR).
[^825]: বাজত (*bājata*, "sounds"; A পাজত); read as রাজত (*rājata*, "shines"), from the sense; reading unclear (OCR).
[^826]: The pichkari is a pump-syringe from which coloured water is sprayed at Holi; "hori" (3431) is the cry of the Holi festival.
[^827]: Kunja-devi means "the goddess of the kunja." By the sakhis' plan Radha, sitting alone under a veil, plays the goddess of this grove, and Vrinda-devi presents Her to Krishna as the goddess of the nikunja (3437–3450).
[^828]: Kunja-devi, "the goddess of the kunja": by the wish of the sakhis Radha plays a forest goddess whom Krishna worships (see 3436–3448). Viparita-rati is "reversed" love-play; Lalita plays on the "worshipper" seating the "goddess" on His lap.
[^829]: Radha deliberately passes herself off as a messenger of Her rival, Chandravali (see 3454), to test Krishna.
[^830]: A play on words: the name Chandravali means "a row of moons"; Radha's moon-like face "surpasses" her.
[^831]: The ninety-six kinds of nayaka (hero) according to the *Ujjvala-nilamani*: four by temperament (dhirodatta, dhiralalita, dhirashanta, dhiroddhata; see 3484–3495), two by status (pati, the husband; upapati, a lover who is not the husband), four by relation to the heroines (anukula, dakshina, shatha, dhrishta), and three by fullness — purna, purnatara, purnatama (in Dvaraka, Mathura and Vraja).
[^832]: Yutheshvaris are the leaders of the groups (yuthas) of gopis.
[^833]: Examples for the definitions of the four kinds of hero: Raghunatha (Rama) is dhirodatta, Kandarpa dhiralalita (3489), Yudhishthira dhirashanta (3492), Bhimasena dhiroddhata (3495). The 1960 edition lacks the lines with Raghunatha and Bhimasena.
[^834]: The *Gaura-charitra-chintamani* is a Bengali work by Narahari Chakravarti himself. The songs 3502–3515 are the words of the Yamuna to the Ganga: the dark Yamuna and the white Ganga, as friends, speak of the love of dark Krishna and golden Radha.
[^835]: Shyama is the dark colour of Krishna, gaura the golden colour of Radha. The understanding of the second half of the stanza is conjectural: তাহার অবধি দেখায়া জগতে, হাসে জগজন নয়নলোভা (*tāhāra avadhi dekhāyā jagate, hāse jagajana nayana-lobhā*).
[^836]: Dark Krishna in golden Radha's embrace resembles a thundercloud with lightning.
[^837]: Dakshina ("courteous"), shatha ("deceitful", 3533) and dhrishta ("brazen", 3535) are types of the hero-lover (nayaka) in his relation to his beloveds in Rupa Gosvami's *Ujjvala-nilamani*, along with anukula ("devoted to one", 3525).
[^838]: The verses of this portion are from the *Ujjvala-nilamani* (the chapters on heroes and heroines); "there" refers to that book, cited above, although songs are quoted immediately before 3521.
[^839]: CC Madhya 8; the quotation runs 3526–3532. A crore is ten million.
[^840]: Svakiya is "one's own" (married to the hero), parakiya is "another's" (bound to the hero by secret love); the text has স্বীয়া (*svīyā*), the same as svakiya. The *Ujjvala-nilamani* counts three hundred and sixty varieties of heroine.
[^841]: Mugdha ("young, inexperienced"), madhya ("middle") and pragalbha ("mature, bold", 3562) are the three varieties of heroine (nayika) by youth and experience.
[^842]: The Sanskrit *tathākṣamā* also allows the reading *kṣamā* ("patient"); the translation follows 3546 ("gentle, incapable").
[^843]: Dhira ("composed"), adhira ("uncomposed") and dhira-adhira ("composed and uncomposed") are varieties of the madhya and the pragalbha by their conduct in mana (3554–3559, 3565–3570).
[^844]: "Pure reproach" — পবিত্র ভর্ৎসন (*pavitra bhartsana*) in all copies; apparently a reproach without coarse words, unlike the adhira (3556).
[^845]: "With hints" follows the 1912/13 and 1888 editions (ভঙ্গিতে); the 1960 edition has ভর্ৎসিতে, "rebuking".
[^846]: Abhisarika ("she who goes to the tryst", 3571–3575), vasaka-sajja ("she who has made [the chamber] ready for the tryst", 3576–3580), virahotkanthita ("she who longs in separation", 3581–3585), khandita ("the wounded one", 3586–3588), vipralabdha ("the deceived one", 3589–3591), kalahantarita ("she who is parted by a quarrel", 3592–3594) and proshita-bhartrika ("she whose beloved is abroad", 3595–3596) are states of the heroine in the *Ujjvala-nilamani*; the eighth is svadhina-bhartrika ("she who rules her beloved").
[^847]: Jyotsni — [going to the tryst] on a moonlit night, tamasi — on a dark night; the attire accordingly light or dark.
[^848]: Vasaka — the turn, the appointed day (night) of the tryst; vasaka-sajjika is the same as vasaka-sajja (3576).
[^849]: "Fallen [at her feet]" follows the 1960 edition (its Bengali rendering of the verse has পদানত); the text has *prāptaṁ patitaṁ*, "who has come [and] fallen".
[^850]: "Krishna" follows the 1912/13 edition (B: কৃষ্ণে); the 1960 edition has কান্তে, "the beloved".
[^851]: The son of Gandini is Akrura (see 3601). The *Hamsa-duta* is a poem by Sri Rupa Gosvami.
[^852]: A play on words: the name Akrura means "not cruel," krura means "cruel."
[^853]: Svadhina-bhartrika, "she whose husband is under [her] sway": one of the eight states of the heroine (nayika) in the *Ujjvala-nilamani*; the definition is in 3606.
[^854]: The number of the definition follows the 1912/13 edition (B ১১২); the 1960 edition has the hundred and second; reading unclear (OCR).
[^855]: Sambhoga is the enjoyment of union; in the *Ujjvala-nilamani* it is of four kinds: sankshipta ("brief"), sankirna ("mixed"), sampanna ("full") and samriddhiman ("perfect"). The same work counts prema-vaichittya among the kinds of separation (vipralambha); Narahari stresses that it is felt in the very midst of union.
[^856]: কুসুম-শেষে (*kusuma-śeṣe*) in all copies; understood as কুসুম-শেজে, "on the bed of flowers"; reading unclear (OCR).
[^857]: The helpers (sahaya) of the hero according to the *Ujjvala-nilamani*: the cheta, a clever servant who arranges meetings; the vita, an expert in attire and in the science of love; the vidushaka, a jester fond of food; the pithamarda, a friend almost equal to the hero; the priya-narma-sakha, an intimate friend initiated into the deepest secrets (the definitions are in 3635–3646).
[^858]: In the Bengali couplet 3637 the name is Bhringa; in the verse, Bhringara.
[^859]: In the verse 3640 the name is Bharatibandha.
[^860]: Arjuna is a cowherd boy, an intimate friend of Krishna in Vraja (not the son of Pandu).
[^861]: Svayam-duti, "one's own messenger": a lover who reveals his or her love in person; apta-duti, "a trusted messenger" (see 3649–3653, 3670–3673).
[^862]: Translated after the 1960 edition, অস্যাসাধারণা (*asyāsādhāraṇāḥ*, "His special"); A has অগ্যাঃ সাধারণা, C অস্যাঃ সাধারণা ("others — common"), probably a corruption of the same reading; reading unclear (OCR). Lingini is a wandering female ascetic, one of the messengers in the *Ujjvala-nilamani*.
[^863]: The gloss follows the 1912/13 edition (B); it is absent from the 1960 edition.
[^864]: সর্বগণাগ্রিমাঃ in A/B, "foremost of all the groups"; C and the 1960 edition have সর্বগুণাগ্রিমাঃ, "foremost in all virtues."
[^865]: The kinds of trusted messengers (apta-duti) according to the *Ujjvala-nilamani*: the amitartha guesses [the feelings of both] from hints on her own, the nisrishtartha is the one entrusted with the task, the patrahari carries letters (3677–3682).
[^866]: The craftswoman (shilpakari), the astrologer (daivajna), the ascetic (lingini), the maidservant (paricharika), the foster-sister (dhatreyi, the daughter of a nurse), the forest goddesses (vana-devi) and the sakhi are the kinds of messengers in the *Ujjvala-nilamani* (3685).
[^867]: "Today" follows B (অদ্য, *adya*); the 1960 edition has অন্য (*anya*, "another").
[^868]: Lavanga Manjari and Bhanumati are Radha's maidservants (paricharikas).
[^869]: Sambhoga is the enjoyment of the lovers' union, as opposed to vipralambha (separation). Primary (mukhya) sambhoga is in waking life, secondary (gauna) in dreams; each is of four kinds (3698–3701).
[^870]: Pravasa is separation through the beloved's going away, either not far or far. Sankshipta means "brief", sankirna "mixed", sampanna "attained", samriddhiman "abundant" sambhoga (3702–3712).
[^871]: All copies read অষ্টগুণে ("with eight qualities"); by sense and by 3717–3729 — অষ্টগণে ("with her eight companions").
[^872]: The title follows A/B (শ্রীবৃহৎকৃষ্ণগণোদ্দেশদীপিকায়াং); it is garbled in the 1960 edition. Apparently the longer recension of Rupa Gosvami's *Radha-krishna-ganoddesha-dipika*. Verses 3716–3730 list the eight companions attached to each of Radha's eight principal sakhis.
[^873]: The order "Malati, Madhavi" follows A/B/C; the 1960 edition names Madhavi first, as in 3717.
[^874]: All copies read সুচিত্রাদি (*suchitrādi*, "Suchitra and the others"); translated by sense.
[^875]: Kamini follows A/B (কামিনী); the 1960 edition has রামিনী (Ramini), C বামিনী (Vamini); reading unclear (OCR).
[^876]: Rangavati follows B/C (রঙ্গবাটী); A and the 1960 edition have রঙ্গবাটা (Rangavata).
[^877]: মধুরেন্দিরা is taken as two names, Madhura and Indira; otherwise the list has seven names.
[^878]: *Ujjvala-nilamani*, the section on sakhis: the seventeen activities of a sakhi. The numbers (1)–(17) are in the text of the 1888 and 1912/13 editions; the 1960 edition does not have them.
[^879]: Gopishvara is Shiva (Mahadeva), guardian of Vrindavana, worshipped in the form of a linga in Vrindavana; not to be confused with the Gopishvara of Mahavana (5.1765).
[^880]: The *Stavamrita-lahari* is a collection of hymns by Vishvanatha Chakravarti. Sanandana and Sanatana are among the sages known as the Kumaras. সোমসোমমৌলে means "[who dwell] with Uma (*sa-umā*), with the moon (*soma*) on your crest".
[^881]: Brahma-kunda in Vrindavana; not to be confused with the Brahma-kunda at Govardhana (5.670).
[^882]: Lord Varaha is speaking.
[^883]: So in the editions of 1888 and 1912/13 (*sita-prabhaḥ*); the 1960 edition has *asita-prabhaḥ*, "dark in colour". "Its" refers to Brahma-kunda (3745–3748).
[^884]: Venu-kupa — "the well of the flute" in Vrindavana; the name is explained in 3753–3755.
[^885]: Krishna swallowed the forest fire to save the residents of Vraja on the night after the subduing of the serpent Kaliya (*Bhag.* 10.17).
[^886]: Govinda-svami-tirtha — a holy place in Vrindavana with the Deity Govinda-svami (see 3760–3761).
[^887]: Cf. Brahma's prayer to be born in Gokula even as a blade of grass (*Bhag.* 10.14.34).
[^888]: Apparently CC Adi 5 (the description of the Deity Govinda in Vrindavana); where the quotation ends is not marked in the text — probably 3783–3785.
[^889]: Manmatha is Kamadeva, the god of love. The common text of the CC has "manmatha-madana" ("the Enchanter of Kamadeva Himself"); here all copies read "manmatha-mathana" ("the Crusher of Manmatha").
[^890]: Padmasana ("the one seated on the lotus") is Brahma; cf. in the CC: "on whom Padmasana meditates in his own world." The line can also be understood as "on whom people always meditate in the lotus posture." The eighteen-syllable mantra is the Gopala-mantra (cf. the *Gopala-tapani Upanishad*).
[^891]: That is, regards the Deity as a mere man-made object.
[^892]: The swing is the Dola-yatra festival, the chariot the Ratha-yatra; the platform (mancha) is apparently the platform on which the Deity is brought out at a festival.
[^893]: Krishna Pandita — according to Narahari, the chief servant of the Govinda temple in Vrindavana (cf. BR 4.272).
[^894]: The father's and the mother's lines.
